IACMI Final Report 6

 Executive Summary
 This project’s objective is to develop and deploy new technologies for manufacturing rare
earth elements (REE), critical minerals (CM), and valuable non-fuel, carbon-based products
(CBP) from coal and/or coal waste in the SoApp Basin, thus revitalizing distressed SoApp coal
communities and reducing reliance on foreign imports of REE and CM. Resource availability
and technological advances alone will not ensure this objective is met. The right mix of business
climate, market forces, and policy will also be needed. This report identifies the critical
technologies, infrastructure, supply chains, human capital, and policy gaps that must be
addressed to manufacture REE, CM, and CBP from coal resources and wastes in SoApp

 The primary goal of the project is to characterize the resource base in the SoApp Basin.
Lignite samples have the highest average REE and REE+Y+Sc (REE+) contents, but the average
is skewed by one sample with very high concentrations. Total REE+ concentrations of CCP
materials are >300 ppm for all but one ash impoundment. There are relatively high
concentrations of Nd and Y in coal ash, which are considered CREEs. Although the vast
majority of the samples collected for this project classify as Promising REE feedstock materials
using the classification method proposed by Seredin (2010) and refined by Seredin and Dai
(2012), development of efficient REE extraction technologies will be required for economic
recovery of these critical elements.

 The large quantities of ash combined with a healthy cement and concrete industry makes
the Southern Appalachian Basin an ideal test case for further exploring REE production from
coal waste. The results produced from a novel asset valuation tool developed by the project find
that the total value of eight ash impoundments in the Southern Appalachian Basin, in terms of
only REO production, ranges from $5-93 million depending on the composition and
concentration of REO in the ash, the total quantity of ash, and the ability to leverage existing ash
beneficial use markets. Biological and magnetic separation techniques will allow producers to
leverage existing construction coal ash markets and improve the economic viability of REO
extraction from coal ash.

 Recent advances in the urban mining of REEs from coal and coal ash, such as the
availability, concentration, and distribution of REEs in coal and coal ash samples of different
origins were surveyed and discussed. Current technologies to isolate and recover REEs from coal
ash through conventional thermo-chemical methods with particular emphasis on biological,
'green' and nature-based solutions for their extraction through new alternative strategies such as
acid leaching, hydrometallurgy, bioleaching, biosorption, green solvents phytoextraction have
advanced substantially over the past decade. It was observed that coal and coal-derived
byproducts have a significant potential to meet the demand for REEs. However, the major
challenge is that many approaches available for extracting and recovering REEs are still at the
laboratory scale. A significant thrust is required for research, development, and deployment to
establish novel eco-friendly pathways for industrial-scale applications to improve economic and
environmental sustainability.
 Federal support for such a thrust could be justified on national security grounds. REE supply
disruptions lead to U.S. economic losses. Increased domestic consumption increases the

IACMI Final Report 7

Manufacturing Valuable Coal-Derived Products in Southern Appalachia DE-FE0032045

economy’s vulnerability to these shocks. US firms recognize how increased consumption of REE
imports increases their exposure to REE supply shocks and impacts their profits. But they do not
recognize how their increased consumption of REE imports impacts other firms. Using a
combination of USGS mining data and international commodity trading databases, we find that
these security costs of REE imports range 1.09%-22.54% of the REE price. By comparison a
comparable security premium for US oil imports, an area that has been a focal point for energy
security debates over several decades, range from 1.4%-18.2% of the oil price.

IACMI Final Report 8

 Introduction
 Coal production in the Southern Appalachian
(hereafter SoApp) Basin (Fig. A) has fallen nearly
70% over the last decade leading to job losses in
counties that are highly dependent on the coal
industry. The decline in coal production is driven
by a shift away from coal-fired electricity
generation that is not expected to reverse.
Revitalizing distressed SoApp coal communities
requires development of new non-fuel uses for coal
and coal byproducts.
 This project’s objective is to develop and deploy
new technologies for manufacturing rare earth
elements (REE), critical minerals (CM), and
valuable non-fuel, carbon-based products (CBP)
from coal and/or coal waste in the SoApp Basin,
thus revitalizing distressed SoApp coal
communities and reducing reliance on foreign
imports of REE and CM. However, technological Figure A. Southern Appalachian Basin.
advances alone will not ensure this objective is met. Source: USGS
The right mix of business climate, market forces, and policy will also be needed. A diverse and
experienced coalition team identified the critical technologies, infrastructure, supply chains,
human capital, and policy gaps that must be addressed to manufacture REE, CM, and CBP from
coal resources and wastes in SoApp.
  Our project leverages unique strengths of the SoApp region and team including:
 • Expert, diverse technology team includes state geological surveys, utilities with large coal
 ash inventories, and technology leaders from ORNL, the Critical Materials Institute, and
 IACMI;
 • Unique manufacturing R&D capabilities in carbon fibers, 3D printing, batteries, and
 graphite;
 • Incomparable REE innovation ecosystem (Fig. B) with massive coal ash inventory (~ 10% of US
 coal ash is in AL-GA-TN typically containing > 400 ppm REE i [~5X SoApp coal average]);
 • Unique separations approaches including biological and electromagnetic separations
 technologies;
 • Commercial and economics/policy focus (economist PI) with expertise in coal markets, regional
 economic development, regulated industries, and coal-based supply chains;

 • Regional mass market synergies in automotive manufacturing (Fig. C) with vehicle

IACMI Final Report 10

Project priorities and objectives
 Initial product priorities were
derived from the Critical Materials
Institute’s (CMI’s) priority list and
SoApp resource and industry attributes.
Because of the region’s REE and
automotive manufacturing ecosystems,
we prioritized REE, carbon fibers,
graphite, and graphene (Fig. D). The
project conducted detailed product
analysis in Phase I for each product.
These product analyses revealed a
unique coal ash-to-REE supply chain
 Figure D. SoApp resource-product-application
that became a project priority for a Phase
 synergy
II application.
 The remainder of the report is organized around six objectives described in the initial
Phase I FOA pp. 15-22:
Objective 1: Resource assessment. The research team identified four knowledge and data gaps
that became the impetus for Task 2: Basinal Assessment of CORE-CM Resources described
below:
 1) Critical mineral contents of underclays, shale interbeds, roof rock (coal refuse/tailing)
 2) REE data are lacking for most ash pond boring samples
 3) Assessment of REE-CM concentrations in AL-TN coal processing/AMD wastes
 4) Correlation of clay units in AL-TN with REE-rich Kentucky fire clays

Knowledge gaps were filled during the project by (i) identifying correlations for AL-TN
underclays with REE-rich KY fire clays; (ii) literature survey of trace mineral and
paleoenvironmental data for coal and associated sediments (especially clays) to include in geologic
model(s); (iii) search of published and unpublished records for REE-CM contents of ash and other
wastes, and database construction of REE-CM concentrations; (iv) geochemical analysis from
core, outcrop, and/or mines and processing facilities; and (v) analysis of coal wastes (emphasizing
REE-CM characterization) by acid digestion, solvent extractions, ICP-MS, XRD, SEM/EDX and
EMP. A geologic model of coal seams and surrounding strata built in IHSPetra from well and map
data with geochemical data, from coal, sediments and coal waste, included assisted in the
assessment of the resource.

Objective 2: Waste Stream Reuse. AL-GA-TN contains almost 250M tons of stored coal ash
(roughly 10% of US inventory), mainly at SO and TVA sites. Economic feasibility of ash-based
REE will require multiple value streams and beneficial use of ash residual after extraction. Any
ash beneficial use must satisfy EPA criteria. REE production from this coal ash resource is the
foundation of Task 3: Basinal Strategies for Reuse of Waste Streams described below.
Notable coalition strengths in waste re-use include (i) massive ash inventory and expertise on re-
use opportunities, characterization/extraction, and economics (SO, TVA, UTK, ORNL, UA,
ARM); (ii) exceptional environmental management and restoration expertise (ORNL); (iii)
distinctive HPC modeling capabilities on REE separations (UTK, ORNL); (iv) novel REE

IACMI Final Report 11

separations approaches (ORNL); (iv) novel product ideas, e.g., 3D printed magnets (ORNL,
UTK); (v) unique facilities, e.g., Ash Beneficial Use Center (see TIC section below); (vi) REE
recycling (Nth Cycle, ORNL); and (vii) Best Practices in workforce development (WFD)
(IACMI). The proposed TIC includes several state-of-the-art pilot facilities useful for small-scale
pilot projects and testing new technologies.

Objective 3: Strategies for Infrastructure, Industries and Business. Ensuring the economic
viability of CORE-CM from coal resources and wastes in SoApp requires consideration of
supply and demand at each link of a prospective supply chain from mining to production of
finished products with CORE-CM inputs. The research team identified five tasks that became the
impetus for Task 4: Basinal Strategies for Infrastructure, Industries, and Business described
below:
 Task 1: Describe current business climate and economic competitiveness of AL-GA-TN
 Task 2: Characterize current SoApp coal and ash supply chains
 Task 3: Characterize current regional REE and select CM supply chains
 Task 4: Develop prototype IO framework to characterize the coal-REE-CM supply chain
 Task 5: Assess US security costs of import reliance
Several links in this CORE-CM supply chain are well-established due to commercial uses for fly
ash and manufacturing in the region. These links must evolve (changes in domestic
production/extraction, input/import substitution) to reliably supply CORE-CM. Several links do
not yet exist. Technological advances alone will not ensure private investment to develop these
links. The right mix of business climate, upstream and downstream market forces, and policy is
needed to enhance existing links and cost-effectively develop missing links.

Objective 4: Technology Assessment. The research team identified three general technology gaps
assessed across the SoApp CORE-CM value chain that became the impetus for Task 5:
Technology Assessment described below:
 i) SoApp ash inventory suggests focusing novel “mining” development on safe, efficient ash handling.
 ii) Separation/purification processes including thermochemical (e.g., by ARM and Nth Cycle),
 biological, and electromagnetic approaches. Novel high-performance computing hardware and
 software was developed to inform and accelerate process selection and optimization.
 iii) Manufacturing priorities identified include magnets (containing REE), carbon fibers, graphite,
 graphene, and construction materials.

Objective 5: Technology Innovation Center. A distributed model was scoped for the TIC. IACMI
created and manages a very successful innovation network based on this model, IACMI
leveraged~ $20M of investment with existing university and FFRDC facilities into a network of
300,000+ sq ft of world-class innovation infrastructure with replacement value $300M+. The
planned TIC would be operated under the Coalition Business Model described Task 6:
Technology Innovation Center described below, incorporating relevant lessons learned from
the National Carbon Capture Center operated by SO. Facilities considered for the TIC include (i)
SO’s 42k sq ft Ash Beneficial Use Center (ABUC), addressing ash utilization; (ii) ORNL’s CMI

IACMI Final Report 12

Manufacturing Valuable Coal-Derived Products in Southern Appalachia DE-FE0032045

labs and materials processing labs, addressing REE separations and materials processing
including graphite production; (iii) UTK’s Center for Renewable Carbon, addressing bio-
refining; (iv) carbon fiber and composites labs at ORNL, UAB, and UTK (~ 40k sq ft), used for
multi-scale carbon fiber and composites development; (v) ORNL’s 42k sq ft Carbon Fiber
Technology Facility for pilot scale carbon fiber production;(vi) ORNL’s 110k sq ft
Manufacturing Demonstration Facility, addressing advanced manufacturing such as 3D printed
magnets containing REE, and prototypical composites; (vii) ORNL’s Battery Manufacturing
Facility addressing manufacturing batteries with graphite anodes; (viii) ORNL’s Leadership
Computing Facility for HPC modeling of separations and product manufacturing esp. of REE;
(ix) UTK’s Baker Center for Public Policy addressing economics, markets, and supply chains
including infrastructure, businesses, logistics, etc.; and (x) ORNL’s world-class materials and
process characterization facilities including the High Temperature Materials Laboratory, Center
for Nanophase Materials Science, and Spallation Neutron Source.

Objective 6: Stakeholder Outreach and Education. IACMI led stakeholder outreach to
communities, state and local governments, relevant business and industry partners, and other
stakeholders such as economic development groups and non-profit organizations. We
communicated and engaged in person and electronically through planned conversations, public
events, conferences, workshops and various forms of media (press releases, social media, policy
briefs). A workforce development (WFD) roadmap was developed that includes aggregation of
Best Practices, identifying existing education and training assets, and a gap analysis. Advice and
input were solicited from impacted communities. Roane State Community College, Wallace State
Community College, Gadsden State Community College and other SoApp community colleges
were engaged, along with industry leaders, operations managers and frontline employees, to create
a technician training roadmap informed by the project’s technology roadmap. It focused on
retraining workers displaced by the coal industry’s decline but also embraced new workforce
entrants. Similarly, the project’s network of industrial stakeholders, universities, and TIC resources
were leveraged to develop plans for training STEM professionals.

Project team and supporting resources
 The coalition team has been assembled to deliver extensive expertise and capabilities related to
the FOA objectives and distinct SoApp basinal and regional attributes. The team’s integrated
expertise addresses resource assessment and geologic modeling; waste re-use; development and
validation of novel technologies for extraction, separation, and production of market-targeted,
valuable CBPs; TIC development; and stakeholder outreach and education; woven into a robust
economics framework accounting for SWOT in supply chains, infrastructure, markets, and policy.
Several other stakeholders including TVA (large ash inventory in TN), ACAA, an AL coal
company, two companies with REE separations technology, and two AL community colleges
engaged and assisted the project without providing accountable cost share.
 IACMI – The Composites Institute, a Manufacturing USA Institute and the project Applicant
successfully created and manages a 100+ member consortium with 50+ projects, collectively
budgeted > $200M with individual project budgets from $100k - $15M, and 90+ members
participating in projects. IACMI has unique experience and capabilities in collaboration
management emphasizing supply chain decision frameworks; DOE project management and risk

IACMI Final Report 13

management; TIC development and management; carbon fiber and composites manufacturing
innovation; and automotive technology implementation.
 PI Charles B. Sims, Ph.D., directs the Center for Energy, Transportation, and Environmental
Policy (CETEP) at UTK’s Baker School of Public Policy. He has 16 years of professional
experience including economic analysis of Appalachia’s coal industry, utility investments in coal-
fired electricity generation, and disposal of produced water from Powder River Basin coal-bed
methane operations. Dr. Sims has extensive experience leading multidisciplinary research teams
of sociologists, economists, engineers, hydrologists, geologists, mathematicians, entomologists,
and ecologists. PM Erin Brophy, PMP, manages IACMI’s $200M+ project portfolio as its Program
Director with ~ 20 years of professional ES&H and PM experience. Cliff Eberle, “architect” of
IACMI’s and ORNL’s low-cost, high-modulus carbon fiber innovation programs, will lead carbon
fiber manufacturing. IACMI’s Communications Director Mark Morrison has 20+ years of public
affairs and industrial communications experience. Joannie Harmon-Heath has established
numerous Best Practices directing IACMI’s acclaimed WFD Program.
 UTK’s Baker School of Public Policy is the leading regional policy center focusing on policy
at the intersection of energy, environment, and global security. A core mission is to provide policy
analysis to state government, federal agencies, and the broader academic community through a
multidisciplinary team of 10 faculty and over 30 Fellows from academia (economics, philosophy,
engineering, ecology, law, political science, sociology, computer science), state agencies (e.g.,
TDEC), federal agencies (e.g,, TVA), nonprofits (Electric Power Research Institute), and private
industry (Microsoft). Matthew Murray, Ph.D, CETEP Senior Fellow, has 39 years of professional
experience in regional economic development and tax policy analysis for numerous industries in
the SoApp Basin including the automotive industry, coal industry, and public utilities. Randall
Jackson, PhD, has 42 years of professional experience performing supply chain and energy policy
analysis including extensive experience related to the Appalachian coal industry. Baker School
Fellow Deborah Penchoff, Ph.D .,will guide the assessment of HPC modeling of REE separations.
Dr. Penchoff has expertise in applying HPC and data science for national security applications
with focus on chemical physics of lanthanides and actinides. UTK also offers outstanding carbon
composites manufacturing R&D capabilities and one of the top 5 supply chain programs in the
U.S.
 GSA has been characterizing AL’s natural resources and supporting the state’s mineral and
energy industries since 1848. It has excellent capabilities for mineral analysis and geologic
modeling led by Marcella McIntyre-Redden who has 21 years of professional environmental
experience. Her principal expertise is in structural geology and hydrogeology with important
publications on CBM reservoir structure and hydrogeology. She is proficient with a broad range
of geological and structural modeling software; developed large databases on unconventional gas
resources and the geologic carbon storage potential of AL; and worked on several DOE-NETL
projects related to CO2 storage and enhanced hydrocarbon recovery.
  Barry W. Miller, Geologist, TGS’s primary coal specialist, directs geologic mapping of the 7.5-
minute quadrangles of the Plateau (coalfields) and Valley and Ridge physiographic provinces of
Tennessee.
 SO is a US top 5 energy utility with industry-leading commitment to innovation and a low-
carbon future. It serves 9M customers through subsidiaries including electric utilities with almost
200M tons of coal ash inventory in AL-GA. Nortey Yeboah, Ph.D., PE, PMP, directs its

IACMI Final Report 14

wastewater treatment, ash handling, coal combustion product beneficial use and coal ash
groundwater R&D and managed design and construction of its ABUC.
 ORNL is the largest US Department of Energy science and energy laboratory, conducting basic
and applied research to deliver transformative solutions to compelling problems in energy and
security. It has core competencies in separations (thermochemical, electromagnetic, and biological
processes), materials synthesis (carbon nanomaterials, carbon fibers, graphite) and
characterization (microscopy, neutrons); advanced manufacturing R&D (carbon fibers, graphite,
composites, batteries, 3D printing); HPC (leading the race to exascale); and environmental
management and restoration of coal wastes, radioactive wastes, contaminated soil and
groundwater. Edgar Lara-Curzio, Ph.D, Distinguished Scientist, leads electromagnetic
separations, graphite and graphene synthesis, and physical characterization; Art Ragauskas, Ph.D.,
Governor’s Chair in Bio-Refining, leads bio-separations and chemical characterization; M. Parans
Paranthaman, Ph.D., Corporate Fellow, leads thermochemical separations and advanced
manufacturing of REE and products containing REE; and Alexander Johs, Ph.D., is a staff scientist
in the Biogeochemical Dynamics group and leads environmental management and restoration
activities.
 UA is an R1 Doctoral University offering R&D expertise in Geological Sciences. Dr. Rona J.
Donahoe has 42 years of R&D / teaching experience with outstanding expertise in geology and
the trace element geochemistry/mineralogy of coal ash.
 UAB specializes in product development with 20+ year history in composite materials
applications and multi-material solutions, including recycling and power generation by-product
utilization. Drs. Pillay and Mahapatra have extensive experience in composite materials R&D and
product development including refractories and oxide glass, electrochemical processing and
characterization, and materials recycling.
RSCC is a valued IACMI WFD partner that delivers hands-on training in advanced
manufacturing disciplines to east TN communities.

IACMI Final Report 15

 Task 1: Project Management and Reporting

Task 1 Team Members: Erin Brophy, Nik Kalolo, Catherine Ross (IACMI), Charles Sims
(UTK)

Meetings, Site Visits, and Outreach
The IACMI project management (PM) team participated in the project team monthly report-out
meetings. Additionally, the IACMI PM group met monthly with the DOE Technology Manager
(TM) and the Project PI to discuss deliverables, contract negotiations, reporting, etc.

The project Principal Investigator (PI) was the main point of contact between the IACMI team
and the project partners.

Project Management and Reporting
The IACMI PM team created and managed all subcontracts (including scope of work and
budgets) with each of the partners on this program. This included execution of any contract
modifications that were necessary due to changes in budget or period of performance.

Throughout the duration of this project, the IACMI PM team worked with the project PI to
gather relevant information to complete and submit all quarterly reporting requirements as
described in the Federal Assistance Reporting Checklist (FARQ). Additionally, the IACMI team
provided an updated spending plan to DOE TM monthly.

Final reporting requirements are currently being collected and submitted by the IACMI PM
team.

IACMI Final Report 16

 Task 2: Basinal Assessment of CORE-CM Resources

Task 2 Team Members: Rona Donahoe (University of Alabama (UA) Professor & Task 2
Lead), Marcella McIntyre (GSA), Chris Hooks (GSA), Barry Miller (TGS), Riley Flowers
(SoCo), Nortey Yeboah (SoCo), Ibrahim Okunlola (UA Ph.D. student), and Kalyn Tew (UA
Ph.D. student)

Ph.D. student, Ibrahim Okunlola, participated in a field trip to visit Ca-bentonite deposits in
Gonzales, TX during the 2023 Clay Minerals Society Annual Meeting in Austin, TX. During the
field trip, Ibrahim was able to collect two samples of bentonite clay for the CORE-CM project and
met a senior geologist at Thiele Kaolin Company who later provided kaolin samples. Ibrahim also
collected bauxitic clay samples and lignite samples from the Andersonville district in Georgia. PI
Donahoe and Chris Hooks collected coal processing waste, settling pond water and sludge samples
at a mine and coal processing plant.

Literature/Database Search and Compilation of Existing SoApp Data
CoalQual and NETL EDX database and literature searches performed by UA identified 1086
coal samples, 17 coal-associated sediments, 5 coal refuse, and 20 coal tailings/waste samples
from the SoApp Basin for which REE concentrations have been previously determined. No REE
data for coal underclay or AMD water samples was found. All historical metadata and
geochemical data for non-ash samples (coal, coal-associated sediments, processing wastes, and
AMD sludge) mined from existing databases (CoalQual and EDX) and the literature were
compiled, put into the NETL data spreadsheet template, and uploaded to EDX
(https://edx.netl.doe.gov/dataset/legacy-coal-data-soapp-basin). Evaluation of the compiled data
revealed that bituminous coal was the primary characterized sample type and that only limited
CM-REE data are available for coal-associated sediments and waste materials. The focus for the
Phase I, Task 2, Southern Appalachian (SoApp) Basin Non-Ash Sampling Plan (Original Scope
of Work) was therefore to collect and analyze the CM-REE contents of coal-associated
sediments, coal processing wastes, coal combustion products (impounded coal fly and bottom
ash), and AMD water/sludge samples. The non-ash Sampling Plan was expanded to include a
variety of clays, phosphate ore, phosphogypsum waste, organic shale, shaley sandstone, and
lignite samples collected from AL, GA, MS and TN as well as 100 additional ponded ash
samples for the Supplemental Scope of Work.

samples for characterization. The core repository and database at the Geological Survey of
Alabama were used to identify and collect samples of underclay and roof rock adjacent to high
REE-bearing coal seams. The Tennessee Geological Survey and U.S. Office for Surface Mining
provided samples of AMD water and sludge, phosphate ore, and Chattanooga Shale.
Gleason/IMERYS provided samples of TN ball clays and Eufala District bauxitic clay, while the
US EPA provided phosphogypsum waste pile samples from the Superfund site at Pascagoula,

provides a summary of the types and number of samples collected for the original and
supplemental scopes of work. Table 2 lists the non-ash samples materials and number of

Sample Characterization Methods and Results
Complete Digestion (ASTM D6357) and ICP-MS Analysis (EPA 200.8)
After complete digestion, concentrations of REE, Y, Sc, Li, Th, and U were analyzed by ICP-MS
for each non-ash sample by Standard Laboratories, Inc. Coal ash samples were analyzed after
complete digestion by Mineral Labs, Inc. or by Standard Laboratories, Inc. The new REE/CM
data for all samples collected for this project were put into the NETL data template and uploaded
to EDX as 4 separate spreadsheets:

   ● New Non-Ash Data SoApp Basin FE032045, 7/2/2024,

Figure 1 shows the average concentrations of REE, REE+Y+Sc, and Li in the different materials
collected for the project. AMD water is not shown because the average REE, REE+ and Li
concentrations were <0.5 ppm. The clay-associated lignite waste samples have the highest
average REE and REE+ concentrations, followed by kaolin, coal ash, phosphogypsum waste,
and bentonite. Figure 1 shows that phosphogypsum waste has essentially the same average
REE+ concentration as coal ash, but coal ash has a higher average REE content than
phosphogypsum waste. Coal processing waste has the highest Li concentration of all sample
materials collected for the project, but coal underclay and coal ash samples are also viable Li
feedstock materials.

  Figure 1. Summary of average REE, REE+Y+Sc, and Li concentrations for all sample types.

XRF Analyses
Pressed pellets of all non-ash samples collected for the project were analyzed by X-ray
fluorescence (XRF) spectrometry for major, minor, and trace element concentrations. The Jan-
March 2024 Quarterly Report provides major and minor element concentrations (Table 2 of the
Quarterly Report), trace element concentrations (Table 3 of the Quarterly Report), and a
graphical summary of average major and minor element concentrations in Figure 1. Table 3
shows that underclays have higher concentrations of trace elements (Ba, Cu, Nb, Rb, Sr, Y, Zn,
and Zr) than any of the other types of non-ash samples collected. Major and minor element
oxide concentrations were also analyzed by Mineral Labs, Inc. for most of the coal ash samples.

IACMI Final Report 20

Manufacturing Valuable Coal-Derived Products in Southern Appalachia DE-FE0032045

XRF data for all samples were added to the NETL data templates as part of the EDX
submissions listed above.

XRD Analyses
The mineralogy of the non-ash samples was determined by X-ray diffraction (XRD) analysis and
the mineral percentages quantified using Rietveld refinement (Table 1 of Sept-Oct 2024
Quarterly Report). The mineral composition of each distinct sample material is summarized as
follows:
 ● Underclay: Illite was the most abundant mineral, with percentages ranging from 20% to 54% and
 an average of over 45%. Kaolinite was the third most prevalent mineral, with percentages varying
 from 0.2% to 35%, averaging 10.4%. Chlorite and muscovite were present in lower amounts.
 ● Roof Rock: The roof clay samples were found to contain muscovite as the most predominant
 mineral, with percentages ranging from 13% to 43%, and an average value greater than 32%.
 Chlorite was the third most abundant mineral, ranging from 6 to 30%, with an average of 19%. K-
 feldspar and albite were present in lower concentrations in the roof clay samples.
 ● Coal Processing Waste: Kaolinite was the most common mineral, ranging from 9-34%, averaging
 >22%. Muscovite was second, ranging from 20-23%, with an average of 21%, followed by K-
 feldspar and chlorite.
 ● AMD Sludge: The most abundant mineral was goethite, which ranged from 10% to 69% and
 averaged >34%. Chlorite was the second most abundant mineral, ranging from 9% to 26%, with
 an average of 17%, followed by K-feldspar and muscovite.

Extractability of REE+ and Lithium by Concentrated Nitric Acid
Sixteen samples of coal-associated sediments (13 underclay; 2 roof rock) and coal processing
plant waste (COG-007), selected for their high REE+ or Li contents, were partially digested in
triplicate using concentrated nitric acid (EPA Method 3051A). The digestate solutions were
subsequently analyzed for major, minor and trace element chemistry by ICP-MS to determine the
acid-extractable element content of these samples. The replicate data were averaged and the
results are shown in Table 1 in the April-June 2024 Quarterly Report. Extraction efficiencies
were calculated as a percentage of the total concentration present in each sample:

 Degree of Extractability (%) = Total REE+ (Partial Digestion) (ppm)  * 100
 Total REE+ (Complete Digestion) (ppm)

Partial digestion using Method 3051A (U.S. EPA., 2007) involves the microwave digestion of
the samples in concentrated HNO3 with the aim of determining the mobile (relatively easy to
extract) fraction of the total REE+ and Li present in the sample, followed by ICP-MS analysis of
the digestate solution.

The degree of extractability represents the percentage of the total REEs or lithium that are
extractable by hot nitric acid (Table 3 in the July-Sept 2023 Quarterly Report). The extractable

IACMI Final Report 21

REE+ concentrations ranged from approximately 2.7–8.1 ppm for partial digestion and 172.57-
305.54 ppm for complete digestion. Underclay samples K111-22-937 and K111-8-1021
exhibited the highest degree of extractability at 3.3% and 3.1%, respectively. The total Li
concentrations for these samples ranged from 133–519 ppm with the degree of extractability
ranging from 0.95%-3.60%. This result shows that a significant portion of REE+ and Li in these
samples is not extractable using hot nitric acid. Samples with the highest REE+ and Li
concentrations do not record the highest degree of extractability, which is likely attributed to
variation in the mineralogical compositions of individual samples, efficiency of the extraction
method, and the specific distribution of REEs within the samples. Micro-XRF analysis is
anticipated to improve the understanding of the factors that limit the recovery/extraction of these
valuable elements.

Micro-XRF Mapping
Co-PI Donahoe and Ph.D. students, Kalyn Tew and Ibrahim Okunlola, collected micro-XRF data
for selected samples at Oak Ridge National Laboratory, using an IXRF Systems ATLAS micro-
XRF spectrometer. A total of 3 trips were made to acquire elemental maps for a total of 52
samples (10 ash samples and 42 non-ash samples; Table 4) to help determine the spatial
distributions and identify the mineral hosts of REEs. During these visits, UA personnel were
assisted with instrument and safety training, badge acquisition, and logistics by Dr. Elena
Tajuelo Rodriguez. A summary of the findings for the selected samples is given below.

Coal-Associated Sediments and Wastes

The µXRF maps collected for coal-associated sediments and wastes indicate a strong spatial
correlation between Sc hotspots and Ca-P and a possible weak correlation with S in underclay
(KIII-22-917). There are also strong spatial correlations between Ca-P-Y in coal processing
waste (COG-006) and AMD sludge (COG-005), suggesting a phosphatic mineral host such as
apatite for Y (Figure 2). Other REEs could not be mapped due to their low concentrations in the
samples.

 15.2

Yttrium was identified in all five samples analyzed by micro-XRF. The concentration of yttrium
in the Greene County samples ranged from 43.8 ppm (Greene-9) to 84 ppm (Greene-16). The
concentration of yttrium in the Gaston samples was 70.2 ppm (Gaston-16) and 74 ppm (Gaston-
18). While cerium was present in all samples in concentrations ranging from 109 ppm (Greene-
8) to 199 ppm (Greene-1), it was identifiable in nine of the ten samples analyzed. Neodymium
ranged from 47.5 ppm (Greene-8) to 83.1 ppm (Greene-1) and was also identified in nine of the
ten samples. Neither cerium nor neodymium was identifiable in Greene-1 likely that their
identifiable peak was masked by other elements present in the sample, despite the high Ce
concentration. Lanthanum was identifiable in samples 9, 11, 12, 13, and 14. Lanthanum
concentration ranged from 50.1 ppm (Greene-8) to 94.5 (Greene-1). No other REEs were
identifiable by micro-XRF.
ORNL 9 contained an intact mineral grain composed primarily of sulfur, likely uncombusted
organic material. Crystalline material attached to the organic material contained P, Ca, Y, and S,
calcium, yttrium, and sulfur in high concentrations. The calcium and phosphorus crystals are
likely xenotime, which has a chemical formula of YPO4 and often contains calcium as an
impurity (Figure 14).
Sample ORNL 15.2 has an intact black material with rounded edges which is composed
primarily of S, Ca, and P and contains little Si, Fe, or Al (Figure 15). Areas composed of sulfur
and little else are likely partially combusted coal containing detrital crystalline material. There
are also areas containing high concentrations of Ca and P correlated with Y (Figure 16). This is
compared with areas of the material with high concentrations of sodium, barium, titanium, and
vanadium correlated with cerium (Figure 17). Neodymium is present throughout the crystalline
material and is primarily correlated with manganese content (Figure 18).

In sample ORNL 11, Y and Nd appeared diffuse, and there were no distinguishable grains
containing high concentrations of either element that could be used for correlation. Overall, Ce
and La are closely correlated in most samples (Figure 19). Both show a negative correlation
with K, P, and Y (Figures 20, 21, & 22). They also have positive spatial correlations with Ba,
Ti, and V (Figure 23). Because these maps look so similar, and Ba, V, and Ti all share peaks
with Ce and La, there is a possibility that the micro-XRF cannot successfully distinguish
between these elements, resulting in a false correlation. Based on XRF analysis for oxide
content, it is known that V, Ti, and Ba are present in the samples, however.

Although grains with high Nd concentrations are also high in Ce in many ash samples, not all
crystals high in Ce contain Nd, and Nd does not show strong spatial correlations with Ba, V, or
Ti (Figures 24 & 25). Yttrium shows a positive correlation with P, often in conjunction with Ca
(Figures 14 & 26).

Table 6 also lists the average Li concentration of coal ash by CCP impoundment. The average
total Li concentration for all 515 samples was 219.52 ppm (±81.92 ppm), well above the 120
ppm concentration cited as the cut-off grade by China. Impoundments averages having a higher
standard deviation is attributed to greater variation in feed coal sources by the power plant.
Figure 28 compares the average concentrations of major REE constituents of coal ash between
different impoundments. The plot shows that coal ash is a valuable source of two CREEs, Nd
and Y.
Figure 29 shows that coal ash average Li and REE+Sc+Y concentrations are positively
correlated (r = 0.589). Positive correlations exist between coal ash Li concentration and Al2O3 (r
= 0.4914), K2O (r = 0.2161), and P2O5 (r = 0.2696) contents. No correlations exist between and
REE+Y+Sc concentration and SiO2, Fe2O3, P2O5 , but moderate positive correlation between
Al2O3, K2O, and total REE+Sc+Y and moderate negative correlations between CaO, MgO, and
Na2O and total REE+Sc exist.

3D Empirical Bayesian Kriging Modeling of a CCP Impoundment
Empirical Bayesian kriging (EBK) is an effective tool for interpolating spatial variation in
continuous geologic features. It more accurately models complex geologic features with limited
data points compared to other forms of kriging, because multiple simulations, overlapping
subsets, and model weights are employed by the model to increase the accuracy of predictions.
A CCP impoundment (Site 2) was selected for EBK 3D modeling that spans approximately 300
acres and contains ~25 million cubic yards of coal ash. The EBK 3D model for Site 2 is based on
analytical data collected by this project for 34 new CCP samples, including ICP-MS data for
REE, Y, Sc, and Li concentrations after complete digestion and additional XRF data for weight
percent major and minor element oxides obtained for 17 of these samples.
After removal of outliers (identified as data points having z-scores with absolute values >3),
sample data were imported to ArcGIS Pro as X & Y point data for total REE+, Ce, Sc, Y, La,
Nd, and Li concentrations. Each element was modeled separately using EBK 3D. In addition,
geologic plausibility was assessed qualitatively based on the similarity of the model predictions
to the actual site characteristics and the coal source history, as shown by aerial photographs and
records provided by the impoundment owners.

 Figure 30. Total REE+ distribution at Site 2 predicted by
 EBK 3D modeling at 10 ft, 30 ft and 50 ft depths at Site 2.

IACMI Final Report 42

 Figure 31. Site 2 EBK 3D model prediction of
 Li concentrations at 10 ft, 30 ft, and 50 ft depths.

The Site 2 models for REE+, Ce, La, Nd, and Li concentrations were successful at predicting
both the increasing concentration trend with depth and the systematic lateral changes in
concentration across the impoundment.
Figure 32 illustrates the change in coal sources over time at Site 2 until closure of the
impoundment in 2019. Based on the trend between REE+, Li and depth at Site 2, it is very likely
that the deeper, high concentration coal ash evident in the model at depths of 30 ft and 50 ft was
sourced from the SoApp Basin, while the shallow material around 10 ft is sourced from the IB.
SoApp Basin coal is known to be higher in REE+ than IB coals, with coal ash average Total
REE+ concentrations of ~591 ppm and ~403 ppm, respectively. Coal ash sourced from the IB is
known to have significantly higher concentrations of Fe2O3 and lower concentrations of Al2O3
than coal ash from the SoApp Basin (Taggart et al., 2016). Correlation coefficients confirm that a
strong negative correlation between depth and Fe2O3 and a strong positive correlation between
depth and Al2O3 content exists, supporting the hypothesis that the Site 2 models represent the
transition in coal source from the SoApp to the IB.

At the power plant, fly ash was collected during combustion by electrostatic precipitators and
stored in baghouse hoppers. Fly ash was then mixed with water, transported through pipes, and

IACMI Final Report 43

Manufacturing Valuable Coal-Derived Products in Southern Appalachia DE-FE0032045

sluiced into the impoundment. In most cases, bottom ash was collected from the boilers and
sluiced simultaneously with the fly ash. The larger and denser materials deposited near the
sluice, while the lighter materials settled farther away, producing gravitational segregation of
particles. The location of the sluice pipe moved over time for both impoundments.
Unfortunately, the sluice pipe location and exact ash placement within the impoundment were
not recorded.

 Figure 32. Coal source information for Site 2 since 1997.

At Site 2, ash REE+, Ce, La, and Nd concentrations are higher in the eastern and western
portions of the impoundment and lower in the central, northern, and southern portions of the
impoundment (Figure 33). While Li shows a similar spatial distribution to these elements in the
eastern portion of the impoundment and at depth, low concentrations of Li are predicted in the
western portion of the impoundment where all other well-modeled elements have a high
concentration; this trend may be explained by the moderate correlation between Li and Total
REE+, compared to the strong correlations between REE+, Ce, La, and Nd concentrations .
Based on the Site 2 model results, high concentration Sc and Y material is randomly distributed
within the impoundment, while the other elements show a strong spatial dependence. Only Y and
Sc were not successfully modeled at Site 2, likely due to the fact that Sc and Y concentrations
lacked the systematic spatial distribution noted for the other modeled elements.

Based on historical aerial photos of Site 2, the sluice point transitioned between two locations in
the northeastern portion of the impoundment from 1998 to 2013. Although there is no
photographic confirmation of sluice location after 2013, the area of low element concentrations
near the last known sluice point is likely reflective of the switch to IB coal, which happened in
2014. In addition, it is likely that the cluster of high concentration ash at 30-50 ft depths in this
area of the impoundment is related to this sluice location. Moderate density ash fractions, which
are typically highest in Total REE+ content, would be expected to settle near the sluice point.

IACMI Final Report 44

Unfortunately, no records of CCP densities or particulate sizes were recorded, so the
gravitational settling hypothesis for this trend cannot be confirmed statistically.

Figure 33. Total REE+, Ce, La, and Nd concentrations at Site 2 predicted by EBK 3D modeling.
 All maps show high concentration areas in the northeastern and southwestern portions
 of the impoundment, and lowest concentrations in the central northernmost portion.

The results of this study indicate that EBK 3D is a useful tool for modeling the spatial
distributions of elemental concentrations in a coal ash impoundment with limited sampling data
in cases where there are areas displaying systematic or clustered distributions of high element
concentrations. This type of spatial pattern occurs when an impoundment is relatively
undisturbed, only a few coal sources are used over its lifetime, the coal source is consistent for
long periods of time, and the concentration of the element of interest is spatially dependent.

The EBK 3D modeling methods outlined in this study can be applied to other coal ash
impoundments where limited samples have been collected. If the impoundment meets the criteria
above, these methods can be used to target areas of the impoundment which contain coal ash
having high concentrations of REE+ or Li for use as a supply chain feedstock. Furthermore, if
the coal sources used by the facility are limited and the major element oxide concentrations are
known for each sample, correlations between the ash depth and major element oxide content can
be used to identify the coal source. Because the average REE+ concentrations of major U.S. coal
sources are known, major element oxide contents can be used as a proxy to target areas of the
impoundment most likely to contain ash with high REE+ concentrations. Therefore, XRF

IACMI Final Report 45

analysis of Fe and Al oxide contents could provide a useful and cheaper alternative to REE+
analyses to assess the feedstock potential of ponded coal ash samples.

Assessment of the Ore Potential of All Materials
Seredin and Dai (2012) proposed a method for assessing the ore potential for coal and ash based
on the ratio of critical REY (CREE: Nd, Eu, Tb, Dy, Er, Y) to excess REY (Ce, Ho, Tm, Yb and
Lu), which was defined as the outlook coefficient (Coutl). REE-enriched materials were classified
into three categories, based on their calculated Coutl and REYdef,rel% values. REYdef,rel% was
defined as the percentage of CREE in the total REY (REE+Y). The REY feedstock potential
categories were defined as follows:
 Category I. Unpromising (REYdef rel% ≤ 26% and Coutl ≤ 0.7)
        Category II. Promising (30% ≤ REYdef, rel% ≤ 51%; 0.7 ≤ Coutl ≤1.9)
 Category III. Highly Promising (REYdef, rel% ≥ 60% and Coutl ≥ 2.4)
Figure 34 is a plot of REYdef rel% vs Coutl for the coal-associated sediment and waste samples
collected for this project. All but one roof rock and three underclay samples classify as Category
II (Promising) REY feedstock materials, as defined by Seredin and Dai (2012) and shown on the
plot within the ellipse.

  Figure 34. Plot of REYdef rel% vs Coutl for the 34 coal-associated sediment and waste samples,
 excluding AMD waters. The blue ellipse indicates the samples that classify as Category II
 (Promising) REY feedstock materials.

IACMI Final Report 46

Figure 35 plots values of REYdef rel% vs Coutl for samples collected for the Supplemental Scope of
Work (13 ball clay, 2 kaolin, 8 lignite, 2 bentonite, 2 unclassified clay, 4 phosphate ore, 4 shaley
sandstone, 20 organic shale, 2 phosphogypsum waste, and 17 bauxitic clay samples). Most of
the samples are classified as Category II (Promising), but most of the bauxitic clay and ball clay
samples, one unclassified clay, one bentonite, two lignite, and two organic shale samples classify
as Category I (Unpromising) as feedstock materials. Although the two phosphogypsum waste
samples have REYdef, rel% values slightly less than 60%, their outlook coefficient exceeds 4.0,
indicating enrichment in CREEs and supporting their classification as Category III (Highly
Promising) feedstock materials.

Figure 35. Plot of REYdef rel% vs Coutl for samples collected for the Supplemental Scope of Work.
  The blue ellipse indicates samples classified as Category II (Promising) and the green ellipse
 indicates lignite samples proposed as Category III (Highly Promising) feedstock materials.

The calculated values of REYdef rel% vs Coutl for coal ash samples are plotted in Figure 36. REYdef
rel% values ranged from 28-52% and Coutl values ranged between 0.57 and 1.76. Almost all of the
ash samples therefore classify as Category II (Promising) feedstock materials.

 The blue ellipse indicates ash samples classifying as Category II (Promising).

Sample Characterization Summary
 • Coal processing waste has the highest average Li concentration of the 15 different types
 of material collected for this project.
 • Lignite samples have the highest average REE and REE+Y+Sc (REE+) contents, but the
 average is skewed by one sample with very high concentrations.
 • Clay (kaolin and ball clay) average REE contents are comparable to those of phosphate
 ore.
 • Coal underclay, coal processing waste, and ponded coal ash contain economic amounts of
 lithium.
 • REEs are primarily associated with phosphate minerals in coal-associated sediments and
 coal ash.
 • REEs are associated with phosphate and alumina in organic shale samples.
 • REE are associated with phosphate and likely with ion exchange sites in kaolin and ball
 clay samples.

IACMI Final Report 48

Manufacturing Valuable Coal-Derived Products in Southern Appalachia DE-FE0032045

 • Coal ash has high average REE, REE+, and Li contents.

 • Total REE+ concentrations of CCP materials are >300 ppm for all but one ash
 impoundment.

 • There are relatively high concentrations of Nd and Y in coal ash, which are considered
 CREEs.

 • REE and other CM are associated with trace minerals not identifiable by XRD, so other
 techniques must be used to identify CM-mineral associations.

 • Micro-XRF mapping suggests REE in coal ash are primarily associated with phosphate
 minerals and organic matter, contrary to previously reported REE-mineral associations in
 coal ash.

 • 3D EBK modeling promises to be useful for targeting high REE and Li zones within
 undisturbed CCP impoundments.

 • Although the vast majority of the samples collected for this project classify as Promising
 REE feedstock materials using the classification method proposed by Seredin (2010) and
 refined by Seredin and Dai (2012), development of efficient REE extraction technologies
 will be required for economic recovery of these critical elements.

Results: Coal Basin Mapping
Alabama Coalfields Project
To create geologic models of coal seams and related strata in IHSPetra, the Geological Survey of
Alabama (GSA) compiled sample locations from the National Coal Resource Data System
(NCRDS), the U.S. Geological Survey coal quality (COALQUAL) database, and the National
Coal Quality Inventory (NaCQI) and all Alabama oil and gas wells in the study area (Subtask
2.1). Data uploaded from the NCRDS, COALQUAL, and NaCQI databases includes site name,
location, and formation and seam name for each sample. Data uploaded for the oil and gas wells
includes well name, type, status, location, total depth and all existing stratigraphic information in
the GSA files. The GSA also searched the literature for information on the stratigraphy and
structure of the coal and related strata in Alabama, Georgia, and Tennessee to make correlations
in the sub-basins that cross state lines and are connected. A data template for creating IHSPetra
models was created and shared with other Task 2 Team members.
GSA produced structure contour and coal thickness (isocore) maps of coal seams in the sub-
basins of the study area: Black Warrior Basin, Blount Mountain, Cahaba Coal Basin, Coosa Coal
Basin, Plateau, Sand-Lookout Mountains. Maps for the Mary Lee coal seam (Black Warrior
Basin), Clark and Gholson coal seams (Cahaba Basin), and the Fairview coal seam (Coosa
Basin) were completed. Coal seam outcrop map digitization was completed for the major coal
seams of all sub-basins in northern Alabama and maps showing coal outcrop traces and fault
traces were finalized. All maps, outcrop traces, and structural features were uploaded to EDX as
ArcGIS shapefiles (https://edx.netl.doe.gov/dataset/alabama-coalfields-project-maps-outcrop-

IACMI Final Report 49

traces-and-structural-features-soapp-basin-fe0032045). All structural and thickness data,
including location, ground elevations, datum elevation, formation top depth, coal cycle top
depth, coal seam top depth, coal seam thickness, and fault cut depths, were uploaded to EDX as
comma-delimited text files (https://edx.netl.doe.gov/dataset/alabama-coalfields-project-study-
area-and-structure-contour-maps-soapp-basin-fe0032045). Stratigraphic columns and data tables
for each sub-basin and a preliminary correlation analysis were also produced and uploaded to
EDX as pdf files (https://edx.netl.doe.gov/dataset/statigraphic-columns-for-alabama-georgia-
and-south-tennessee-coalfields-soapp-basin-fe0032045).

Southern Tennessee Coalfields Coal Reserve Project
The GIS coal resource map project for the Southern Tennessee Coalfields was completed using
the ArcGIS Mapping Software ArcMap 10.8.1. The study area encompasses approximately
seventy 7.5-minute quadrangles. The coal horizons and outcrops were digitized from geologic
quadrangles and coal maps. LiDAR imagery was used to improve the accuracy of the coal mine
excavations and outcrop locations. In addition, shapefiles of relevant sites from the U.S. Office
of Surface Mining and Reclamation (OSMRE) and the Tennessee Mine Land Reclamation
Section (TMLRS) were added to the GIS project. Information from these shapefiles included
mine adits, openings, mine refuse areas, and acid mine water discharge points.
These coal maps contain almost 3,700 mine feature points that were interpreted from LiDAR
imagery which in most cases were not previously identified on any geologic or topographic map.
These mine features included gob piles, mine adits, mine collapses, mine excavations, prospect
pits, prospect trenches, and ventilation shafts.
The LiDAR Hillshade imagery was a great tool to determine the location of geologic contacts
and the stratigraphic location of the various coal seams, but requires some geologic
reinterpretation. Many old mine features were catalogued using the LiDAR Hillshade imagery,
including old adits, ventilation shafts, gob piles, and prospect pits and trenches. These features
help constrain the coal seam horizons. Approximately 750 coal mine maps and coal geology
maps were scanned to supplement the coal mine information obtained from published maps.
These maps were borrowed from engineering firms, coal mine museums, and local geologists.
Coal horizons and outcrops were digitized from geologic quadrangles and coal maps. LiDAR
imagery was used to improve the accuracy of the coal mine excavations and outcrop locations.
In addition, shapefiles of relevant sites from OSMRE and TMLRS were added to the GIS
project. Information from these shapefiles include mine adits, openings, mine refuse areas, and
acid mine water discharges. These coal maps also consist of approximately 684 strip mine
excavations that were interpreted from LiDAR imagery including contour strip mines, area strip
mines, and underground mine benches (face ups). Approximately 635 underground mine extents
were delineated from digitized mine maps that were scanned during this project. Approximately
18 auger highwall mine extents were defined from the same scanned mine maps. Almost 300
coal seam thicknesses were collected from mine map information, outcrop measurements, drill
hole data, and prospect and roadcut measurements.

 Task 3: Basinal Strategies for Reuse of Waste Streams

Assessment of beneficial ash use opportunities
The Southern Appalachian Basin states (Alabama, Georgia, and TN, hereafter AL-GA-TN)
contains almost 250M tons of coal ash stored in surface impoundments (ponded ash). This
constitutes roughly 10% of US ponded ash inventory and is stored mainly at Southern Company
and Tennessee Valley Authority (TVA) sites. Because of this high regional ash inventory,
identifying potential avenues for ash beneficial use is a major project priority.
Most fly ash currently produced is sold into beneficial use applications. The fly ash market is
experiencing some uncertainty due to diminishing fly ash supplies with declining coal-fired
power generation, nevertheless it remains strong. This project’s attention is therefore on
identifying and developing beneficial uses for ponded ash, which has considerably lower value
than production fly ash while also being more costly to extract and process.
The following key constraints were identified that guide the analysis of potential ash beneficial
use opportunities and pathways.
 • Compliance with relevant regulations such as Environmental Protection Agency criteria 1;
 • Potential for delivering a sound business case;
 • Priority on mass markets that use appreciable quantities of ash, e.g., > 100,000 tons/year;
 • Minimum waste – e.g., processes for extracting rare earth elements (REE) must also
 render the ash residual (99+% of the total mass) into (a) form(s) that can also be used
 beneficially; and
 • Preferably contributes to decarbonization; at minimum life-cycle analysis of the product
 suite indicates carbon neutrality.

AL-GA-TN Ash Inventory
There are 219 million cubic yards of coal ash in AL-GA-TN stored in 58 surface impoundments
(see Table 7). The vast majority of these facilities are operated by Southern Company and the
Tennessee Valley Authority. The coal ash stored in these impoundments is mostly fly ash with
smaller amounts of bottom ash mixed in. Coal ash was initially disposed of in these
impoundments because the market wasn’t fully developed or equipment investments had not
been made at the time it was produced. Ongoing additions to the many impoundments represent
the small amount of fly ash not sold for beneficial uses.
Surface impoundments vary in terms of their current operation and the method used to close the
facility. Thirty-two of these surface impoundments are closed or the operator has submitted a
formal notice that it will stop dumping coal ash into the unit and begin the closure process in the
near future (“Notice of Intent to Close”). Forty facilities will close the impoundment by leaving

1 https://www.epa.gov/coalash/frequent-questions-about-beneficial-use-coal-ash

IACMI Final Report 52

the coal ash where it is and “capping” it with a cover. At these sites, the operator must continue
groundwater monitoring and other post-closure care for the unit for at least 30 years. Twenty-
seven facilities will close the impoundment by excavating the coal ash and transporting it to a
different disposal unit for permanent disposal. 2

Near-Term Market Opportunities
The current market for ponded ash is very limited. The near-term market opportunities are
deemed to be those markets that have already been penetrated by fly ash. Declining fly ash
supplies can potentially be offset by substituting bottom/pond ash. Barriers to such substitution
include the cost of extracting and processing bottom/pond ash vs. the cost of mining and
processing competing raw materials, as well as chemical and dimensional consistency/quality of
bottom/pond ash. Unencapsulated uses are inhibited by costly documentation and record-keeping
requirements, especially those related to environmental, safety, and health concerns.
Development of co-value streams from bottom/pond ash may improve its potential for use in
near-term markets that currently use fly ash. For example, if REEs are extracted (see discussion
below) in a way that secures their value while leaving the ash residual (99+% of the mass) in a
condition that is readily usable in current fly ash markets, that synergistic value creation can
make the combination of markets economically viable when neither or none of them alone are
viable. It also satisfies the aforementioned waste minimization criterion.
Primary current fly ash markets include cement production, concrete production (block, precast,
and ready-mix), mine reclamation, and structural and roadway fills 3. There are 92 concrete
producers in AL-TN; mostly in Alabama. There are also 95 ready mix concrete producers in
Alabama and Tennessee. Cement producers are less prevalent. Due to the cost of transporting
coal ash, proximity to coal ash surface impoundments is an economic advantage for these
producers. Concrete/cement producers in Alabama and Tennessee are on average 70 and 115
miles from a coal ash impoundment, respectively.

Rare Earth Elements
REE production from coal and coal wastes has been consistently and repeatedly articulated as a
DOE priority and is included in the work scope of most (or all) of the 13 CORE-CM projects.
AL-GA-TN bottom/pond ash typically contains REE at concentrations > 400 ppm. This is
considerably higher than REE concentrations in most raw coals. These concentrations would
imply that a single Southern Appalachian coal ash impoundment may contain up to 15,000 tons
of rare earth oxide – roughly 1% of U.S. reserves. Because of the Southern Appalachian basin’s
massive volume of REE-rich coal ash, REE extraction/separation from coal ash is a project
priority.
As previously noted, economic feasibility of ash-based REE is anticipated to require multiple
value streams and beneficial use of ash residual after extraction. This imposes significant
constraints on the REE extraction/separation process(es). Existing prototypical and pilot REE
extraction processes will be evaluated, especially those in development by other CORE-CM
project teams or in other DOE-funded projects. Other potential extraction / separation schemes
with high potential for leaving the ash in good condition for co-product manufacturing will be

4 These market opportunities are sourced from unpublished studies and reports available to the project team

 58

evaluated. Unique high performance computational modeling is a distinctive capability of the
project team that will be deployed toward evaluating REE extraction and separations processes.
Figure 37 illustrates key elements of the project team’s REE evaluation and innovation ecosystem.

 Figure 37. Southern
 Appalachian REE ecosystem

 Figure 37. Southern Appalachian REE ecosystem
Asphalt
Asphalt is a very interesting potential market due to its size – tens of millions of tons annually –
and its similarity to concrete and roadway fill applications. Fly ash is currently used in asphalt,
however in disappointingly low volume. As of 2018, there was only 10,000 tons used as mineral
filler in asphalt reported in the United States. 5 Bottom/pond ash will face additional challenges
as discussed in the section on near-term potential markets.
Project manager IACMI has a relationship with the Western Research Institute (WRI), which is
renowned for its expertise and research on asphalt. WRI is currently conducting research on the
use of coal ash in asphalt. WRI is located in Laramie, WY, proximate to the University of
Wyoming (UW) which is managing two CORE-CM projects. The project PI is a UW graduate
and IACMI also has important relationships with UW specifically addressing coal-to-products
ambitions. The project team will capitalize on the confluence of these organizational and project

5 Coal Ash Recycling Rate, 2019

 59

relationships to collaborate on evaluating the potential technical and business cases for using
coal ash in asphalt and application of those findings to AL-GA-TN coal ash inventories.
Polymer fillers
Plastics, metal, paints, rubber, and adhesives are potential industries for ash beneficial use. Ash
can be used as mineral fillers in composites, replacing products such as calcium carbonate, silica
and carbon fillers. It can add stiffness and strength to the product as well as lower manufacturing
costs. Some concerns with using coal ash in these materials include inconsistent ash quality and
particle size, color limitations, and metals concentrations in the ash prohibiting some use. Many
of potential applications for coal ash in these industries are still being researched and some
manufacturers have successfully used them as alternative fillers in carpet backing, shingles, and
floor matting.
This application is particularly interesting to the project team. The market size could easily
exceed 100,000 tons/year with significant growth potential. The project team has exceptional
expertise in polymers and polymer composites. IACMI manages a consortium of more than 100
industrial companies in the US composites industry, including resin manufacturers and
compounders. Project participants University of Tennessee, Knoxville and University of
Alabama, Birmingham have unique expertise and capabilities that enable them to thoroughly
evaluate, further develop, and produce prototypical products with ash-filled polymers. Therefore
it is the project team’s intent to conduct a thorough evaluation of the potential for using coal ash
as a filler in resins for mass market composite materials applications with decarbonization
impact such as electric vehicles, wind turbines, and energy efficient building construction.

Other Developing Applications
The technical and economic feasibility of several potential market opportunities are not well
understood including masonry blocks (bricks, cinder blocks, pavers etc.), ceramic tiles, and
refractory liners. Technical feasibility of masonry blocks and ceramic tiles have been
demonstrated in India and China. The limiting factor in the U.S. is economics. Production of
masonry blocks and ceramic tiles in India and China is likely heavily subsidized or leverages
vertically integrated firm structures that improve the economic feasibility of these technologies.
The project team will make a screening evaluation of these developing applications, followed by
a deeper dive on any application determined to merit further attention.

Summary
The large volume of ash and other coal combustion products stored and ponded in AL-GA-TN
represents a significant untapped strategic mineral resource. Most fly ash currently produced is
sold into beneficial use applications. Primary current fly ash markets include cement production,
concrete production (block, precast, and ready-mix), mine reclamation, and structural and
roadway fills. Several long-term market opportunities have been identified for evaluation within
this project including rare earth elements, asphalt, and polymer fibers. While technological
readiness has been demonstrated for many of these applications, economic feasibility remains a
critical hurdle to widespread deployment in the U.S. Promising strategies to improve the
economic feasibility of these developing applications include minimizing impacts to other
existing uses (e.g., cement and concrete), developing valuable co-products, and developing clean

 60

production processes that limit the costly handling of any waste by-products. The large quantities
of ash combined with a healthy cement and concrete industry makes the Southern Appalachian
Basin an ideal test case for further developing these applications.

Coal ash storage facility valuation tool
The findings from Task 2 suggest that coal ash contains concentrations of rare earth elements
comparable to many hard rock mining sites. However, it is unclear whether these resources are
economically viable. Placing an economic value on mine assets is common. Indeed several
studies have been undertaken to place an economic value on likely production from rare earth
elements mines across the globe. O’Brien et al. (2024) develop a cost estimator tool for rare earth
mining projects that can be used to approximate the capital and operating expenditures of
greenfield REE mining projects. However, little is known about the economic viability of
unconventional rare earth element feedstocks such as coal ash storage facilities. This knowledge
gap makes it difficult to assess the degree to which unconventional REE sources will attract
private capital, the need for federal incentives to support domestic REE production, and the
overall characterization of domestic REE resources.

To address this knowledge gap, researchers at UTK (Sims), Southern Company
(Flowers/Yeboah), TVA, and Alabama (Donahoe) developed an initial prototype for an asset
valuation tool for coal storage sites. The tool, programmed in Matlab, provides the economic
value of the coal ash storage site, the economically preferred ash harvest strategy, and the
expected lifetime of the REE resources at the site based on information on ash volume, REE
concentrations, and three types of costs: extraction costs, refining cost, and residual storage
costs. The valuation tool accounts for several novel features of REE extraction from coal ash
feedstocks:
 1) current ash beneficial use markets and opportunities (see Task 4 for details)
 2) federal regulations around landfilling and long-term storage of coal ash
 3) REE concentrations based on coal ash sampling results provided by University of Alabama at
 8 different coal ash impoundments in the SoApp basin (see Task 2 for details)
 4) economies of scale in coal ash extraction

Methods
The asset valuation tool calculates the value of the coal ash storage facility as the solution to a
Markov decision model which uses dynamic programming to solve for the schedule of ash
extraction over time that maximizes the value of the coal ash storage facility. Thus, instead of
calculating economic feasibility from a pre-determined extraction plan, the asset valuation tool
calculates the maximum economic value of the site based on an economically rational extraction
plan determined by the valuation tool itself. Other suboptimal extraction plans may also lead to
an economically viable resource. However, the values reported by the valuation tool should be
interpreted as a best-case scenario given model parameters and assumptions.

The valuation tool decides how much ash to extract from an ash storage facility that will be
closed and abandoned after T years of operation. The price of extracted Rare Earth Oxide (REO)
 61

is p dollar per thousand tons, and the total cost of extracting x thousand tons of REO in any year,
given that the storage facility contains s thousand tons at the beginning of the year is C2(s,x)
dollars. The storage facility currently contains s0 thousand tons of REO. Refining and separating
the REO from the extracted ash costs C1(x) dollars. Storing any residual material produced from
the refining and separation process costs C3(x) dollars. C3(x) will be positive if the residual
material cannot be sold in existing ash markets and will represent the cost of storing the residual
material either on-site or after being moved to an off-site landfill. C3(x) will be negative if the
residual material is of a quality that would allow it to be sold in existing ash markets and
represents the additional revenue generated from those sales.

The annual net benefits from REO extraction from an ash storage facility is f(s,x) = px - C1(x) -
C2(s,x) - C3(x). Based on conversations with the utility partners the following functional forms
for the cost functions are assumed:
       C1(x) = c1*x
 C2(s,x) = (a*xj)/(1+b*s) where j=0 implies no economies of scale in production
       C3(x) = c3*x

The value of the ash storage facility, given that it contains s thousand tons of REO at the
beginning of year t, satisfies the Bellman equation
Vt(s) = maxx {px - C1(x) - C2(s,x) - C3(x) + βVt+1(s-x)}
subject to the terminal condition
VT+1(s) = 0
where β = 1/(1+r) is the annual discount factor and r is the discount rate set by the user.

The Matlab interface allows users to input various parameter values such as discount rates,
prices, and REO concentrations. Once all required information is provided, the user initiates the
calculations by triggering a dynamic programing algorithm programmed by Sims. This algorithm
generates a schedule of ash extraction quantities, the dynamics of the ash supply, the time until
the ash supply is exhausted, and the value of the ash storage facility for REO production. The
algorithm automatically recasts the Bellman fixed point equation as a root finding problem that is
solved using Newton’s method. Newton’s method applied to the Bellman equation of a discrete
Markov decision model traditionally has been referred to as policy iteration.

Resource characterization data for the valuation tool is drawn from Task 2 sampling activities.
REE concentrations in coal ash (g REE/ton) are calculated based on coal ash sampling results
provided by University of Alabama at 8 different coal ash impoundments in the SoApp basin.
REE concentrations were converted to stocks of 12 different REO (Sc, Y, La, Pr, Nd, Sm, Eu,
Gd, Tb, Dy, Er, Yb) using initial ash volume at these 8 different coal ash impoundments
(provided by utilities), molar mass of the metal and molar mass of the oxide.

Economic data were drawn from several different sources. REO prices ($/thousand tons) were
taken from USGS. Information about costs and technical feasibility of coal ash extraction,
removal, transportation, and storage were drawn from conversations with utility representatives
at a 1-day workshop on coal ash beneficial use held at the Baker School at the University of
Tennessee campus. During this workshop, utility representatives shared their best available cost
information and provided insight on the feasibility of coal ash extraction from ponded sources
given the current regulatory climate. Areas of consistency between utilities were noted and

 63

averages were taken when utility estimates diverged. Information about ash beneficial use
markets was also collected from the utility partners including quantities sold and price.
For purposes of demonstration, the valuation tool makes three key underlying assumptions that
can be relaxed with additional information:
 • All products are sold as REO
 • 100% REO recovery
 • Rare earths deemed “excessive” as excluded from the analysis
Results
The results produced from the asset valuation tool are reported in Table 11 and Figure 38. All
ash impoundments are predicted to produce less than 1,000 tons of total REO in the first year
with production falling each year as the available REO quantity falls. All economic REO
reserves are exhausted in 4-18 years. The total value of these ash impoundments in terms of only
REO production ranges from $5-93 million depending on the composition and concentration of
REO in the ash and the total quantity of ash.

The results in Table 11 only consider revenues from the production of REO. If REO can be
separated the ash and the residual from this separation process can continue to be sold in existing
ash markets, the economic viability of REO extraction from coal ash improves. To illustrate, one
can calculate the breakeven refining and separation cost necessary to ensure a positive stream of
profits from REO extraction. This breakeven cost for plant 1 is given by the solid black line in
Figure 39.

 x 104

 Figure 39. Break-even refining and separation cost necessary to ensure positive flow of profits
 from REO extraction at an ash impoundment in Southern Appalachian basin.

Traditional chemical (leaching) separation methods will render any residual unsuitable for the
construction coal ash market. In this case the marginal cost of storing the residual will be
positive and the breakeven cost for a separation and refining technology is less than $15,000 per
thousand tons of REO equivalent. If the residual must be landfilled offsite, REO extraction is not
economically viable for any separation and refining cost.

Alternative separation methods such as biological and magnetic separation techniques detailed in
Task 5 would, in theory, would produce a residual that is chemically identical to coal ash and
should be suitable for construction coal ash markets. In this case the marginal cost of storing the
residual will be negative and the breakeven cost for a separation and refining technology can
exceed $20,000 per thousand tons of REO equivalent. Thus alternative separation techniques will
allow producers to leverage existing construction coal ash markets and improve the economic
viability of REO extraction from coal ash.

 Task 4: Basinal Strategies for Infrastructure, Industries, and Business

Existing Business and Industry Structure
The analysis begins with the identification of products that may utilize rare inputs. These
products are then aligned with industry sectors as defined by the standard accounting framework
of the North American Industrial Classification System (NAICS). Data on the number of
business establishments and employment for the identified industry sectors is then presented for
the three-state region that includes Alabama, Georgia and Tennessee, as well as the U.S. An
important finding is that the three-state region has a greater relative presence (or specialization)
of industry sectors using or potentially using rare inputs than the nation. The implication is that
the region has the opportunity to be a major user of rare inputs, supporting industrial production
and job creation and reducing dependence on rare input imports.
The first step in the analysis is identification of products that may use REE, CM and CBP, using
publicly-available resources and the expert judgement of the research team supporting this
project. 6 Table 12 provides a listing of products using or potentially using rare inputs. Most of
these are intermediate products that are used to produce other intermediate products, though
some final products that are further downstream in the supply chain are on the list as well. For
example, rare inputs may be used to make construction materials, including bricks. These bricks
may then be used in a variety of construction applications. Batteries may be an end product for
direct use by a consumer or an input to the production of a computer or another product.

The second step is to link the products shown in Table 12 to specific industry sectors, a process
that requires judgement calls. For example, body armor may be produced using CM/CBP.
However, as the NAICS data show, there are 24 different industry sectors that produce or utilize
some form of body armor. In some instances, judgement calls are straightforward across these
sectors. For example, NAICS 561612, security guards and patrol services, is not included since
this sector reflects provision of a service that is several steps downstream from the direct use of
rare inputs. The same applies to NAICS 928110, national security, another service activity
several links away from direct rare input use. 7 The focus here is on sectors that are directly
involved in the use of rare inputs or just one step downstream in the production process, a
distinction that is difficult to make in practice.

and their business employment and establishment ecosystem in the southern Appalachian region.
