Note from Tables 15-16 that Alabama and Tennessee are driving these overall findings, with
Georgia individually lagging the U.S. in terms of the share of both employment and
establishments linked to the possible use of rare inputs. 9 (Because of Georgia’s size, it has a
large number of establishments and jobs in the target sectors.) The establishment share figures
are less than 1 percent for all the regions while the employment share figures are all in excess of
2 percent.
Relative employment shares are higher and thus the degree of specialization is greater in the
three-state region than the U.S. in all sectors other than 3335, 3336, 3341, 3342, 3344, 3345 and
3359. With a smaller number of exceptions, the relative share of business establishments tends to
be higher in the three states than the U.S. There are 263,833 manufacturing jobs in the southern
Appalachian region, with 78,167 in Alabama, 91,768 in Georgia and 93,898 in Tennessee.
Counts of establishments at the county level in Alabama, Georgia, and Tennessee, on average,
range between zero and three for the NAICS codes of interest. The small number of firms
explains why employment data for the key sectors is suppressed at the county level, as
previously discussed. Summing across all of the four-digit NAICS reveals that there is an
average of 11 total establishments for counties in Georgia. Similarly, there is an average of 18
and 17 total establishments for counties in Alabama and Tennessee. Fulton County in Georgia,
which encompasses most of Atlanta, has the highest number of total establishments (212
establishments), followed by Gwinnett County in Georgia (201 establishments). Ranking third
and fourth is Jefferson and Madison County in Alabama, with a total of 173 and 146
establishments, respectively. Davidson County in Tennessee, which includes Nashville, has a
total of 145 establishments across the sectors of interest.
Together these data indicate that the Alabama-Georgia-Tennessee region offers a fertile base to
exploit the application of rare inputs. The industries identified here will have an existing supply
chain and a trained workforce that should be able to adapt to new production technologies. In
addition, the overall business environment is already supportive of manufacturing business
activity by virtue of the presence of a large share of employment and establishments in the
region.

Preliminary Assessment of Essential Infrastructure to Support CORE-CM Businesses:
Transportation Networks, Electricity and Broadband
Building the business case to support beneficial use of resources extracted from ash ponds
requires ensuring that essential infrastructure is in place to support business operations, including
transportation networks, electricity (and natural gas) and broadband. In practice, potential
business activity can be arrayed along a continuum to include separation processes applied to
pond ash, intermediate input production from extracted rare earth elements (REEs), the
production of final products for consumer and business use that embody REEs and wholesaling
along all stages of the continuum. The focus here is on separation processes and first stages of
their productive use and essential infrastructure.

9 This is consistent with the states’ relative reliance on the overall manufacturing sector where Georgia places 3rd among the three

states. U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages, 2020.

 74

The use of fly ash in the production of concrete, cement blocks, bricks and related products
should be viewed as a success story that demonstrates broadly the efficacy of underlying forces
supporting market-based use of coal-based waste, including infrastructure. Especially important
has been the proximate location of large volumes of coal ash to cement and concrete producers.
Because of the large volume and modest value of fly ash, proximity to producers has been
critical to facilitating the maturation of regional markets. Large volumes of fly ash have been
moved efficiently and safely by both rail and trucks along established transportation networks to
support production.
The exploitation of REEs from coal waste has not seen the same success. While global markets
are mature in the sense that REE suppliers have been able to connect to producers, domestic
markets simply do not have robust supply chains from source materials. This is in part an artifact
of dominance by international suppliers of REEs, especially China, which has hampered the
cultivation of domestic supplies. Extracting REEs from coal waste has its own unique challenges
that will have to be addressed to ensure the establishment of more complete domestic markets.
These challenges include:
 • Incomplete pond characterization. There is still an incomplete characterization of ash
 ponds and the scope and variation of embodied REEs. Variations in source coal, the
 nature of coal use and ash storage mechanisms contribute to this heterogeneity. The
 Southern Company is in the process of evaluating its ash ponds. Preliminary testing
 indicates REE concentrations of 400-500 ppm or nearly one pound of REEs per ton of
 ash; this level, along with the distribution of specific REEs, varies across sites.
 • Unproven separation technologies. While separation of REEs from coal ash is technically
 feasible using chemical and other processes, there is no market-proven, scale-level
 separation technology available today for ash ponds. Moreover, it is not clear whether
 separation processes can extract unique REEs from ponds without damaging the capacity
 to extract other REEs or usable resources from the same ash. This is important since the
 resource value of the ash ponds is the joint value of the product streams that can be
 extracted. The absence of an economically-feasible technology hampers supply and
 broader market development.
 • Uncertain market deployment. There is limited publicly-available data and information
 on REEs and their use in the supply chain and the production of intermediate and final
 products. This means uncertainty in the potential scope of REEs take-up when supplies
 ultimately become available.
 • Stigma. There is a stigma associated with coal waste generally and ash ponds in
 particular. This is part perception and part reality. There have been visible ash spills and
 coal ash does contain elements that can be harmful to both health and the environment,
 including arsenic, cadmium, mercury and lead. These issues will hamper the willingness
 of some firms and the communities they locate in to take advantage of potential business
 opportunities tied to ash ponds and extracted REEs.
The first two challenges identified here are perhaps most important to overcome in the near term.
Pond characterization will indicate potential REE supplies while separation technologies and
their costs will determine the extent to which extraction is economically feasible. The emergence

 75

Final Report DE-FE0032045

of viable suppliers and a supply chain will erode the market’s limited information problem while
economic opportunities and demonstrated successes from REE extraction and use can help offset
stigma concerns.
In the remainder of this preliminary assessment, the focus falls on the ability of essential
infrastructure to support REE extraction and their productive use in the Southern Appalachian
Region of Alabama, Georgia and Tennessee. The primary conclusion is that there appears to be
adequate infrastructure capacity to support the development of domestic supplies of REEs and
their use.

Infrastructure and Economic Development
Economists generally model infrastructure like transportation systems, energy supplies and
communication networks as primary elements of a region’s ability to produce, along with
common inputs like labor and private capital investment deployed by individual firms. The
significant scope of infrastructure investment on the part of public and private bodies has
motivated a vast literature seeking to evaluate infrastructure’s impact on economic development.
This research has produced surprisingly mixed findings, regardless of the type of infrastructure
studied. An example is a recent study for the Tennessee Department of Transportation that
sought to evaluate the effects of the state’s major highways and interstates on county-level
growth. 10 The report reviews the broader literature on infrastructure and economic development,
documenting mixed findings and the daunting challenges confronted by researchers. The
empirical analysis, applied to Tennessee, failed to identify strong causal links between roadway
spending and local growth as measured by metrics like jobs and personal income. Findings like
this are common in the literature.
One practical interpretation of this research is that infrastructure is an enabler of economic
activity, not a primary causal factor. In other words, the presence of infrastructure doesn’t
necessarily cause growth to occur but instead accommodates growth that is driven by underlying
market forces. In some instances, the absence of infrastructure can be a bottleneck to growth. 11
For example, building out high-speed internet requires service take-up and use on the part of
households and businesses that have viable ideas and practices; internet access alone does not
create viable enterprises. But the absence of broadband can in principle keep ideas from coming
to the market. This interpretation is consistent with the view that roads to nowhere are a waste of
resources. Roads, like communication systems, must have a network of viable users to make
them good investments for the public sector and private enterprises.
For businesses involved in the processing of coal waste and the use of extracted inputs, there will
be considerable heterogeneity in the nature of required essential infrastructure and its use. For
example, battery production is highly energy intensive which means that access to natural gas as

10 See Matthew N. Murray and Jilleah G. Welch, “Evaluating the Private Sector Returns to Transportation Investments in

Tennessee, prepared for the Tennessee Department of Transportation, March 19, 2021.
https://www.tn.gov/content/dam/tn/tdot/long-range-planning/research/final-reports/res2020-final-reports/RES2020-
01_Final_Report_Approved.pdf
11
 Note that infrastructure quality is also very important. For example, moving freight requires a more advanced
road system than what would be required for personal vehicles. Similarly, Internet access can vary from basic
service to high-speed fiber.

 76

Final Report DE-FE0032045

an energy source may be critical; for many other firms, access to electricity to support production
may prove sufficient. Businesses that make complex use of data and information, including
accessing super computing capacity, may require high-speed fiber internet access while other
businesses can utilize more basic services. Firms that work in the realm of ideas may have little
need for advanced roadways, while businesses that move coal and coal waste must have
advanced roadways that can support freight movement and ensure public safety. A good example
is the development of the market for the use of fly ash in cement and concrete production which
would never have occurred absent good roads and rail connections. 12 This is another case where
the absence of a network could create a market bottleneck. Greater distance in general means
greater cost; distance and associated transportation costs can limit or preclude market
development.

Ash Ponds as a Productive Input Supply Source
Coal ash is currently designated and regulated as a solid waste. 13 The public views ash sites as a
nuisance and community hazard. An important alternative perspective is that ash ponds are a
source of supply for productive inputs that can be used to produce things of value to society. The
use of fly ash in cement and concrete is once again an excellent example. Despite the practical
challenges, coal ash ponds should be marketed as a productive input source that can help drive
regional innovation and economic activity. This may enhance the willingness of business
investors and local communities to embrace economic activity around ash pond resource
exploitation and use. Society had to overcome a similar but more significant perception problem
with nuclear reactors and power generation.
Separation and extractive processes could be applied to coal ash in two very different settings.
The first is a hub and spoke model that would move encapsulated ash from individual ponds or
other storage sites to a central hub processing facility. The capacity to move large volumes of ash
has been demonstrated in the context of fly ash. However, relatively high transportation costs
would be incurred to ship large volumes of ash that embody small amounts of REEs. This means
that hubs would need to be located relatively close to ash sites; the expected life of investment
tied to a hub would be limited by the amount of locally-available ash. New processing sites
would need to be established and all attendant regulatory issues would need to be addressed,
leading to costs and uncertainties. Siting these facilities would be problematic as communities
may object to having them in their vicinity. Once processed, over 99 percent of the ash would
remain as a residual that would need to be stored on site or moved to an alternative storage
facility, entailing additional costs and risks.
A second option would be an on-site processing and separation facility, potentially a mobile
technology that could move from site to site on rail or truck beds. There is considerable appeal
associated with such an approach. The transportation network surrounding ash ponds is already
well established, including high-quality road and rail options. The same is generally true of ash
ponds across the country, so such a mobile technology could in principle be used at these sites as

12
 See CSX Transportation, “Safe Transportation of Coal Ash,”
https://www.csx.com/index.cfm/library/files/customers/commodities/waste/coal-ash-brochure/
13
 The Environmental Protection Agency designates ash as a solid waste under Subtitle D.

 77

Final Report DE-FE0032045

well. The ability to move processing facilities from site to site also enhances the capacity to
derive a return on associated capital investment. Proximity to power generation facilities ensures
an abundant nearby energy source to enable processing support. Communication networks could
also likely be accessed at relatively low cost at or near these sites. Finally, the ash ponds have
already satisfied federal, state and local regulatory requirements, so it may be easier to
accommodate new processing facilities relative to a greenfield site.
While this discussion is hypothetical, it points to an important potential path for ash pond
resource exploitation. Individual ash ponds could effectively be transformed into a regional
supply source, in principle analogous to any business input supplier. The extracted inputs could
support regional production activities or be shipped to users elsewhere. Once processed, the
small volumes and relatively high value of REEs make long-term shipment more attractive from
a transportation cost perspective.

Spatial Location of Ash Ponds and Regional Transportation Networks
Figure 40 provides a regional view of ash ponds in the Alabama-Georgia-Tennessee area, as well
as areas immediately adjacent to the states. There are 58 sites spread across the three-state
region, generally in close proximity to the generation facilities that produce the same ash. Many
of the sites are close to major metropolitan areas. This is important from an economic
development perspective since metropolitan areas have large, diverse and well-educated labor
pools, abundant business location sites, major universities and colleges that can offer R&D
support, and an array of business and professional services that in turn create a network for other
businesses. Metropolitan areas are also especially attractive because of their amenities which
help attract and retain workers and households.
Overlays on the maps show interstate highways and major railroad lines (generally operated by
CSX in the three-state region). With few exceptions, there are strong railroad and interstate
linkages to the ash sites. This should come as no surprise since generation facilities require a
strong transportation network to enable the shipment of large quantities of bulky coal. This
network could in principle support either of the coal ash processing options noted above—the
hub and spoke model or the mobile separation model.
An additional map overlay indicates industrial sites in the three-state region, all of which have
been certified through the respective states as business-ready. 14 Importantly, note that these
represent a small subset of industrial sites available in the states by virtue of the selection and
certification process. Many other sites are available in publicly-supported industrial parks across
the region. The certified sites generally have essential infrastructure like road connections,
communications networks (including high-speed fiber) and industrial-capacity energy supplies in
place to support fast-track business location; many sites also have rail connections, though this is

environmental review. They also tend to be well established within the communities where they
are located and thus are accepted by nearby residents. Many recognize the importance of the sites

Typology of REE Security Cost Measures
REE security cost estimates may be used to motivate policy and research and development
(R&D) action, design policy to address REE dependence, or evaluate existing policies and R&D
programs. It is unlikely that a single estimate of REE security costs can be designed to fully
capture the costs of REE dependence in all policy contexts in which these estimates will be used.
If analysts anticipate their estimate will be used to motivate policy action and R&D spending,
they strive for a “clean” measure that filters out the effect of other market failures and existing
policies to arrive at a more complete measure that accurately depicts the security costs of REE
dependence (however that is defined). If analysts anticipate their estimate will be used to
evaluate existing policies and R&D programs, they strive for a “realistic” measure that would
more accurately reflect the possible, realized benefits of policies intended to reduce REE
dependency. If analysts anticipate their estimate will be used to design policy, the type of policy
(e.g., import tariff, domestic production tax credit, federal research and development funding)
will influence how REE security costs should be estimated. Clearly, REE security costs arise
from different economic phenomena so one measure that is applicable in all settings should not
be expected. Similar issues arise when economists estimate oil security costs (Bohi and
Montgomery 2015). Thus, the appropriate question is not which measure of REE security cost is
correct but which measure is correct in a particular policy context.
To match the appropriate REE security cost measure to the appropriate policy context, the
project team proposes a two-stage taxonomy:
Stage 1 - Scoping: The first stage addresses the various cost components that arise due to a lack
of a clear definition of REE security costs. Figure 41 illustrates the cost typology. The typology
first distinguishes between pecuniary externalities (changes in the distribution of income that do
not affect REE market efficiency) and efficiency losses originating from the most common
market failure cited for REEs – supply-side market power. Pecuniary externalities arise from
changes in the terms of trade during stable market conditions due to noncompetitive REE supply
(Type 1). Efficiency losses are disaggregated into those originating from market failures in the
world REE market (Types 2 and 3) and market failures in other capital and labor markets

 80

Final Report DE-FE0032045

 Figure 41. Stage 1 typology for REE security costs

Stage 2 - Purpose: The second stage addresses the way the REE security cost estimate will be
used to inform domestic policy. For example, some REE security cost estimates will be used to
motivate policy action that has not yet taken place, others will be used to inform the design of a
particular policy such as the size of an import tariff, and others will be used to evaluate a policy
already in place. These end-use, policy considerations will influence the decision to adopt a
premium versus total cost approach and the selection of the appropriate benchmark for analysis.
Figure 42 illustrates how these policy considerations lead to four types of REE security costs.

According to this two-stage taxonomy, each REE security cost estimate is assigned a letter to
identify the method used (Figure 42) followed by a series of numbers to identify which costs
were included in the estimate (Figure 41). Table 17 provides a list of domestic policy contexts
that may be informed by REE security cost estimates. For each policy context, the taxonomy can
be applied to identify appropriate policy scope of the estimate (Stage 1) as well as the purpose of

the estimate (Stage 2). REE security cost estimates used in the context of international
agreements may also need to consider spillover impacts that accrue to other nations who are
similarly reliant on REE. These spillover impacts are not considered in the stage 1 typology
presented in this report.

Ideally, all cost components in Figure 41 would be included in all REE security cost estimates.
As with any economic analysis, there are tradeoffs between scope and accuracy. Economic
models can be designed to capture many of the cost components in Figure 41. Typically, these
more expansive models provide less accurate estimates of any single cost component. Due to
this tradeoff, cost components must be carefully matched to the policy context. For example, it
would be challenging to design an economic model that estimates the political cost of REE
dependence and changes in the terms of trade attributable to monopolistic pricing.
Hypothetical world REE markets (Type C and D) are generally the preferred benchmark when
motivating the need for a particular policy. These measures provide an indication of the
potential benefits of reducing REE imports that are more appropriate for framing questions about
the need for policy action. In contrast, a current-state-of-the-world benchmark (Type A and B) is
preferred for designing and evaluating policies since these measures provide a more accurate
measure of the impacts that will actually be experienced when the policy is enacted. Selecting
the estimation approach will depend on the policy being considered. Policies that focus on
influencing the price of world REE (e.g., import tariff) will require a premium approach (Type A
and C). Policies that focus on altering the quantity of world REE imported will require a total
cost approach (Type B and D).

 82

REE World Trade and Security Premium Estimates
Many Congressional reports and executive orders have been designed to build a secure domestic
supply chain for REE. Policymakers are particularly concerned about the threat of supply chain
disruptions, mainly because the US is 100% import reliant for RE metals. This is despite a
significant amount of mine production by the US. For example, the US mined 43,000 metric tons
of REEs in 2023, but the import reliance was still 100%. A natural question arises as to why the
US depends on imports if it mines its own. This is because of the lack of refining capabilities. US
can only mine unrefined REEs and exports them to China for refining.
Sims, working with an economics graduate student, has developed a technique for estimating an
REE security premium (Type 4 security costs in Figure 32). REE supply disruptions lead to U.S.
economic losses. Increased domestic consumption increases the economy’s vulnerability to these
shocks. US firms recognize how increased consumption of REE imports increases their exposure
to REE supply shocks and impacts their profits. But they do not recognize how their increased
consumption of REE imports impacts other firms. Specifically, greater domestic consumption
implies that a given supply shock has a bigger impact on import prices which affects all US
firms. The economic losses associated with supply disruptions— GDP losses and transfers
abroad—are costs that can be quantified as REE security premiums.
While a plethora of studies have explored US security premiums for oil (Deutch et al. (2006);
Leiby (2007), Brown and Huntington (2013), Brown (2018)), none exist for the US REE
consumption. An important point is that simple definitions of reducing imports could be
misleading. Greene et al. (2007); Greene (2010); Greene (2011) suggest that minimizing
dependence can be achieved by minimizing oil consumption or oil imports, but this is costly in
the event of supply disruptions. REE consumption and imports are not the problem. The problem
is the variety of potential market failures manifest as inefficiently high domestic REE
consumption and imports. The security premiums in this report represent the economic cost of
one such market failure.

Method
The methodology draws from Brown and Huntington (2013) who provide a welfare analytic
model of oil security premiums. The model starts by defining US welfare as a function of RE
metals consumption:

W = Y(Q) - PQ – E(ΔP)Q – E(ΔY)

Y(Q) is the US GDP associate with RE metals consumption
PQ is the US REE import costs (during stable market conditions)
E(ΔP)Q are the expected wealth transfers abroad because of REE supply disruptions
E(ΔY) are the expected GDP loss associated with price shocks resulting from REE
 supply disruptions

 83

Final Report DE-FE0032045

The welfare maximizing level of US imports ensures
                                       𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕           𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕      𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕(∆𝑃𝑃)             𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕(∆𝑌𝑌)
                                            = 𝑃𝑃 + 𝑄𝑄      + 𝑄𝑄           + 𝐸𝐸(∆𝑃𝑃) +
                                       𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕           𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕         𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕                   𝑄𝑄

The left-hand side of the above equation represents the marginal benefits of increased REE
imports from increased US GDP. The right-hand side represents the marginal costs of increased
REE imports. Clearly the marginal cost of REE imports exceed the world price from the US
perspective.

The second term on the right-hand side is the change in the terms of trade for imported REE
during stable market conditions. This term is not included in the security cost measures because
it does not represent a loss in market efficiency. The third and fourth terms capture the changes
in expected import costs during unstable market conditions. Firms will be expected to include the
fourth term since it impacts their individual profits. In other words, US companies are aware that
REE supply shocks are possible and can assess the expected magnitude of these shocks. Since
firms anticipate this fourth term, it is also not included in security premium estimates. However,
the third term is ignored by US companies and is included in the security premium estimate. The
fifth term on the right-hand side is the changes in expected GDP losses during an REE supply
disruption that results from increased REE imports. This term is also ignored by individual US
companies and is included in the security premium estimates.
Based on this welfare approach, the security premium for imported REE represents the increased
external costs (not captured by REE markets) of the expected GDP losses and income transfers
to foreign REE producers that result from changes in the consumption of imported REE:

 + ∑𝑛𝑛𝑖𝑖=1 𝜑𝜑𝑖𝑖 𝑄𝑄

where φi is the probability of a supply shock Qi.

Data
The data for this study comes from multiple sources. The primary data is from the United States
Geological Survey (USGS) National Minerals Information Center. The USGS annually releases
reports detailing the mine production and reserves of REEs. These reports express quantities in
metric tons and are denoted as Rare Earth Oxide (REO) equivalents. The same reports also
mention the proven reserves of REEs for the countries. The data is digitized from these reports,
spanning 1995-2021, which provides a country-level annual overview.
The data from the World Integrated Trade Solution (WITS) can be augmented to get the data on
the US GDP in current US$. The data on GDP is needed to calculate the economic loss
associated with REEs. Third, a proxy for the US prices of REEs is needed. This comes from the
BACI CEPII database. The production and consumption are reported as a basket of all the REEs
(and not individual elements). Hence, a proxy is created for the price as a basket. Using the trade

 84

database, an annual REE metals price for the US is calculated by dividing the volume by
quantity. In the trade data, volume is reported in thousands of current US$ and the quantity is
reported in metric tons. A weighted mean (weighted by trade volume of each country) is taken of
these prices to get the final price for the US in each year. The final prices are measured in current
US$/kg.
The final piece of information needed is the size and probability of REE supply disruptions.
Unlike oil imports, supply disruptions of a size that would create national security concerns are
rare in the REE import data. The Chinese export restrictions in 2010 and 2011 represent one
example of such a shock. Instead, the approach of Brown and Huntington (2013) is adopted.
Brown and Huntington use an expert panel to elicit oil shortfalls as well as expert elicitation of
the probability of each shortfall. The oil shortfalls in Brown and Huntington is converted to a
percentage of US imports and those percentages are applied to US REE imports to produce a
series of REE supply shocks that are proportionally similar to the oil import shocks produced by
the expert oil panel. This approach produces a histogram of shortfalls that roughly resembles an
exponential distribution ranging from 0 (the most likely) to 8000 metric tons (the least likely)
which is roughly 50% of US imports.

Results
Table 18 shows each component of the security premium calculation as well as the total security
premium. The changes in expected import costs during unstable market conditions (wealth
transfers) are very small. The security premium is almost entirely composed of the changes in
expected GDP losses. These losses range from $0.94 in 2022 to $5.73 in 2016. These security
premiums range from 1.09%-22.54% of the REE price. By comparison oil security premiums
range from 1.4%-18.2% of the oil price.

 Table 18. REE security premiums for 2010-2022 (in current USD). Unit: $/kg of REE

Southern Appalachia REE Ecosystem Asset Map Project
This map was created by Task members Sims and Welch over the reporting period and
completed in November 2023. It demonstrates the potential to develop a domestic rare earth
element (REE) supply chain from coal and/or coal waste in the Southern Appalachian Basin,
which includes Alabama, Georgia, and Tennessee. REE refers to 17 metallic elements on the
periodic table. Their strategic importance originates from their critical role as an input in several
key industries such as electronics, clean energy, and defense. Despite their name, REEs are more
abundant in the Earth’s crust than copper, zinc, and gold. Instead, the rarity of REE comes from
their dispersion in the earth’s crust (i.e., not found in large, concentrated deposits) and the
complex, costly separation and refining processes required to convert mined rare earth ore into
rare earth metals used in production. The Southern Appalachian Basin is unique in that the
region contains several potential REE feedstocks (e.g., coal mines, coal combustion residual
facilities), several ancillary industries that influence the supply and demand of these feedstocks
(e.g., electric power generation, cement and concrete producers) as well as several downstream
industries that rely on REEs (electric vehicle and battery production). The development and
deployment of new technologies for manufacturing rare earth elements, particularly in the
Southern Appalachian Basin, may help to revitalize distressed coal communities that have seen
declines in coal production, lead to widespread production of existing and new products, and
reduce national reliance on foreign imports of rare earth elements.
The interactive map is publicly available on the UTK Baker School website at
https://baker.utk.edu/energy-and-environment-reports/southern-appalachia-rare-earth-element-
ecosystem/. It provides the following data in a central, visual database to easily convey the
different REE assets and drivers in the region:
 • Coal producing counties: Coal mines are one potential source of REE. The counties in
 orange identify coal producing counties that had positive coal production at least at some
 point in time between 2001 and 2021, and coal production data is from the U.S. Energy
 Information Administration (EIA) and the U.S. Department of Labor, Mine Safety and
 Health Administration (MSHA).
 • CCR facilities: Coal ash, also known as fly ash or coal combustion residuals (CCR), is
 another promising source of REE. CCR is a byproduct of burning coal to generate
 electricity. Following the 2008 ash spill in Kingston, Tennessee, there have been growing
 rules and regulations on coal ash disposal at power plants, including storage in ash ponds,
 landfills or impoundments. The map includes the location and operating status of CCR
 facilities, which are from Earth Justice. Detailed data for specific ash pond units includes
 the plant name, operator, and the estimated volume, or quantity of CCR in cubic yards,
 which is shown separately for individual CCR units and for the plant as a whole. When
 applicable, the volume of water held in the unit is also displayed.
 • REE concentrations: Geologists from the University of Alabama collected REE
 concentration data for select ash pond units, and the plant average concentration (parts
 per million) and standard deviation across samples are shown for 14 different rare earth
 elements or lanthanides for five plants in Alabama and four plants in Georgia. (For plants
 without ash pond sample data, empty cells are shown for these variables.) Similarly, REE
 concentrations for non-ash samples were also collected, and the location of these samples
 are shown by the brown diamonds on the map. Clicking on a specific non-ash sample

 86

 displays the sample concentration (parts per million) for each lanthanide, the total REE
 concentration, the name of the coal seam, and the sample material (e.g., seam floor rock,
 sludge, coal mine drainage, or coal tailings).
 • Electricity generation: Generator-level data from EIA is used to demonstrate the
 location of operating power generators by fuel source. Clicking on a specific generator
 provides additional data, such as the plant name, fuel type, number of operating
 generators as of 2022, and the total nameplate capacity (in MW). Additionally, for coal
 generators, the detailed data include whether or not coal ash (e.g., fly ash, bottom ash, or
 Gypsum) was sold by the plant in 2021. Additional data from EIA is shown on the map,
 including where coal generators in the region have been retired, which is represented by
 the black, outlined squares. Clicking on a retired coal generator point reveals the plant
 name, the number of coal generators that have been retired at the plant since 2002, and
 the associated total nameplate capacity (in MW) of the units.
 • Construction: There has also been an increased emphasis on beneficial reuses of coal
 ash. According to a survey report by the American Coal Ash Association, 60 percent of
 coal ash that was produced in 2021 was recycled. Primarily, coal ash has been used as a
 substitute for cement in manufacturing concrete, but CCRs have also been used to replace
 mined gypsum in the production of wallboard or drywall. Cement and concrete
 producers are shown on the map by type (cement, concrete, or ready mix). This data was
 supplied by Southern Company for Alabama and Georgia, and data for Tennessee is from
 the Southeast Cement Promotion Association.
 • Industrial Sites: The outlined circles in green indicate industrial sites in the three-state
 region, all of which have been certified through the respective states as business-ready
 and are from each state’s economic development agency. These industrial parks represent
 a significant subset of industrial sites available in the states, with the certifications
 implying that the sites have essential infrastructure to support fast-track development of
 business locations. These industrial parks have the potential to support advanced
 separation processes and industrial applications relevant to REEs.
 • Automotive industry: The map also demonstrates the electric vehicle ecosystem for the
 region by showing the location of facilities that manufacture electric vehicles (EV) and
 batteries for electric vehicles. These points (which are distinguished by stars on the map)
 demonstrate the current and potential demand for REEs as inputs for batteries and electric
 vehicles. The location and name of EV manufacturers were provided by the Department
 of Economic and Community Development in each state. Clicking on a specific
 manufacturer will display more detailed product information (e.g., electric trucks and
 sport utility vehicles, electric vehicle batteries, lithium-ion battery recycler, etc.).
 • Disadvantaged communities: The region also has one of the highest concentrations of
 disadvantaged communities in the country. A regional REE supply chain from coal and
 coal wastes could be a source of economic growth for these communities or exacerbate
 the health and economic disparities facing these communities. The darker shaded areas
 across counties identify disadvantaged communities, which are from the U.S. Council
 on Environmental Quality’s Climate and Economic Justice Screening Tool. Using census
 tract level data, which is a smaller unit of geography than counties, and a variety of
 publicly available datasets, a census tract is identified as disadvantaged if it meets

 87

 thresholds in at least one of the tool’s burden categories and is above the threshold for an
 associated socioeconomic burden. Clicking within a census tract displays which burden
 categories (i.e., energy, climate change, legacy pollution, transportation, water and
 wastewater, health, housing, and workforce development) are present for a given census
 tract and the total number of categories that exceeded the threshold criteria.

CM-REE Economic Input-Output Based Supply Chain Impacts

The objective of the input-output-based CM-REE supply chain effort was to devise and
implement a prototype analytical framework to demonstrate how such a model can help us better
understand the impacts of and tradeoffs characterizing the imbalance between imports and
domestic production of CM-REE materials. As the project progressed, it became increasingly
clear that meeting this objective would require focusing on a small subset of all CM-REE and
supply chain linkages. As a result, the focus was narrowed to magnet-related CM-REE.
Task 4 team members (Sims, Murray, Jackson, Welch) worked closely with ORNL team
members Pu and Paranthaman to identify key data needed to model the REE inputs in an electric
motor and generator supply chain. The methodology developed rests on the enhancement of
existing input-output accounts to model critical material and rare earth supply chains. While the
methodology developed by the team could feasibly be applied to any REE-dependent supply
chain, the electric motor and generator industry was selected as a strategically important industry
to demonstrate the supply chain modeling methodology. The overarching objective for the study
of critical material and rare earth element (CM-REE) supply chains is to identify those industries
that either produce CM-REE or depend heavily on the use of CM-REE in their production
processes (those whose output embodies CM-REE). If the most substantial dollar flows (physical
units multiplied by price per unit) of CM-REE into the industrial production system and those
products that embody CM-REE can be identifed, a full representation of the CM-REE supply
chain can be modeled. All of the requested data for the economic supply chain analysis are
denominated in financial units, namely millions of 2012 dollars.

Details of the methodology are described below.

Definitions:
 • Products: Also called “commodities” and sometimes simply “goods,” can refer to raw
 materials, other tangible materials, and services. Commodity output refers to its value of
 domestic production of the commodity or commodities in question. Contributions to
 commodity output can come from more than one industry.
 • Industries: Collections of establishments whose output is dominated by the commodity
 that shares the industry’s name. Industry output refers to the value of the domestic output
 of the industry in question. Industry output includes the value of all commodities it
 produces.
 • Intermediate goods: Commodities sold by industries to other industries for use in the
 purchasing industry’s production processes.

 88

 • Final goods: Commodities sold to final consumers (e.g., consumers, governments,
 exports, etc., sometimes called final demand activities).
 • Final demand: demand for goods by final demand activities.
 • Intermediate demand: demand for intermediate goods.
 • Value added: Compensation, Payments to Governments, Gross Operating Surplus
 reported by industry.
 • Sector: A general term that can refer to commodities, industries, and final demand
 activities, often used in place of or along with a modifier, such as industry sectors,
 commodity sectors, or final demand sectors. Sectors in industrial classification systems
 often refer to higher levels of aggregation, i.e., collections of similar industries.
 • Direct requirements: Input purchases per output dollar (both inputs and outputs can be
 commodity or industry sectors).
 • Indirect requirements: Inputs of a commodity or industry output used directly or by
 upstream producers.
 • Total requirements: Direct plus indirect requirements for industry output or for
 commodity output.
 • Input-output (IO) accounts: For a given reporting year, IO accounts report a
 comprehensive and consistent accounting of which commodities are sold by industries;
 the use of commodities by industries and by final demand commodities; and the
 payments by industries to value added components.
 • NAICS: North American Industrial Classification System

The input-output accounts published by U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis provide the
foundation for identifying key links in supply chains. The accounts data can be manipulated
mathematically to depict the dollar values of inter-industry purchases and sales. Because many
industries produce more than one product, the IO accounts can be further manipulated to
quantify the dollar value of the direct use of each input commodity per dollar of commodity
output, for all commodities. This representation also supports the estimation of total supply chain
production requirements by commodity, i.e., commodities required directly by the producer and
indirectly by upstream suppliers for production, by commodity.
The IO accounts are published at a level of detail that classifies all producers into roughly 400
commodity and industry sectors. Yet this highest level of IO account reporting detail,
unfortunately, does not include one or more sectors that correspond to CMREEs in isolation. As
a result, dollar flows of commodity inputs to support the production of commodity output
include both CMREE and non-CMREE. The challenge is to quantify and extract the value of
CMREE from the reported aggregate commodity flow values for the purpose of adding one or
more explicit CMREE commodity sectors to the accounting framework.

To overcome this challenge, algorithms were developed to emphasize a single CM-REE
industry, sixteen industries with strong supply chain linkages to the CM-REE industry, and
aggregations of the remaining industries, for a total of 35 industries in the resulting IO

 89

Final Report DE-FE0032045

accounting framework. Deriving the parameters for the CMREE industry was necessitated
because the U.S. Benchmark Input-Output do not isolate a CM-REE industry or commodity, but
instead report relevant purchases and sales transactions as part of related industries and their
commodities.

Next, because domestic production of these is negligible, virtually all CM-REE used in the
production of magnets at present is imported. Likewise, in the absence of domestic producers,
and in the absence of superior data from other sources, the CM-REE production recipe was
modeled as a linear combination of three related industries as shown below.

Industry output was then estimated, consisting entirely of CM-REE commodity and no
secondary commodities, as the value of net trade in three commodities from the publicly reported
2021 US Trade Data reproduced below.

Implementing the algorithms generates a set of 35-sector U.S. IO accounts for 2021 16 that report
detail on magnet-related CM-REE, strongly linked supply-chain industries, and the rest of the
economy. The industrial classification scheme is reproduced below.

demonstration), 442,374,283, and 339,315,171.

Demonstration Application
The prototype model is a synthetically parameterized representation of what the U.S. economy
would look like were all CM-REE imports replaced by domestic production from a hypothetical
CM-REE industry. Further, all CM-REE industry output is consumed by U.S. industries. Like
most extractive industries, backward (upstream) linkages to other industries are much weaker
than forward (downstream) industries, and final demand is generally dominated by trade, as the
number of final uses for such raw materials is essentially limited to exports demand, which is
initially set to zero.

With the resulting demand-driven IO accounts, one can implement traditional IO impact analyses
to determine the direct and indirect impacts of final demand changes. 17 One could, for example,
query the model for impacts of increased exports final demand. Because the model is linear and

17 The impacts assessments and linear programming exercise are both based on IO models open with respect to households, so

induced income impacts are excluded. These impacts are often quite substantial and often increase sectoral output multipliers by
50% or more. Limiting total impacts to direct and indirect and excluding income induced impacts provides a clearer
understanding of the CM-REE supply chain relationships without adding in the induced income impacts that flow from
expenditures from labor income. One can refer to the Type I multiplier impacts as production impacts. Note that the linear
programming example is based on a system for which there are no material input substitutes for the CM-REE.

 91

impacts of final demand increases or decreases are symmetrical, one can also query the model on
impacts of reductions in final demand, despite the initial absence of CM-REE final demand.

Of potentially far greater interest, however, are impacts of shortages in the supply of these
critical inputs, as the resulting estimates reflect the vulnerability of the U.S. economy to
shortages due to any number of causes, including international conflict and natural disasters.
Viewed similarly, such an analysis provides a means of quantifying economic vulnerability due
to over-reliance on imports of these critical materials. Hence, to quantify the economic impacts
of CM-REE supply constraints, a linear programming problem was formulated that constrains
CMREE sector output to its initial value, less ten percent, and optimize to determine maximum
system output subject to the supply constraint.

Unadjusted, the linear programming (LP) results capture linked-industry impacts and the impacts
of the ten percent reduction in CM-REE industry production. If the intent is to quantify the
impacts on the U.S. economy from a reduction in foreign supply, the impacts of the CM-REE
output reduction can be netted out from the LP estimates. Bearing in mind that the estimates are
based on numerous assumptions and synthetic production functions not based directly on actual
data, were the foreign supply of magnet-related to be reduced by $32.9M, the resulting loss to
the economy would be as shown below.

 92

Final Report DE-FE0032045

The first column, Implied Final Demand Impacts, reports the reduction in the U.S. system’s
ability to satisfy system final demand due to the imposed supply constraint. Thus, a $33.9M
reduction in supply results in economic choke points and bottlenecks that preempt the production
of $226M in system output and reduce the system’s ability to deliver to final demand by $137M.
Because the sum of final demands is roughly equivalent to GDP, total final demand impacts can
be read as approximate GDP impacts. In addition, there would be a loss of 926 jobs and almost
$50M in wages, which, if modeled, would generate additional impacts roughly half again as
large as the reported production impacts. Because the IO framework can be represented as a

 93

system of linear equations, the values in the table scale linearly. E.g., doubling the magnitude of
the supply constraints would double the reported impacts.

and industry for all but the government services sectors, for which supporting data are
inadequate. These impacts are reported in Table 19.

Next Steps
Unknowns in the prototype model were parameterized using data from existing industries in
related activities along with published secondary data. Input-output literature provides guidance
on the identification of key parameters, i.e. those to which model solutions are most sensitive.
The aggregation bias literature, e.g., tells us that aggregation bias is minimized when maximum
detail is retained for sectors of special analytical interest. It is known that the model outcomes
are most heavily influenced by the parameters with the largest values. These general principles
can be used to develop a strategy for seeking out superior data with which to enhance model
accuracy.
IO accounts are routinely published in commodity-by-industry format, where a Use table reports
the row industry commodities used by column industries and a Make table reports the column
commodities produced by row industries. These two matrices can be combined to build industry-
by-industry accounts (and other formats) by standardization and multiplication. To guide next
steps, consider the following diagrams displaying the accounting framework structure in a
stylized format.

The highest priority for superior data will be the cells in yellow in the Use and Make tables, with
emphasis on the largest values in the yellow CM-REE row and column. The next highest priority
will be the largest elements in the orange rows and columns, which correspond to those
industries with the strongest supply chain linkages. Remaining green cell values are less
important to the analysis of the targeted (CM-REE) sector.
To enhance the target sector data requires a willing and knowledgeable industry expert. Ideally,
the expert will have some background not only in industrial engineering processes but also some

 97

facility with economic modeling, as it was discovered that some terms fundamental to both areas
can have different meanings to experts from different problem domains.

Other Applications and Model Construction
The approach described in this document have targeted magnet-related CM-REE, but the
approach is general enough to be applied to other commodities of special interest, whether there
is an existing industry already present in the economy. When the industry is in development
stages, expert opinion will play a heavy role in parameterizing the model. Where industries are
operating at scale, the model construction procedures are simpler.
There are several sequenced steps in the construction of this kind of model. The list below is a
high-level description.
 1) Use current year BEA annual and quinquennial benchmark (highly detailed) IO accounts
 to add benchmark detail on the most important supply-chain linked industries to the less
 detailed annual tables and aggregate less important sectors.
 2) Extract targeted industry and commodity subsectors from parent sectors.
 3) Develop and devise modeling scenarios.

 Task 5: Technology Assessment

Recovering rare earth elements from coal and coal ash
According to the International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry (IUPAC), REEs are groups
of 17 different elements, including 15 lanthanides from lanthanum (La) to lutetium (Lu) along
with two other elements, that is, scandium (Sc) and yttrium (Y)(Leal Filho et al., 2023). REEs
are not very rare in comparison to precious metals (for example, gold, silver, and platinum), but
due to their uneven distribution around the globe and because they are not abundantly available
at one location in the form of economically viable ore deposits, they are considered critical raw
materials (Scott & Kolker, 2019).
Owing to the unique magnetic, luminescent, and electrochemical properties of REEs, they are
considered modern tools for reducing energy consumption with high efficiency and achieving the
miniaturization of devices with high speed, accuracy, durability, and thermal stability (Balaram,
2019). Thus, their use has skyrocketed in the last decade for applications in modern devices,
computers, technology-based industries, mobile phones, televisions, superconductors, fluorescent
materials, and health industries. The recent push for achieving net zero by reducing greenhouse
gas (GHG) emissions and adopting ‘green’ technologies and renewable energy also led to the
utilization of REEs at a faster pace due to their extensive usage in wind turbine magnets,
rechargeable batteries and cells used in electric vehicles, super magnets and solar panels
(Elshkaki, 2021). Regarding ‘green’ technologies, REEs account for 91% of the total value of the
global REEs market (Liu et al., 2023c).
Conventionally, mining of REEs is carried out at commercial scale from ores such as bastnäsite
or fluorocarbonate (a carbonate-fluoride mineral, (La,Ce)FCO3), monazite (predominantly a
phosphate mineral but a group of arsenates and silicates might be present, (Ce, La,Y,Th)PO4)
and xenotime (YPO4). Scanning electron photomicrographs of REE-bearing minerals in coal,
kaolinite, calcite, gypsum, bastnaesite, and monazite are depicted in Fig. 43 (Huang et al., 2023).

 99

Final Report DE-FE0032045

Fig. 43. Scanning electron photomicrographs of minerals in coal. (a) Kaolinite (Kln), (b) calcite
(Cal), (c) gypsum (Gpy), (d) Bastnaesite, (e,f) Monazite. Adapted from Huang et al. (2023)
under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license
(https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/).

REEs have also been reported in apatite (Ca5(PO4)3(F,Cl,OH)), allanite
((Ce,Ca,Y)2(Al,Fe3+)3(SiO4)3OH), and loparite ((Ce,La,Na,Ca,Sr)(Ti,Nb)O3) eudialyte
(Na4(Ca,Ce)2(Fe2+,Mn,Y)ZrSi8O22(OH,Cl)2 and steenstrupine
(Na14Mn2(Fe,Mn)2(REE)6ZrSi12O36(PO4)7⋅3H2O) are also commonly reported but not currently
exploited (Eterigho-Ikelegbe et al., 2021; Liu et al., 2023c; McNulty et al., 2022). Typically, ores
contain about 1–10% w/w of REEs, but only 50-80% of REEs can be extracted by mining;
however, the concentration and content of REEs are highly dependent upon the type of ores and
have a significant impact on the economic viability of REEs extraction (McNulty et al., 2022).
The REE concentrations in the different earth materials are listed in Table 20.

Another challenge is that REEs are distributed in different ways worldwide; for example, China
is rich in REEs, controlling approximately 70% of global reserves (44 MMT) compared to only
1.5 MMT of its reserves in the USA. Thus, China has a near monopoly over the global supply
chain, refining, and industrial applications, with 85% of global rare earth oxide (REOs)

 100

processing capacity, and is the largest consumer in the world (Fig. 44) (Golroudbary et al., 2022;
Liu et al., 2023c). Owing to this imbalance in the distribution of REEs and the increased supply-
demand gap for the development of modern technologies, which ultimately reduces the
overdependence on some countries, the world is now looking forward to novel and alternative
resources for mining REEs.

Fig. 44. Mining rare earth oxides in different countries (containing Y; 1985–2022). 2022E is the
estimated value. (Adapted with permission from Liu et al. (2023c).
Limited and uneven availability, supply risk, high demand, and diverse industrial applications in
various sectors have made REEs a critical mineral resource globally. One solution is to identify
and develop alternative resources for enrichment, extraction, and recovery from secondary
resources or waste streams. Mining of REEs from waste or secondary resources, such as
electronic waste or e-waste, biomass-derived waste, phosphogypsum, red mud from aluminum
production, and coal ash, might provide a sustainable solution in a circular economy concept
(Agrawal et al., 2023). REE recovery via urban mining has attracted wide attention because
some of these essential waste resources have significantly higher concentrations of REEs than
naturally occurring ores available in certain parts of the world (Deng et al., 2022b; Gaustad et al.,
2021). This provides an alternative route to meet global REEs demand with reduced GHG
emissions, lower environmental impacts, and an economical method (Agrawal et al., 2023).
However, since technology development is in its nascent stages to recover REEs from such
diverse waste resources, it is prudent to screen them in primary stages; therefore, it is essential to
target and explore the potential of a single selected waste resource to understand the current
status of available technologies, its life cycle impacts, techno-economic analysis, and future
applications.
Recently, coal ash has been recognized as a promising alternative resource for REE extraction
mainly due to its i) abundant availability (~750 MMT coal ash is generated each year across the
globe), ii) high concentrations of REEs in coal ash (about 30 times enrichment of REEs in the
coal ash as compared to coal as a resource) (Deng et al., 2023), iii) REEs recovery from coal ash
serves the dual purpose of ash management and economic and environmental benefits by

 101

eliminating the need for energy-intensive processes involved in excavation and processing
during conventional mining, and iv) as the recovery of REEs from coal ash technology could be
potentially integrated with an existing coal-based power plant, providing a uniform quality of
raw material on-site; therefore, many of the supply chain management issues will be resolved. It
has been estimated that REEs worth $4.3 billion can be recovered from coal ash. Therefore,
many countries, including the US, are now focusing on recovering REEs from coal ash (Liu et al.
2023a). The number of publications on the research topic “REEs from coal and coal fly ash
published between 2000 and 2024 (ongoing) is depicted in Fig. 45.

Fig. 45. Number of publications for REEs from coal and coal fly ash, published between 2000 to
2024 (ongoing). (This data has been generated by an AI-powered search engine, i.e.,
www.dimensions.ai)
In a previous study, the viability of REEs enrichment from coal ash was evaluated using physical
separation methods, such as size, magnetic field, and density (Lin et al., 2017). Higher REEs
concentrations were detected in the finer sections of coal ash, and magnetic separation showed
that REEs were enriched in non-magnetic fractions. Although maximum REEs enrichment was
observed with the density separation technique, a combination of these techniques was
recommended for REEs enrichment (Lin et al., 2017). A novel proof-of-concept was recently
established to selectively enrich REEs in coal ash using supercritical nitrogen, supercritical air,
and tributyl phosphate (Zhu et al., 2023a).
The conventional methods for REE recovery suffer from several limitations such as sluggish
reactions with longer processing times, low yields and inferior purity of REEs, poor
extractability, and generation of hazardous and toxic waste stream post-processing (Salehi et al.,
2023). Acid leaching is one of the most explored technologies that has been investigated for the
extraction of REEs from coal ash. However, it was observed that one of the strongest acids, nitric
acid, could only extract 1.6% of REEs from some of the coal ash samples of US power plants
(Taggart et al., 2016). Furthermore, the efficiency of acid leaching depends on the type and
composition of the REEs species. REEs phosphates, zircon, and glass phases have often been
attributed to hard-to-extract REEs species (Deng et al., 2022a). Deng et al. (2022a) recently
developed an ultrafast, energy-efficient, electrothermal process (~3000°C, ~1 s) based on flash

 102

Joule heating (FJH) for the recovery of REEs from coal ash. The FJH method thermally
decomposes the hard-to-extract REEs species with high thermodynamic solubility to improve the
REEs recovery by 2X times using mild acid such as 0.1 M HCl. Therefore, there is an urgent
need to develop novel and efficient methods for extracting REEs from coal ash. Similarly, a
recent study from Rice University, USA, showed that REEs could be easily extracted from coal
ash using short pulses of high electrical heat. This technique was twice as efficient as
conventional methods. Kean (Kean, 2022). Rassy et al. (2023) investigated an eco-friendly
approach to bioaccumulate REEs by more than 50% through microorganisms, and conducted
experiments to determine the localization, binding, incorporation, and occurrence of REEs in
microbial cells.
Literature survey was performed to explore 'coal ash' as a raw waste material or secondary
resource for urban mining of REEs. Many earlier studies have investigated the composition of
REEs in coal ash and their recovery. The survey focuses on recent updates on the utilization of
coal ash for the recovery of REEs, which will improve the current understanding of the form and
structure of REEs present in coal ash, thus providing a guide for designing and developing
alternative separation technologies. Multiple tools to identify and characterize coal ash both
qualitatively and quantitatively from diverse locations, state-of-the-art analytical techniques to
identify and quantify REEs (such as XRF, inductively coupled plasma optical emission
spectrometry (ICP-OES), laser-induced breakdown spectroscopy (LIBS), instrumental neutron
activation analysis (INAA), glow discharge mass spectrometry (GD-MS), and inductively
coupled plasma mass spectrometry ( ICP-MS) were reviewed. Current technologies to isolate
and recover REEs from coal ash are discussed from conventional thermo-chemical methods to
recent studies. Particular emphasis on biological, 'green' and other environmental methods have
been discussed in detail, providing new alternative strategies for the extraction of REEs from
coal ash. This will enable researchers to take future research directions and make investment
decisions depending on the concentration and composition of REEs in coal and coal ash, identify
gaps in knowledge, and highlight recent advances in available and sustainable technologies.
What are REEs
A cluster of 17 chemically similar elements comprising 15 lanthanides (La, Ce, Pr, Nd, Pm, Sm,
Eu, Gd, Tb, Dy, Ho, Er, Tm, Yb, and Lu), scandium (Sc), and yttrium (Y) are known as rare
earth elements or REEs (Balaram, 2023b). The electron configurations of REEs, Y, and Sc are
[Xe]4fn5dm6s2, [Kr]3d14s2, and [Ar]3d14s2, respectively. Because of the analogous valence
electron (the electrons in the outermost shell, or energy level, of an atom) patterns observed in 17
REEs, they show similar physicochemical properties (Table 21) (Haque et al., 2014; Lide, 2005).
Despite the subtle differences in the electron structure patterns (e.g., different numbers of
electrons in the 4f orbitals), REEs differentiate from each other for critical properties, making
them suitable for advanced applications in research and technologies (Table 17) (Fu et al., 2022).
REEs can be further categorized into light REEs (such as La, Ce, Pr, Nd, Pm, Sm, and Y, with
atomic numbers between 57 and 62) and heavy REEs (such as Eu, Gd, Tb, Dy, Ho, Er, Tm, Yb,
and Lu, with numbers 63–71) depending on their atomic number, electron arrangement, and
chemical properties (Malhotra et al., 2020). Light REEs are more abundantly available and less
expensive than the rarely available heavy REEs, which are highly valued in the market (Balaram,
2023b). REEs are usually silvery-white in appearance and exhibit high electrical conductivity
(Malhotra et al., 2020).
