Presence of REEs in coal and coal ash
REEs are accumulated in coal deposits by major processing types, viz. a) tuffaceous (a process of
accumulation of REEs in coal by natural alkaline and acid leachates); b) hydrothermal (due to
the action of hot water and heated fluids); 3) infiltration or meteorite aquifer (due to water-driven
meteoric water; 4) terrigenous (which requires action of surface water) (Eterigho-Ikelegbe et al.,
2021). Terrigenous and tuffaceous types were synthesized during the planet’s peat swamp stage.
In contrast, the infiltration type is typically epigenetic in nature and hydrothermal types are
formed during coal basin development (Meriestica et al., 2021). Moreover, REEs are also
accumulated by natural sedimentation processes due to the physiochemical weathering of rocks
and aggregation of REEs with humins during coalification and diagenesis (Eterigho-Ikelegbe et
al., 2021).
In coal, REEs remain mainly associated with organic components (it refers to the elements
chemically bound to organic content such as carboxylates (carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, oxygen,
and sulfur content) and the inorganic association of elements with the organic content in the
minerals), minerals (or inorganic associations include the elements that are hosted
macroscopically and microscopically visible minerals or non-crystalline mineraloids), and
intimate organic (it involves those elements that are hosted in the micron to nano-sized minerals
and the elements that are adsorbed on the surface of organics or dissolved in pore waters of the
coal (non-mineral elements). REE associations include (a) syngenetic clastic and pyroclastic
minerals (mainly monazite and to a lesser extent xenotime) or as isomorphic admixtures in
minerals of terrigenous and tuffaceous origin (e.g., zircon, apatite, and others); (b) diagenetic and
epigenetic minerals of authigenic origin: rare earth + yttrium-bearing aluminum phosphates and
 sulfates of the alunite supergroup (APS
 minerals); (c) water-bearing REY
 phosphates (rabdophane group and
 cherchite); (d) oxides (rarely); (e)
 carbonates or fluorocarbonates; and (f)
 organic compounds (Thomas et al., 2024).
 The predominant REE containing minerals
 are monazite (a phosphate mineral - a
 complex in [(Ce, La, Nd, Th)PO4]);
 xenotime (a byproduct of monazite);
 zircon (ZrSiO4; a mineral commonly
 found in acidic rocks and abundant in coal
 fly ash); allanite; apatite; alunite
 supergroup minerals; water bearing REY
 phosphates (rhabdophane group and
 churchite); rarely a few oxides, carbonates
 and fluoro-carbonates (Thomas et al.,
 2024). The mineralogical speciation of
 REEs in coal is shown in Figure 46.
Figure 46. Mineralogical speciation of REEs in coal. Adapted from Thomas et al. (2024) and
Dai et al. (2021) under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY)
license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)

 107

Multiple studies have been conducted in different parts of the world to estimate the
concentrations and compositions of REEs in coal and coal ash. For example, the USA Coal
Quality Database showed that the USA coal basins were enriched with coal ash containing more
than 700 ppm REEs (Seredin et al., 2013). More than 300 ppm of REEs were determined in
selected coal basin sites of Illinois, Northern Appalachian, and Central Appalachian in the USA
(Harrar et al., 2022). Similarly, Lin et al. (2017) reported that coal ash samples analyzed at the
National Energy Technology Laboratory, USA, are composed of 312–623 ppm of REEs. The
distribution of REEs in coal fly ash was determined using a Sensitive High-Resolution Ion
Microprobe (SHRIMP) with Reverse Geometry method (Kolker et al., 2017). The results showed
that REEs were partitioned into aluminosilicate glasses formed at boiler temperatures, with Ca,
Fe-enriched aluminosilicate glasses having higher REEs contents than pure Al-Si glasses (Figure
47). REEs were present in the Fe-oxide magnetospheres in fly ash but were depleted in
quartz/SiO2. Therefore, the glass phase should be targeted for recovery of REEs from coal fly
ash (Kolker et al., 2017; Scott & Kolker, 2019).

Figure 47. Electron microprobe elemental maps of coal fly ash samples: iron (Fe), calcium (Ca),
aluminum (Al), and silicon (Si). Al-Si is aluminosilicate cenosphere and Fe-oxide; Ca-rich is Ca-
enriched glass. The field-of-view width was 225 micrometers (μm). False-color maps indicate
relative concentrations (red and yellow indicate higher concentrations, and blue and green indicate
lower concentrations). Black areas represent spaces between fly ash grains. Adapted from Kolker
et al. (2017) and Scott and Kolker (2019) under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons
Attribution (CC BY) license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)

 108

Investigation of the REEs bearing fluoro-oxides and monazite present in the coal ash by SEM-
EDS showed that the occurrence of REEs in the coal ash was intimately connected to that in the
raw coal (Figure 48). (Huang et al., 2023).

Figure 48. Scanning electron photomicrographs and energy-dispersive spectroscopy of REEs
minerals in the coal ash (a) REEs fluoro-oxides attached to the edges of aluminosilicates, with the
accompanying energy spectrum; (b) enlargement of the boxed area in Figure 48a, with the
accompanying aluminosilicate energy spectrum; (c) monazite in fly ash, with the accompanying
energy spectrum. Adapted from Huang et al. (2023) under the terms and conditions of the Creative
Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)

In India, the concentrations of REEs in coal and coal ash samples are 150–200 and 600 ppm,
respectively (Modi & Kumari, 2023). However, few studies have reported very high
concentrations of REEs (i.e., more than 1200 ppm) in coal ash from a thermal power plant in
India (Saikia et al., 2021). Fu et al. (2024) screened 166 REE-bearing minerals containing 149
ppm of REEs in 12, 930 samples of bituminous coal collected from Hubei, China. In the studied
coal samples, fluorocarbons, monazite, and zircon-bearing (Sc, Y) were the predominant REE-
containing minerals with concentrations greater than 10 % (Fu et al., 2024). In another study, a
coal ash sample from China comprised 234 ppm of REEs (Zhu et al., 2023b). Wu et al. (2022)
found that 630 ppm of REEs were present in the coal ash sample recovered from the Qianxi coal-
fired power plant in Guizhou province, China. The REEs concentration in the high-ash coal
samples from the Waterberg Basin coalfields in South Africa was identified as 225 ppm (Harrar
et al., 2022). In coal ash samples recovered from coal-fired power plants in Australia, the REEs
concentration was 448 ppm (Abaka-Wood et al., 2022). The coal ash samples recovered from a
power plant in Poland showed that the REEs concentration varied from 307 to 617 ppm
(Adamczyk et al., 2023). The REEs content in the coal ash sample obtained from Baganuur,
Mangolia, was 414 ppm (Jamiyansuren et al., 2023). A few studies have noted that coal ash
samples contain REEs equivalent to or even higher concentrations than inferior grade REE ores,
with 721 ppm to 4551 ppm levels (Xing et al., 2023). For example, high-alumina fly ash samples
chiefly available in central and western Inner Mongolia and northern Shanxi province, China,
were composed of 652 ppm of REEs (Xing et al., 2023). In Russia, more than 400 ppm of REEs

 109

were found in coal ash samples initially isolated from the Pereyaslavskoye (Siberia) and
Erkovetskoye (Far East) regions.
However, the average REEs concentrations in coal ash and coal are 400 ppm and 70 ppm,
respectively, compared to the 1000-5000 ppm available in conventional mining sites (Abaka-
Wood et al., 2022; Choudhary et al., 2024; Eterigho-Ikelegbe et al., 2021; Harrar et al., 2022).
Thus, considering that 780 million metric tons of coal ash is generated in the world (Tamanna et
al., 2023) (with more than 66% in Asia, followed by Europe and USA) and 400 ppm is the
average REEs concentration in coal ash, it could produce approximately 312,000 Tons of REEs
every year, which is substantially higher than the annual demand for REEs in the world. Thus,
REEs recovery from coal ash alone has a high potential to meet the annual needs of REEs
worldwide. However, for the efficient extraction of REEs from coal ash, the optimum
concentration of REEs should be in the range of 800–900 ppm. (2017).

Analytical techniques to identify REE
With the ever-expanding demand for REEs worldwide, there is a sudden spurt in the exploration,
mining, recovery, and analysis of REEs from novel sites and raw materials for enhanced
economic viability and sustainability. Although REEs exhibit similar chemical properties, they
differ in their minor physical characteristics that impart distinct electromagnetic properties.
Owing to the similar properties of REEs, their identification using conventional analytical
procedures such as gravimetric, titration, and spectrophotometric techniques is challenging. It
becomes more complicated during the identification of specific REEs in mixtures of other
similar REEs due to multiple interferences and coincidences and at crustal concentrations (i.e.,
between 0.3 − 33 µg/g in crustal rocks) in various geological materials (Balaram, 2023a).
Rapid, precise, energy-efficient, and cost-effective analytical techniques are essential for further
progress in this field. Therefore, sophisticated analytical techniques are currently being deployed
to detect, identify, and estimate REEs during exploration, geochemical mapping, mining, ore
processing, and metallurgical operations. A few examples of such advanced analytical
techniques are INAA, inductively coupled plasma–mass spectrometry (ICP-MS), inductively
coupled plasma-tandem mass spectrometry (tandem-ICP-MS), inductively coupled plasma-time-
of-flight- mass spectrometry (ICP-TOF-MS), inductively coupled plasma-high-resolution-mass
spectrometry (HR-ICP-MS), inductively coupled plasma-multi-collector-mass spectrometry
(MC-ICP-MS), and Mattauch–Herzog geometry- inductively coupled plasma-mass spectrometry
(MH-ICP-MS). These techniques offer multi-element capability, high sensitivity, wide linear
dynamic range, low interference, and ease of operation (Balaram, 2023a). A schematic diagram
of the different techniques used for elemental, isotopic, and mineralogical studies of REEs is
shown in Figure 49.

 110

Figure 49. Schematic diagram of different techniques used for elemental, isotopic, and
mineralogical studies of REEs. (Modified after adaptation from “Advances in Analytical
Techniques and Applications in Exploration, Mining, Extraction, and Metallurgical Studies of
Rare Earth Elements” authored by Balaram (2023a) under the terms and conditions of the Creative
Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)).

Bulk analysis
Bulk analysis is a nondestructive method used to determine the elements and molecules present
on the surface of objects. The techniques used for this analysis included XRF, atomic absorption
spectrometry, ICP-OES, ICP-MS, microwave plasma atomic emission spectroscopy (MP-AES),
LIBS, INAA, and laser ablation inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry (LA-ICP-MS).
Fayyaz et al. (2023) developed a quick method to detect REEs in phosphorite deposits through
LIBS. In a study by Balassone et al. (2021), bulk chemical analyses were conducted by ICP-MS
to detect the REEs abundances in the analyzed bulk samples.

Isotope analysis
Isotopes are atoms with the same number of protons but different numbers of neutrons. Isotopes
have similar chemical properties but vary in their physical properties owing to differences in
their masses. Some are stable isotopes, which do not emit radiation, while others are unstable
isotopes or radioisotopes that emit radiation. Isotopic analysis involves the identification of
different isotopes of elements, such as isotopic signatures or fingerprints (set of ratios between
the amount of various isotopes of an element in a sample) and the abundance of certain stable
isotopes of chemical elements within organic and inorganic compounds. The different techniques
used for isotope analysis include laser ablation multi-collector inductively coupled plasma mass
spectrometry (LA-MC-ICP-MS), secondary-ion mass spectrometry (SIMS), SHRIMP, MC-ICP-
MS, and thermal ionization mass spectrometry (TIMS).

 111

Mineral Identification
Mineral identification analysis is based on various physical properties, such as color, density,
streak, lustre, hardness, crystal shape, cleavage, specific gravity, and behavior. Some of the
techniques used for mineral identification are short-wave infrared (SWIR) Spectroscopy, Raman
spectroscopy, Energy Dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS), XRD, and scanning electron
microscopy (SEM). For example, qualitative and quantitative XRD analyses of 10 bulk samples
(10 kg each) of carbonates originally collected from the waste mounds of the Assemini (Cagliari)
site in Italy revealed that it consists of different wt. % mixtures of minerals such as calcite,
dolomite, quartz, fluorite, muscovite, barite, K-feldspar, kaolinite, gypsum, and illite (Balassone
et al., 2021). Furthermore, an SEM-EDS study showed that calcite and dolomite were
predominantly present in these samples, followed by quartz and fluorite (Balassone et al., 2021).
In another study, XRD, SEM-EDS, and X-ray absorption near edge structure (XANES)
spectroscopy were used to determine the mineralogy, structure, and oxidation state alterations of
the representative REE-bearing phases during coal burning and to identify the REEs speciation
in calcite (Liu et al., 2020). XRD and SEM-EDS analyses were used to identify and characterize
neodymium recovered from mixed rare-earth aqueous solutions using organophosphorus
derivatives (Ruiz et al., 2020).
Liu et al. (2019) used both bulk and microscale analysis approaches to investigate REEs
speciation. The primary techniques used for this study were scanning and transmission electron
microscopy coupled with energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (S/TEM-EDS), electron probe
microanalysis (EPMA), laser ablation inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry (ICP-MS),
and synchrotron X-ray microscopy and spectroscopy. Although SEM-EDS was able to identify
most REE-bearing phases with an REE content of >0.1 wt %, but apatite and REE-bearing glass
is only identified by μXRF/μXANES, likely due to the difference in detection limits of these
techniques. However, REE-bearing lime and zircon were not identified by μXRF/μXANES (Liu
et al., 2019). A comparison of the advantages and disadvantages of different methods for the
identification of REEs speciation showed that SEM-EDS was helpful in the identification of
REE-bearing phases and in determining particle morphology and distribution. However, it lacks
the detection of REE-bearing phases and quantification of REE species. Furthermore, μXRF and
μXANES were useful for estimating the distribution of REEs at trace levels of oxidation states
and for identifying REE-bearing phases. Nevertheless, it provides no information on REE
extractability, REE species, REE distribution, and quantification (Liu et al., 2019). The detection
limits and capabilities of some of the commonly utilized analytical techniques for REEs
investigation are listed in Table 22.

Enrichment, extraction, and recovery of REEs
Conventional approaches
Many different approaches have been applied to extract and recover REEs from coal ash,
including precipitation, adsorption, and solvent extraction. Conventionally, hydrometallurgical
extraction processes have been used to extract and recover REEs from coal ash through chemical
leaching and solvent extraction. Hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, and nitric acid are commonly
used to solubilize REEs, followed by solvent extraction to separate them from one another
(Brown et al., 2023). A recent study observed that alkali treatment with NaOH and KOH
enhanced the REEs recovery by 1.61 and 1.32 fold, respectively. In contrast, acidic treatment
with HCl and oxalic acid improved it by 1.27 and 1.09 times, respectively (Choudhary et al.,
2024). However, the sequential extraction procedure, that is, alkali (NaOH) treatment followed
by acid (HCl) treatment, was found to be the most efficient in the extraction of REE from coal
ash (Choudhary et al., 2024). Conventional approaches often involve hazardous chemicals, high
temperatures, and high pressures, thus leading to a high environmental and carbon footprint. In
addition, poor selectivity, low purity, high processing costs, high chemical consumption, and the
generation of large volumes of toxic waste pose a threat to the environment. Therefore, there is a
growing interest in developing novel alternative environmentally friendly approaches for the
enrichment, extraction, and recovery of REEs from coal ash, as discussed in the following
sections, with the help of a few recent examples. A few recent studies utilized microbial
bioleaching to recover REEs from coal fly ash are presented in Table 23.

Physical techniques
Lin et al. (2017) examined different physical separation techniques based on particle size,
magnetic properties and density for the enrichment of REEs from the coal, coal ash, and coal
byproducts. The results showed that for all coal ash samples, higher REEs concentrations were
found in the finer fractions; however, for clay and shale samples, the REEs concentrations
decreased as the particle size decreased, possibly because the REEs minerals were not effectively
released by grinding. Magnetic separation showed that REEs were enriched in non-magnetic
fractions for all ash samples. The density separation method was found to be more efficient for
REEs as the densities of the REEs minerals were higher. Thus, extensive grinding of coal and its
byproduct samples and a combination of different physical separation techniques might play a
critical role in enriching REEs.

Green solvents
‘Green’ solvents such as citric acid, metatartaric acid, and ethylenediamine succinic acid were
investigated for the recovery of REEs by optimizing the residence time, concentration, pH, and
temperature (Kołodyńska et al., 2023). The mixture of citric acid, metatartaric acid, and H2O2 at
concentrations of 0.6 M, 0.3 M, and 2%, respectively, led to higher binding with REEs and their
extraction (Kołodyńska et al., 2023). Liu et al. (2023b) demonstrated a green approach to recover
60% REEs from the coal ash. Later, the addition of oxalate selectively precipitated concentrated
REEs from the leachate via the formation of weddellite (CaC2O4·2H2O), while other trace
metals remained in the solution. Huang et al. (2017) developed a novel green approach to
sequentially extract REEs based on a cation-exchange mechanism using acidic phosphorus
extractants (i.e., di(2-ethylhexyl) phosphoric acid and 2-ethylhexyl phosphonic acid mono-2-
ethylhexyl ester. This technique eliminates ammonia emissions and improves the recovery yields
of REEs. A detailed study by Ruiz et al. (2020) evaluated the performance of three commercial
organophosphorous-based solvents (D2EHPA > PC 88A > Cyanex 272) and optimized the
process conditions such as time, pH, concentration, temperature, and diluents to selectively
extract REEs such as neodymium, terbium, and dysprosium.

 119

Ionic liquids (IL) have been investigated as green solvents for the recovery of REEs from coal
ash owing to their low vapor pressure, low flammability, ability to dissolve both organic and
inorganic compounds, high thermal stability, high conductivity, and wide electrochemical
window (Do-Thanh et al., 2023). In this extraction method, the aqueous solution containing the
REEs comes in contact with an IL mixed with a suitable extraction solvent, which allows the
chelation of REEs in the ionic liquid phase. REEs can be extracted from the IL phase after phase
separation by utilizing high-affinity solutions of REEs, such as acidic aqueous solutions
containing nitric acid or hydrochloric acid. The remaining IL phase can be reused for additional
extraction (Do-Thanh et al., 2023).

Electrochemical pathways
Kean (2022) reported that they had developed an environmentally benign technique to extract
REEs from the coal ash with a two-fold yield increase compared to the current approaches. In
this approach, coal ash was mixed with carbon black to improve the electrical conductivity and
placed in clear quartz tubes with capacitors attached to both ends of the tubes, and a pulse of
current was passed through it. Within 1 s, the temperature of the mixed powder spiked to 3000°C
and then rapidly cooled. This thermal spike was sufficient to shatter the microscopic bits of glass
prepared inside the ash during coal burning and release the REEs for extraction. In addition,
phosphates of REEs are transformed into REE oxides that are more soluble and easily
extractable. As a result, REEs were extracted with concentrations of hydrochloric acid 120 times
lower than the current extraction methods and still managed to extract twice as many REEs
(Kean, 2022). An ultrafast electrothermal process (~3000°C, ~1 s) was developed to improve the
REEs extraction two-fold through FJH, thereby improving the solubility of REEs in the leaching
solvent. This technology is energy-efficient with a low electrical energy consumption of 600
kWh ton−1 (Deng et al., 2022a).

Membrane separation approaches
Recently, membrane separation technology has attracted significant interest for the extraction of
REEs because of its environmentally friendly nature, broad applicability, high selectivity, high
recovery, simple operation, and generation of low waste. Membrane separation is an alternative
approach to avoid thermal processing and to develop a combined process for the simultaneous
removal and extraction of REEs (Bashiri et al., 2022). REEs are recovered through the
membrane primarily by three different mechanisms: a) complexation of REEs with the extractant
embedded in the membrane matrix, b) adsorption of REEs on the surface-created-active sites on
the membrane, and c) rejection of REEs with organic materials from the membrane(Bashiri et
al., 2022).

Nature-based biological processes-biomining
As previously mentioned, traditional hydrometallurgical methods of REE production typically
employ harsh acid leaching and solvent extraction processes. These methods are not
environmentally sustainable and generate large amounts of hazardous waste, leading to adverse
effects on soil and water ecosystems. As an alternative, biological methods can reduce harmful
environmental impacts and hazardous waste, providing viable options for efficient, inexpensive

 120

Final Report DE-FE0032045

approaches for extraction and recycling of REEs. There has been a growing interest in the
development of nature-based biological processes to replace conventional REEs extraction and
purification methods. Over the past decade, a notable surge in research has been focusing on
biological methods, particularly for the extraction of precious metals such as copper and gold.
Biological extraction methods are currently utilized to extract 5% of the world’s gold and ~15%
of the world’s copper supply (Schmitz et al., 2021). Many of these techniques have been studied
extensively using microorganisms to extract high-value metals in the mining and wastewater
treatment industries. Microorganisms can adapt to extreme environments and are
environmentally friendly and cost-efficient, making them a good choice for the recovery of REE.
The recent biomining REEs from coal ash are summarized in Table 24.

 121

Bioleaching
In the bioleaching process, REEs are extracted by utilizing the enzymes and/or metabolic
products of microorganisms. It has the potential to efficiently extract REEs from coal ash, waste,
and low-grade ores in an environmentally friendly manner, at lower costs. The significant
challenges in bioleaching are sluggish reaction processes and maintaining optimal microbial
growth conditions to provide consistent output and yield (Brown et al., 2023).

Biosorption
The binding of REEs in the cellular structure of biomass and other bio-based macromolecules,
such as bacteria, algae, fungi, and lignocellulosic biomass, is known as biosorption. REEs must
be extracted from biosorbents through solvent extraction for REEs recovery. It offers several
advantages such as inexpensive processing, high efficiency, low waste generation, and recycling
of the biosorbent. In addition to REEs extraction, loaded biosorbents can be utilized for
combined heat and power generation, leading to enhanced sustainability (Brown et al., 2023). In
a study, Escherichia coli/Caulobacter crescentus was genetically engineered for high-density cell
surface displays of lanthanide-binding tags, peptides that exhibit high affinity and selectivity for
REEs. The bioengineered microbes exhibited enhanced adsorption capacity and selectivity for
REEs, and the REEs can be desorbed for recovery (Park et al., 2017). In a promising study, a
protein (lanmodulin) was immobilized onto a porous agarose microbead support material using
thiol-maleimide chemistry (Figure 50) to selectively extract REEs in an aqueous separation
scheme without any organic solvent (Dong et al., 2021). The protein helped bind the REEs at
low pH with high stability over numerous low-pH adsorption/desorption cycles to achieve high-
purity separation of Nd/Dy with 88 mol % purity in a single column run while using ∼90% of
the column capacity (Dong et al., 2021). In a similar study, up to 99% purity REEs such as Eu,
Lu, Dy, and La were extracted by utilizing structurally engineered proteins (Cui et al., 2023).

Bioprecipitation
Bioprecipitation is an environmentally friendly method that uses microbially produced
metabolites to react and precipitate REEs (Brown et al., 2023). In this approach, the REEs
present in soluble form are transformed into their respective insoluble precipitates (such as REEs
sulfides, carbonates, phosphates, or hydroxides) by the action of microbes, thereby facilitating
the recovery and extraction of REEs. For example, phosphatase enzymes are produced by
bacteria (Serratia sp.), which facilitates the release of inorganic phosphates by the mineralization
of organic phosphates. Inorganic phosphate binds to REEs and causes their precipitation(Lhamo
and Mahanty 2022).

 122

Figure 50. Process for immobilization of lanmodulin protein onto agarose microbeads through
thiol-maleimide click chemistry and its application for REEs extraction. (Adapted from “Bridging
Hydrometallurgy and Biochemistry: A Protein-Based Process for Recovery and Separation of Rare
Earth Elements” authored by Dong et al. (2021) under the terms and conditions of the Creative
Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)

Agromining or Phytomining
Agromining and phytomining technologies use hyperaccumulator plants and mushrooms to
accumulate REEs in their tissues. The hyperaccumulator plants were grown, harvested, dried,
and recovered after ashing and purification. Agromining or phytomining is a low-cost and
environmentally friendly approach that can generate high-quality bio-ore but requires large land
areas to grow plants and constant care for farming (Brown et al., 2023). In plants, metal
hyperaccumulation occurs with the help of the roots through transport proteins. Finally, metals
bind to organic biomolecules, leading to high levels of metals in old tissues, trichomes with hair-
like structures on plant surfaces, and cell organelles (Jones & Bismarck, 2024). The phytomining
process is illustrated in Figure 51.

 123

Final Report DE-FE0032045

Figure 51. Metal hyperaccumulation in plants. (Adapted from “Mycomining: perspective on
fungi as scavengers of scattered metal, mineral, and rare earth element resources” authored by
Jones and Bismarck (2024) under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution
(CC BY) license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)

Recent reviews and publications seem to suggest that compared to single strain microorganisms,
cooperation among different microorganisms often demonstrates higher REE bioleaching
efficiency. This effect is undoubtedly due, in part, to the inclusion of multi-extraction
mechanisms being involved in the solubilization and extraction of REEs. Furthermore, the
microbial genetic regulation mechanisms occurring with REE bioleaching needs to be further
understood. Advanced biotechnologies, such as genetic engineering and transcriptomic
sequencing need to be employed to advance REE extraction and shorten biomining processing
parameters.

 124

Although the techno-economic analysis of biomining REEs is very limited, a report by
Thompson et al. claimed that bioleaching REE’s from fluidized catalytic cracking using the
heterotrophic bacterium Gluconobacter oxydans could be marginally profitable based on techno-
economic analysis (Thompson et al., 2018).

Conclusions
Recent advances in the urban mining of REEs from coal and coal ash, such as the availability,
concentration, and distribution of REEs in coal and coal ash samples of different origins were
surveyed and discussed. Multiple tools are needed to identify and characterize coal ash both
qualitatively and quantitatively with state-of-the-art analytical techniques to identify and quantify
REEs. Current technologies to isolate and recover REEs from coal ash through conventional
thermo-chemical methods with particular emphasis on biological, 'green' and nature-based
solutions for their extraction through new alternative strategies such as acid leaching,
hydrometallurgy, bioleaching, biosorption, green solvents phytoextraction have advanced
substantially over the past decade. It was observed that coal and coal-derived byproducts have a
significant potential to meet the demand for REEs. However, the major challenge is that many
approaches available for extracting and recovering REEs are still at the laboratory scale. A
significant thrust is required for research, development, and deployment to establish novel eco-
friendly pathways for industrial-scale applications to improve economic and environmental
sustainability.

 125

Final Report DE-FE0032045

High Performance Computing (HPC) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) Driven Solutions
Accelerating the development of meaningful solutions for REE separations requires
multidisciplinary synergy including computer science, engineering, nuclear and radiochemistry,
economics, and public policy. Developing strategies to reduce dependence on foreign sources of
REEs requires developing efficient greener separation processes with selective binding agents.

Goal: to design green separation environments which include selective ligands (see Figure 52)
 1. To target binding of an REE over another
 2. While utilizing solvents less toxic than used currently

Advanced computational modeling can greatly aid optimization of REE separations:
 1. By allowing screening option at large scale while eliminating experimental trial-and-error,
 laboratory costs, and waste management
 2. Through trial-and-error reduction in laboratory settings by evaluating multiple options and stages
 with computers instead of experimentation, particularly useful when managing radioactive
 elements

Conclusions: HPC- and AI-driven solutions to accelerate optimization of REE separations to
design: 1) robust selective ligands and 2) green processes that eliminate the use of toxic solvents

Figure 52. Schematic example showing the organic and aqueous phases in a liquid separation,
with Ln representing the REE, and HR the ligand.

During the first three quarters of 2023, the UTK team (led by Deborah Penchoff) added 2,610
reactions to the data set to train AI algorithms. From these, 2,555 correspond to reactions with

 126

REEs, and 55 to reactions with thorium. Although thorium is not an REE, it is commonly found
in REE deposits, and it therefore needs to be separated from the REEs. The total of reactions
added per REE contain 157 ligands. Each of the 2,610 reactions contain chemical binding
information, and reaction specifics relevant to temperature, ionic strength, and other
characteristics. Data curation for the data set follows reliable building principles to guarantee
systematic and comparable reaction mechanisms.
During the third quarter of 2023, ligands from the data set are being parameterized to train the AI
algorithms. Molecular modeling with electronic structure theory is being utilized to obtain
optimized molecular geometries for each of the 157 ligands.
The AI algorithms are being trained based on the REACKT framework. REACKT is an AI
platform designed to accurately predict reaction characteristics (such as logK values) involving
REEs and actinides (https://icl.utk.edu/reackt/). REACKT stands for Rare Earth Actinides
Constants & logK Thermodynamics. It provides reliable estimations that are vital for a range of
scientific and industrial applications including REE-binding reactions. Its innovative architecture
not only ensures precise predictions but also contributes to a deeper understanding of the
complex interactions between these elements and extracting agents – contributing to fundamental
understanding of REEs and actinides’ selective binding.

Task members Pu, Penchoff, and Johs visited Southern Company’s ABUC at Plant Bowen in
Georgia. Team member Riley Flowers hosted the group and led a 2-hour tour of the
demonstration facility as well as the full-scale facility that is currently under construction.
Representatives from the company charged with managing the ABUC were on hand to answer
questions about the technical and market aspects of the new ash beneficial use facility. Initial
meetings were held between April and June 2023 between the REE separations team at ORNL
and Eco Material Technologies to discuss the possibility of sharing coal ash samples for
assessing separations technologies.
Team members visited University of Kentucky’s Center for Applied Energy Research in
September 2023 and discussed potential collaboration on REE separation and sharing coal ash
samples for assessing separations technologies. Team members attended the DOE/NETL
Resource Sustainability Project Review Meeting held in Pittsburgh, PA in April 2024.

 Task 6: Technology Innovation Center

Technology Innovation Center plan
The team developed an initial draft of the Technology Innovation Center (TIC) Plan. The
primary purpose of the TIC will be:

 1. To accelerate research that will enable commercial deployment of advanced
 processing and production of REE-CM and high-value, nonfuel, CBPs.

 2. To support engagement of public-private partnerships and basinal industries to
 advance new and innovative technology development as well as the potential for
 new product production.

 3. To advance opportunities for the education and training of the next generation of
 technicians, skilled workers and STEM professionals.

Key aspects of TIC identified:

 1. Designed around the SoApp Basin’s unique regional resources, capabilities, and market
 opportunities:

 ● The basin’s coal production is modest but there is a major regional
 concentration of ponded ash (> 200 million tons) with high REE
 concentrations (typically > 400 ppm).

 ● The southeastern US is a major automotive manufacturing region and is
 becoming a hub for EV and EV battery manufacturing (Figure 53 and
 Table 25).

 ● This regional concentration of EV assembly, EV battery production, and
 battery materials manufacturing prompts the Southern Appalachian
 CORE-CM team to focus on products that will be valued in EV
 manufacturing and supply chains such as REE, carbon fibers, graphite,
 and graphene.

 2. Focus on mid- and large-scale prototyping capabilities from Technology Readiness
 Levels 4-7 to successfully cross the technology “valley of death”.

 Task 7: Stakeholder Outreach and Education

Stakeholder Outreach
Task 7, Stakeholder Outreach and Education, was designed to establish a comprehensive
framework for community and stakeholder engagement in support of the broader initiative to
develop valuable coal-derived products in Southern Appalachia. As part of this effort, the Task 7
team focused on the identification and compilation of a robust stakeholder contact list to inform
and guide outreach activities in future project Phases.
For Outreach, stakeholders were categorized into four primary groups (Figure 54): (1) Federal,
State, and Local Government; (2) Business and Industry Partners; (3) Economic Development
and Non-Profit Organizations; and (4) Environmental Justice Groups. This categorization
facilitates targeted communication strategies and ensures that outreach efforts are inclusive of the
diverse interests and priorities across the region.

 Figure 54. Primary stakeholder groups
To assess the impacts of coal mining and coal waste streams in underserved communities in
Alabama and Tennessee, the Task 7 team worked closely with Dr. Nikki Luke (UTK). Dr. Luke
has extensive experience working on environmental and energy justice issues in the U.S. South
and was instrumental in in developing the stakeholder list for social justice organizations in
Tennessee and Alabama. In Phase 2, these organizations would help identify members of
underserved communities in and around current and legacy coal mining communities and
communities impacted by coal waste streams
In addition to stakeholder mapping, the Task 7 team developed a series of strategic messaging
frameworks, including a series of three articles by scientific and technical experts on the project
team. The intent of the messaging and articles is to help the project team and the Department of
Energy explain the benefits of manufacturing valuable coal-derived products in Southern
Appalachia as a stable domestic supply of rare earth elements and critical materials for which the
United States currently relies upon foreign entities. These summaries and educational materials
are designed to support community information sessions. These materials are structured to
provide accessible, accurate information and will be utilized in Phase 2 of the project to support
public engagement activities led by IACMI. This work lays the groundwork for transparent,
informed dialogue with regional stakeholders and enhances the project's capacity to foster broad-
based community support.

that can be used to engage the community:

Stakeholder Outreach
Project partner Roane State Community College—an accredited public institution located in
eastern Tennessee with its main campus in Harriman—played a key role in identifying Wallace
State Community College in Hanceville, Alabama, as a strategic community partner. Wallace
State will support the local implementation of training efforts within Alabama’s coal
communities. IACMI’s workforce development team, which leverages an extensive network of
subject matter experts, institutional partners, and training resources, plans to collaborate with
Wallace State to expand a hub-and-spoke training model. This approach is designed to scale
workforce development efforts and strengthen the regional talent pipeline aligned with coal-to-
products innovation.
In Phase 2, throughout the project, IACMI would lead stakeholder outreach efforts in the basinal
areas with local communities, state & local governments, business & industry and economic
development groups and non-profit organizations.
IACMI will communicate and engage in-person and electronically through:
 • Planned conversations and meetings
 • Public events and workshops
 • Various forms of media including website updates, press releases, e-newsletters, and
 social media posts

Cliff Eberle attended the CORE-CM workshop in Denver (6/27-28/23). Team members Luke
and Harmon attended NETL EJ/workforce Working Group meetings in April, June, July, and
August.

 142

 Conclusion
 Coal processing waste has the highest average Li concentration of the 15 different types of
material collected for this project. Lignite samples have the highest average REE and
REE+Y+Sc (REE+) contents, but the average is skewed by one sample with very high
concentrations. Clay (kaolin and ball clay) average REE contents are comparable to those of
phosphate ore. Coal underclay, coal processing waste, and ponded coal ash contain economic
amounts of lithium. REEs are primarily associated with phosphate minerals in coal-associated
sediments and coal ash. REEs are associated with phosphate and alumina in organic shale
samples. REE are associated with phosphate and likely with ion exchange sites in kaolin and ball
clay samples.
 Coal ash has high average REE, REE+, and Li contents. Total REE+ concentrations of CCP
materials are >300 ppm for all but one ash impoundment. There are relatively high
concentrations of Nd and Y in coal ash, which are considered CREEs. REE and other CM are
associated with trace minerals not identifiable by XRD, so other techniques must be used to
identify CM-mineral associations. Micro-XRF mapping suggests REE in coal ash are primarily
associated with phosphate minerals and organic matter, contrary to previously reported REE-
mineral associations in coal ash. 3D EBK modeling promises to be useful for targeting high REE
and Li zones within undisturbed CCP impoundments. Although the vast majority of the samples
collected for this project classify as Promising REE feedstock materials using the classification
method proposed by Seredin (2010) and refined by Seredin and Dai (2012), development of
efficient REE extraction technologies will be required for economic recovery of these critical
elements.
 The large volume of ash and other coal combustion products stored and ponded in AL-
GA-TN represents a significant untapped strategic mineral resource. Most fly ash currently
produced is sold into beneficial use applications. Primary current fly ash markets include cement
production, concrete production (block, precast, and ready-mix), mine reclamation, and
structural and roadway fills. Several long-term market opportunities have been identified for
evaluation within this project including rare earth elements, asphalt, and polymer fibers. While
technological readiness has been demonstrated for many of these applications, economic
feasibility remains a critical hurdle to widespread deployment in the U.S. Promising strategies to
improve the economic feasibility of these developing applications include minimizing impacts to
other existing uses (e.g., cement and concrete), developing valuable co-products, and developing
clean production processes that limit the costly handling of any waste by-products. The large
quantities of ash combined with a healthy cement and concrete industry makes the Southern
Appalachian Basin an ideal test case for further developing these applications.
 The results produced from a novel asset valuation tool developed by the project find that all
eight ash impoundments evaluated are predicted to produce less than 1,000 tons of total REO in
the first year with production falling each year as the available REO quantity falls. All economic
REO reserves are exhausted in 4-18 years. The total value of these ash impoundments in terms of
only REO production ranges from $5-93 million depending on the composition and
concentration of REO in the ash and the total quantity of ash.
 If REO can be separated the ash and the residual from this separation process can continue to
be sold in existing ash markets, the economic viability of REO extraction from coal ash
improves. Traditional chemical (leaching) separation methods will render any residual unsuitable
for the construction coal ash market. Biological and magnetic separation techniques will allow

 143

producers to leverage existing construction coal ash markets and improve the economic viability
of REO extraction from coal ash.
 Building the business case to support beneficial use of resources extracted from ash ponds
requires ensuring that essential infrastructure is in place to support business operations, including
transportation networks, electricity (and natural gas) and broadband. The use of fly ash in the
production of concrete, cement blocks, bricks and related products should be viewed as a success
story that demonstrates broadly the efficacy of underlying forces supporting market-based use of
coal-based waste, including infrastructure. Especially important has been the proximate location
of large volumes of coal ash to cement and concrete producers. The exploitation of REEs from
coal waste has not seen the same success. While global markets are mature in the sense that REE
suppliers have been able to connect to producers, domestic markets simply do not have robust
supply chains from source materials. This is in part an artifact of dominance by international
suppliers of REEs, especially China, which has hampered the cultivation of domestic supplies.
Incomplete characterization of ash storage facilities and unproven separation technologies are
perhaps the most important challenges to overcome in the near term.
 Building the policy case will require quantifying the security cost of US reliance on foreign
sources of REE – notably China. REE security cost estimates may be used to motivate policy and
research and development (R&D) action, design policy to address REE dependence, or evaluate
existing policies and R&D programs. To match the appropriate REE security cost measure to the
appropriate policy context, the project team develops a two-stage taxonomy that address the
scope and application of security cost measures.
 The project team also estimated one particular type of REE security cost. REE supply
disruptions lead to U.S. economic losses. Increased domestic consumption increases the
economy’s vulnerability to these shocks. US firms recognize how increased consumption of REE
imports increases their exposure to REE supply shocks and impacts their profits. But they do not
recognize how their increased consumption of REE imports impacts other firms. Using a
combination of USGS mining data and international commodity trading databases, we find that
these security costs of REE imports range from $0.94 in 2022 to $5.73 in 2016. These security
premiums range from 1.09%-22.54% of the REE price. By comparison oil security premiums
range from 1.4%-18.2% of the oil price.
 The team also developed and applied an initial model of the domestic REE supply chain
which can be used to show the economic impacts of building the economic and policy case for
domestic REE extraction from coal wastes. The prototype model is a synthetically parameterized
representation of what the U.S. economy would look like were all CM-REE imports replaced by
domestic production from a hypothetical CM-REE industry. The model is used to illustrate the
impact were the foreign supply of magnet-related to be reduced by $32.9M. The $32.9M
reduction in supply results in economic choke points and bottlenecks that preempt the production
of $226M in system output and reduce the system’s ability to deliver to final demand by $137M.
In addition, there would be a loss of 926 jobs and almost $50M in wages, which, if modeled,
would generate additional impacts roughly half again as large as the reported production impacts.
 Recent advances in the urban mining of REEs from coal and coal ash, such as the
availability, concentration, and distribution of REEs in coal and coal ash samples of different
origins were surveyed and discussed. Multiple tools are needed to identify and characterize coal
ash both qualitatively and quantitatively with state-of-the-art analytical techniques to identify

 144

and quantify REEs. Current technologies to isolate and recover REEs from coal ash through
conventional thermo-chemical methods with particular emphasis on biological, 'green' and
nature-based solutions for their extraction through new alternative strategies such as acid
leaching, hydrometallurgy, bioleaching, biosorption, green solvents phytoextraction have
advanced substantially over the past decade. It was observed that coal and coal-derived
byproducts have a significant potential to meet the demand for REEs. However, the major
challenge is that many approaches available for extracting and recovering REEs are still at the
laboratory scale. A significant thrust is required for research, development, and deployment to
establish novel eco-friendly pathways for industrial-scale applications to improve economic and
environmental sustainability.

 APPENDIX A: Summary of Community Considerations

 Community and Worker Considerations and Carbon Ore,
 Rare Earth, and Critical Minerals Extraction from Coal Ash
 in the Southern Appalachian Basin

 Nikki Luke and Caleb Gore

In 2021, the U.S. Department of Energy funded a consortium led by the Institute for Advanced
Composites Manufacturing Innovation and University of Tennessee through the Carbon Ore,
Rare Earth, and Critical Minerals (CORE-CM) funding opportunity announcement to assess the
potential of producing rare earth elements (REE), critical minerals (CM), and high-value,
nonfuel, carbon-based products in the southern Appalachian coal basin stretching from Alabama
through Georgia and to Tennessee. This research focused on REE and CM production from coal
waste streams – namely mine tailings and coal combustion residuals from coal-fired power
plants, or coal ash. While creatively working to transform waste into resource, new extraction
from coal is not without controversy. Communities across Southern Appalachia and the
Southeast have a long history of living with the legacy waste of the coal industry and on-going
impacts to environmental and human health associated with coal-fired power production. This
report briefly documents the history of coal use as well as research on the environmental and
human health implications from coal waste. The primary focus is on coal ash given the early
findings of the broader research team that coal ash represented the highest potential for beneficial
reuse and CM extraction. The report also considers primary data collected to assess stakeholders’
awareness and priorities related to coal ash clean-up. Primary data was gathered from expert
interviews with representatives from environmental and community organizations, and unions
working across the three-state region (n=9) and a representative, web-based survey of
households (n=996) living within an average distance of 18 miles from coal ash impoundments
across AL, GA, and TN. Across these sources, it is found that there is limited awareness of coal
ash, however, respondents described support for the beneficial reuse of coal ash that increases if
reuse could address environmental pollution. This report details best practices for community
engagement to increase awareness of the issue and improve information sharing and
transparency. It also documents community concerns and identified best practices required for
coal ash handling to protect worker and community health.

History of Coal Use and Coal Ash Regulation in the Southern Appalachian Basin
The Southern Appalachian region has a nearly two century long history of coal mining and coal
use. The region is home to thousands of acres of abandoned coal mines. In Alabama, an
estimated 7.49% of the state population, 347,000 people, live within a mile of an abandoned coal
mine, compared to 1.17% of the state population, or more than 75,000 people in TN. Following
the recent wave of bankruptcies, many more stand to be functionally abandoned (Eggers, 2024;

 161

Shelton and Cromer, 2024). Coal is and was mined to power heavy industry, rail, and electric
power production.

For nearly 150 years, utilities across the South have relied on coal. From a system that was once
heavily reliant on coal, coal use has declined in the region (U.S. Energy Information
Administration, 2022). Today, in Alabama, there are three operating coal plants. in Georgia, two
coal plants, and in Tennessee, three coal plants remain in operation (Alabama Power, 2025;
Georgia Power, 2025; Tennessee Valley Authority, n.d.). At a total of 23 current and historical
sites, coal ash, or coal combustion residuals, which are produced from burning coal, are stored in
large slurry ponds (EarthJustice, 2022). These slurry ponds were not a long-term solution to
managing coal waste and a series of spills have plagued the region. In 2008, a dike containing a
coal ash dewatering pond at the Kingston Fossil Plant failed, spilling 5.4 million cubic yards of
ash into the Emory River and covering more than 300 acres (Ruhl et al., 2010). The Kingston
spill was the single largest industrial accident in U.S. history. Efforts to clean up the spill took
more than six years. Of the thousand workers employed on the project, 400 have been sickened
and more than 60 have died (Satterfield, 2023).

Following the Kingston spill, the EPA ordered a review of coal ash impoundments in 2009. A
proposed rule issued in 2010 would have regulated coal ash as either a hazardous waste or
required disposal of waste to lined landfills. The “Disposal of Coal Combustion Residuals from
Electric Utilities” rulemaking was published in 2015 and established technical requirements
under the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) for storage of coal ash in landfills
and surface impoundments while also addressing disposal risks related to dust, groundwater
contamination, and recycling and reuse of coal ash (US EPA, 2024b). Notably, these rules are
intended to encourage the “beneficial use of coal ash in an appropriate and protective manner,”
which the EPA identifies can produce environmental, economic, and product benefits such as:

 - reduced use of virgin resources
 - lower greenhouse gas emissions
 - reduced cost of coal ash disposal
 - improved strength and durability of materials (US EPA 2024a).

According to the American Coal Ash Association, which is a trade association that has surveyed
coal ash reutilization annually since 1968, more than 60% of all coal ash produced in 2022 was
recycled. The most common end uses include concrete, cement, and industrial fill (American
Coal Ash Association, 2023; Lu et al., 2022). As the price of building materials increased during
and after the COVID-19 pandemic, the market for coal ash has grown.

Rulemaking around coal ash has continued in the years since. In 2016, Congress adopted the
Water Infrastructure Improvements for the Nation (WIIN) Act, which gave states the authority to
operate coal ash permit programs if EPA determines those standards are as protective as federal
law (US EPA, 2017). The Georgia Environmental Protection Division’s permitting program was
approved in 2019 (US EPA, 2019). The Alabama Department of Environmental Management’s
application was denied in 2024 (US EPA, 2023). In 2024, the EPA issued updated rules to close

 162

loopholes in the CCR rules by requiring compliance from closed coal ash impoundments and
issuing more stringent regulation of wastewater from coal plants (Lydersen, 2024).

This rule-making emerged in part from environmental and public health research into the effects
of coal-fired power generation (US EPA Office of Land and Emergency Management and US
EPA Office of Resource Conservation and Recovery, 2024). Extensive epidemiological research
on heavy metals and radioactive components found in coal link these materials to premature
death, increased risk of respiratory and cardiovascular disease, cancers, and infant mortality
(Kravchenko and Lyerly, 2018; Lauer et al., 2015). While coal ash is less-studied than coal
combustion, researchers have observed increased reports of respiratory symptoms following
exposure to ash as well as behavioral and sleep issues in children (Hagemeyer et al., 2019;
Longest et al., 2022; Sears and Zierold, 2017; Zierold et al., 2021, 2023; Zierold and Sears,
2015). The most extensive research of groundwater and surface water contamination from coal
ash are related to the Kingston coal ash spill (Ramsey et al., 2019; Ruhl et al., 2009, 2010). In a
broader study of fifteen sites in Tennessee, Kentucky, Georgia, Virginia, and North Carolina,
Harkness et al. (2016) reported systematic evidence of coal ash contaminants in wells and
groundwater adjacent to coal ash impoundments, leading to strong concerns of environmental
risks from unlined ponds (see also Frankel et al., 2025; Lombardi, 2009; Vengosh et al., 2019).
The connection between coal pollution and asthma as well as coal pollution and premature death
from cardiovascular, cancer, and respiratory disease is well-established (Hendryx et al., 2020;
Henneman et al., 2023), leading also to concerns about the control of dust in the dewatering and
transport of coal ash and contamination from fugitive coal waste.

Given these health concerns, coal ash disposal has been an issue of on-going lawsuits
challenging interpretation and enforcement of environmental law within the service territories of
each of the major utilities in the study area. A central focus has been on the method of disposal.
Environmental advocates have pushed for utilities that have stored coal ash in unlined pits to
excavate these ponds and remove waste to lined landfills to prevent leaching into the water table
(EarthJustice, 2022; Georgia Coal Ash, n.d.; Southern Environmental Law Center, n.d.). Some
key cases have alleged environmental contamination harmed workers and communities. In the
past two years alone, a series of settlements related to coal ash have been announced. In 2024,
Georgia Power settled a lawsuit with 69 residents of Juliette, Georgia, who alleged that clusters
of cancer and renal and respiratory diseases in the town were related to coal ash from Plant
Scherer contaminating well water (Blankenship, 2024; Blau, 2021). That same year, the EPA and
Alabama Power came to a settlement regarding alleged violations at Plant Barry in Mobile Bay
requiring the utility to pay a fine and implement “more robust groundwater monitoring program
and to revise its Emergency Action Plan,” to “help protect surrounding communities along the
Mobile River from potential coal ash contamination” (Pillion, 2024). In 2023, the TVA’s
contractor Jacobs Engineering settled a suit out of court from workers and family members
affected by the Kingston clean-up (Satterfield, 2023). The transport of waste from the Kingston
coal ash spill to the Arrowhead Landfill, located in a majority Black community, in Uniontown,
Alabama had also previously sparked local and national outrage over dumping in a poor and
minoritized community (Black Belt Citizens Fighting for Health and Justice, 2022; Engelman-
Lado et al., 2021; Foster, 2011). In Memphis, where TVA agreed to remove coal ash from an
unlined pit and store in a lined landfill, there remains on-going concern and community outcry
regarding TVA’s process to remove ash from the Allen Fossil Plant to a landfill on trucks that

 163

drive through Black neighborhoods in Memphis (Fears, 2022; Hilles, 2022; Interview July 11,
2023).

Given these concerns about health and safety in disposal, recycling and reuse is often seen as a
“win-win” situation because coal ash is removed from communities and permanently stored in
physical infrastructure and utilities can earn a return for sale of the waste product (Interview
August 8, 2022). Yet, advocates have raised particular concerns about allowable, unencapsulated
uses such as fertilizer or roadway or foundation fill given the potential to disperse pollution and
challenges in tracking and monitoring (Evans and Hallowell, 2017; Locke et al., 2020; Quinn,
2022). Across all beneficial reuse applications, there is limited research on environmental and
human health effects. Advocates interviewed in this study raised concern about dispersing
environmental contamination as coal ash is reutilized in building materials and called for
research into beneficial reuse (Interview, July 11, 2023; July 27, 2023). In addition, new uses for
coal ash from critical material extraction may present novel health concerns associated with the
process of refining and processing materials. Limited research available on critical mineral
extraction and processing indicates there is also a link to adverse environmental and human
health impacts, however, given that the vast majority of refining, especially for rare earth
elements occurs in China where environmental law is less stringent than in the U.S., greater
research and monitoring is needed to assess both the impacts and potential mitigation measures
to prevent serious environmental health concerns (Ali 2014; Martin and Iles 2017; Yin et al
2021).

Without attention to the risks of coal ash, improper handling threatens to exacerbate systemic
inequalities and vulnerabilities to adverse health impacts. Across the study region, disparities in
pollution exposure are so severe advocates have long raised the issue of protection from an
environmental harm as a civil rights issue under Title VI of the Civil Rights Act (Copley, 1994;
on coal ash and environmental disparities see also Broto, 2013; Eldridge, 2018; Evans et al.,
2011; Longest et al., 2022; Russ and Evans, 2018). Nationwide, coal-fired power plants are
disproportionately located in minoritized and low-income communities. According to the
NAACP, in 2014, 68% of Black and African American individuals lived within thirty miles of a
coal fired power plant (Hayes et al., 2014). These same patterns hold in Georgia, where a study
found that Black communities make up forty-five percent of the population that lives within one
mile of a coal-fired power plant despite the state’s population being only thirty percent Black
(De Amorim et al., 2022). However, when looking nationally, recent research found
socioeconomic status may be a better predictor of exposure to coal ash than race. Census tracts
with high poverty were twice as likely to contain coal ash pits and non-white populations had
90% lower odds of being proximate to coal ash pits (Shingara et al., 2024). Understanding who
lives near coal ash impoundments is especially important to consider given that low-income and
minoritized communities are often more likely to face existing health issues related to lack of
access to affordable medical care and healthy food that increase vulnerability to environmental
exposure (Prochaska et al., 2014). Long-standing public health research emphasizes the need to
assess cumulative impact to exposure. To safeguard health and well-being for communities
adjacent to coal ash impoundments that are potentially exposed to water and air pollution from
coal use and coal ash waste requires a comprehensive lens that considers historic, cumulative
impact and the social factors that may further exacerbate vulnerability (Liévanos, 2018; Morello-
Frosch et al., 2011; Su et al., 2012). Further, these same communities may have barriers to

 164

accessing information or participating in public processes to give feedback on industrial and
energy processes in their communities.

Methodology
This analysis is based on nine expert interviews with environmental and community
organizations and union leaders in AL, GA, and TN (referred to as the study region) and a survey
of households living adjacent to coal ash impoundments. Interviews helped to establish a
baseline of recent regulatory decisions, document notable legal and regulatory challenges to coal
ash disposal and record sustained concerns of residents and workers who have organized around
the issue of coal waste disposal across the region.

The survey was conducted using panel data from Qualtrics. Respondents were asked to conduct a
15-minute survey and those that completed the questionnaire received a gift card. The survey
was open to residents living in or adjacent to counties with coal ash impoundments. A complete
list of counties in which survey respondents live can be found in Table A1. More than 1200
people responded to the survey with 996 complete responses. Relative to the regional population,
survey participants disproportionately identified as women (73%). The majority of respondents
identified as white (66%), roughly equal to the share of regional residents identifying as white
across the three states comprising the study area (70%). Average age of respondents was 43
years. Median household income was $25,000-49,999, which is below the median household
income for AL ($62,027), GA ($74,664), and TN ($67,097) between 2019-2023. Among the
most cited occupations were retired, disabled, and unemployed, indicating that low- and fixed-
income people are overrepresented in the survey and hence bring down the overall income of the
sample. The highest level of education completed varied widely with a median response of some
college. The average household size of respondents was 2.9 persons, which roughly corresponds
with average household size across the region (2.47-2.64). The distance that respondents live
from coal ash impoundments was calculated using an estimate from the center of a zip code to
the location of the nearest ash pond. The average distance respondents live from coal ash
impoundments was 18 miles.

The survey questions were informed by interviews and literature review of hazards identified in
coal ash. The questions focused on concerns related to the environment and utilities; awareness
of coal ash; preference for safe handling of coal ash; and access to information. The survey first
aimed to assess awareness and concern about coal ash, asking generally what the primary
environmental concerns that respondents identify in their communities. Protecting open land,
animal habitat, and waste and waste management were the most important and pollution related
to coal was the least likely to be identified as an issue of extreme concern. The survey also
provided an open text response where respondents could add other concerns. The most
referenced concerns not cited in the survey were trash and crime. Only one person mentioned
“coal ash” as well as “mercury contamination.” Four respondents referenced nearby or specific
gas or coal power plants and one mentioned pollution from coal mining.

 Figure A1. Environmental Concerns

After establishing baseline environmental concerns, the survey then asked participants about coal
ash specifically and provided a definition of coal ash, understanding that this may not be an issue
with which all are familiar. The survey asked: “Coal combustion residuals or coal ash is
produced as a byproduct from burning coal. Coal ash must be stored or disposed of following
proper management procedures because it contains contaminants that can pollute waterways,
drinking water, and the air. How aware are you of coal ash in your community?” Despite media
attention on coal ash across the Southeast and Southern Appalachia, as detailed above, most
respondents ranging from 56% in AL to 46% in TN reported they were “not at all aware” of coal
ash in their communities (Figure A2). Individuals living in TN, which notably experienced the
largest coal ash spill in the US in 2008, were slightly more likely to be aware of coal ash.
Proximity to coal ash impoundments did positively affect a respondent’s awareness. When
controlling for education, income, race, gender, and state of residence, those living within 15
miles of a coal ash impoundment were 1.27 times more likely (95% CI 1.12 - 1.44) to be aware
of coal ash residuals compared to those living greater than 15 miles from a coal ash
impoundment.

Understanding that many respondents were not at all aware of coal ash, the survey still asked
about common pathways to handle and reuse coal ash. Notably, capping coal ash in place
received strong opposition relative to other proposed disposal strategies with more than 20% of
respondents saying they do not support at all and less than 50% of respondents supporting
compared to 55% support for coal ash reuse. This is an important finding given that utilities in
the region have proceeded with plans to cap ash ponds in place, including those in unlined pits
that remain submerged in groundwater, despite concern from environmental advocates about the
known potential risks to human and environmental health (cf. Georgia Coal Ash, n.d.). The
beneficial reuse of coal ash to be recycled into other products received the greatest support with
more than a third of respondents strongly in favor of this proposed strategy (see Figure A3).
These responses should be read alongside the feedback from communities and workers who have
organized after experiencing adverse effects of coal ash. Specific harms and strategies for redress
are discussed in the following section and recorded in Figures A9 and A10.

When looking specifically at differences across states, there is some variation in support for the
beneficial reuse of coal ash with the greatest enthusiasm in TN and the strongest concern in GA
(Figure A4). Given the lack of awareness regarding coal ash express generally, it is assumef that
support for reuse reflects the general social enthusiasm for making new, useful products,
reflecting the “win-win” mentality of recycling, as well as aversion to landfills, which are widely
opposed due to potential or perceived environmental harms.

To try to tease out the concerns related to coal ash reuse, an attempt was made to gauge both
whether coal ash recycling and reuse would affect personal sense of safety and whether it is
something that respondents would be willing to endorse not only on a general level, but in their
communities. Most respondents indicated that they would either feel completely or somewhat
safe with the reuse of coal ash and that they would completely or somewhat support reuse of coal
ash in their communities. However, respondents were more ambivalent on the specific questions
of critical material extraction from coal ash. While there is still general support, the greatest
share of respondents selected a neutral response that they would neither support, nor oppose
critical material extraction (Figure A5). Notably, when controlling for education, income,
gender, state, and distance from a coal ash impoundment, non-white respondents were 1.37 times
more likely (95% CI 0.98 - 1.93) to express safety concerns about reusing coal ash compared to
white respondents.

Given that most respondents self-identified as having no to slight awareness of coal ash as a
concern in their communities, the survey also asked what factors would influence their potential
support for the beneficial reuse of coal ash. Strikingly, the most important factor was pollution
reduction (40% would be extremely likely to support), followed by saving customers money
(36% would be extremely likely to support), and creating local jobs (27% would be extremely
likely to support). In contrast, having more information about the process was less likely to have
a strong influence on potential support. This indicates that in communicating about beneficial
reuse programs, companies may be best served in demonstrating tangible benefits to
communities of the removal and reuse programs.

The DOE encourages pro-active, two-way communication to ensure community feedback on
proposed projects. As utilities consider both expanding beneficial reuse of coal ash and critical
mineral extraction, there is a need to understand how neighboring residents access information
and how public feedback can be solicited to ensure adequate response to community concerns.
Social media, local media, and local government are the places where respondents most often
turn for news (Figure A7). Utilities could also use their platforms through monthly billing to
notify residents of any new programing related to coal ash reuse in their communities. Across the
region, there is strong trust in utilities with 74% of respondents saying that they moderately to
completely trust their utility. Utilities can communicate information about changes in coal ash
handling and establish mechanisms to register and respond to community concerns. Notably,
respondents also referenced that they access information through community centers, senior
centers, community meetings, and homeowners association meetings. Utilities can approach
community organizations, churches, and other trusted stakeholders to provide information about
new facilities or processes related to coal ash reuse and disposal and hear feedback on any
concerns related to how and where ash is moved. Respondents also indicated that they are most
likely to share concerns through social media (Figure A8). Given this, in addition to proactive
community meetings and outreach with stakeholder groups, online resources for reporting
feedback on reuse programs may be an effective way to gather stakeholder input.

Discussion and Conclusion
The results of the survey demonstrate that residents have limited knowledge about coal ash in
their communities, yet clear priorities for improving environmental quality and reducing
pollution. Advocates who are more informed than the average survey respondent about coal ash
have raised consistent and significant concerns related to coal ash for decades, focusing
particular attention on 1) cost and 2) process for coal ash handling to ensure worker and
community safety. Cost has been a particular concern in Georgia where advocates before the
state Public Service Commission have protested the additional burden of coal ash clean-up in
ratepayer bills (Beilin, 2022; Kann, 2022; Worley, 2024). One way that utilities could address

 171

these concerns is by providing greater transparency about calculation of costs, process of clean-
up, and revenue from the sale of fly ash, gypsum, and other recycled materials.

Concerns about the process for coal ash handling have been widely shared through advocate
networks who have developed a set of best practices. For instance, affected communities and
workers across the state in Tennessee came together to issue a set of “Unifying Principles on
Coal Ash.” These principles were co-signed by twelve environmental, community, labor, and
faith organizations. They are reproduced in their entirety in Figure A9. Notably, organizations
call on “federal, state and local decision makers” including TVA, EPA, the Occupational Health
and Safety Administration, the Tennessee Department of Environment and Conservation to
enforce and uphold proper handling of ash. Importantly, community members and families of
workers affected by the Kingston coal ash spill have also compiled best practices for worker
health and safety on coal ash projects. Five major areas are outlined: 1) Pre-Procurement
Screenings; 2) Coal Ash Safety Training; 3) Protective Equipment and Procedures; 4) Anti-
Retaliation; and 5) Enforcement and Regulation. These recommendations are included in their
entirety in Figure A10.

To ensure representation and accountability to the many residents living near coal ash
impoundments requires expanded strategies for engagement and information sharing. Recent
years have seen increasing pressure for universities, companies, and scientists to participate more
actively through community engagement that democratizes access to scientific knowledge and
improves pathways for stakeholder participation and feedback (Stylinski et al., 2018). Some
states have gone so far as to require community engagement, building on and strengthening long-
standing tenets of the National Environmental Protection Act, which requires consultation with
affected stakeholders (e.g. State of Vermont, Agency of Natural Resources 2023). In particular,
with relation to the energy industry, a raft of research on energy development demonstrates the
importance of proactive and two-way engagement to share plans for new energy development
from large-scale renewables to carbon capture and demonstration projects (Coyle, 2016; Devine-
Wright, 2011; Farley et al., 2021; Gooding et al., 2023; Ross and Day, 2022). Proactive
community engagement can build community trust and address concerns that may arise before
project development begins, mitigating risk that a project will move forward or face legal
challenges. The National Renewable Energy Lab recommends five best practices that serve to
meaningfully and proactively engage communities and work to build community capacity and
knowledge over time to interact and give feedback in stakeholder processes. These key lessons
for energy plannings include:

 1. Do your homework in preparation for community interactions.
 2. Be humble, authentic, and honest in your interactions with community members.
 3. Respect community agency in every step of the process.
 4. Meet the community where they are.
 5. Democratize participation (Ross and Day, 2022, 1).

 172

These same lessons can be applied to processes of plant conversion or closure and coal ash pond
closure (Lessick et al., 2021; Tarekegne et al., 2021), including in the closure, dewatering,
excavation, and reuse of coal ash.

The research found there is a high degree of trust in utilities across the study region from survey
respondents. However, advocates have long opposed utility coal ash handling policies and state
regulatory agency’s deference to the industries that they oversee, including raising concerns
about devolution of coal ash oversight and administration to state regulatory authorities in EPA
hearings in Alabama in 2023 and Georgia in 2019 (see also Foster, 2025; Southern
Environmental Law Center, 2019). Relatedly, advocates have raised objections that community
input can feel pro forma without actually having an impact on decision-making for how coal is
moved (Hilles, 2022). As utilities consider expansion of beneficial reuse programs and the
possibility of reopening ash pits to excavate materials, greater transparency is needed to rebuild
trust. This requires collaboration to develop strategies for removal; to recognize community lived
experience as expertise; to respond to and meaningfully address community feedback; to build
community capacity to participate in decision-making fora; to improve monitoring for
groundwater and air pollution; and to implement strategies to mitigate and communicate about
spills and other potential hazards.
