 Figure A9. Tennessee Unifying Principles on Coal Ash.
 Reproduced from: (Remember Kingston, 2022)

 Tennessee Unifying Principles On Coal Ash

 Tennessee communities and workers currently impacted by coal ash, and those
 threatened by it in the future, have joined together with a shared vision and common
 goals for the future of coal ash in our state. We call on federal, state and local decision
 makers, including TVA, OSHA, EPA, the Tennessee Department of Environment and
 Conservation, the TN congressional delegation, TN state senators and representatives,
 and local governments, to make sure no community is left to suffer from coal ash in the
 future.

 We extend our thanks to the Alliance of Carolinians Together (ACT) Against Coal Ash,
 whose unifying principles in North Carolina served as an initial template for the
 principles below.

 1. We believe that all people, regardless of race and socio-economic class, have a right to
 healthy communities, safe jobs, clean water, clean air, and safe soil and food.
 2. We believe that because of TVA’s mishandling and storage of coal ash, living and
 working in close proximity to TVA’s coal ash dumps infringes on these basic human
 rights.
 3. We demand a transparent process to coal ash cleanup in which TVA and TN decision
 makers are open and honest about the health effects of contaminants found in coal ash,
 and any plans for disposal or recycling of coal ash.
 4. We demand that TVA and TN decision makers hold public hearings both statewide and
 in every impacted community whenever any decisions regarding coal ash cleanup are
 made, with time for community members and experts invited by the community to
 provide input. These hearings should be advertised through multiple public media
 platforms for at least 30 days.
 5. We demand that TVA and its contractors take responsibility for their acts, past, present,
 and future, that have contributed to the toxic coal ash situation, including but not limited
 to the contamination of groundwater, surface water, air, and the neglect of worker safety.
 6. We demand that TVA, OSHA and EPA prioritize safety and enforcement for contract and
 annual workers during all phases of coal ash cleanup and site remediation by following
 regulations and improving current safety practices, enforcement and whistleblower
 protections via input from local experts and impacted workers.
 7. We ask that TVA suspend executive bonuses until every coal ash site is cleaned up safely
 and in cooperation with workers and impacted communities. We demand that the ash be
 stored at an elevated and isolated, well-lined location, that is high, dry, and away from
 people and water.
 8. We call on TVA, EPA and TDEC to urgently respond to the need to test any water supply

 175

Final Report DE-FE0032045

 or source that may have been contaminated by coal ash, not just those within a half mile
 of the ash ponds. The tests must be performed by a disinterested, independent lab that is
 concealed to TVA employees and using the most sensitive and comprehensive testing
 methods for coal ash.
 9. We call on TN decision makers to require independent oversight of the coal ash cleanup
 process, independent analysis of current coal ash contamination in the air, water, soil, and
 in living organisms, research by public and private entities to find the best solutions to
 this problem, and random and unannounced inspections of the coal ash sites by state
 regulators.
 10. We call on TN decision makers and TVA to strive for a permanent solution to coal ash
 that prioritizes community safety. We will not accept dumping of the ash in other
 communities or capping-in-place as solutions. As stated above, we demand that the ash
 be stored at an elevated and isolated, well-lined location, and kept high, dry, and away
 from people and water.
 11. We call on TVA and TN decision makers to invest in a sustainable, healthy, affordable,
 and responsible energy future for TN that supports the growth of solar, wind energy, and
 energy efficiency programs, and moves away from coal, methane gas, and other harmful
 and expensive methods of generating power that poison communities and affect
 Tennesseans’ quality of life.

  Figure A10. Recommended best practices for worker health and safety on coal ash projects.
 Reproduced from a resource provided by Appalachian Voices.

 PROTECTING WORKERS DURING COAL ASH HANDLING AND CLEAN UP

 The following coal ash worker safety proposals have been developed by community
 members and worker families in East Tennessee. While we believe these policies and
 practices are critical for worker protection when handling coal ash, this list is not
 comprehensive.

 Proposals:

 1. Pre-Procurement Screenings
 • Develop a process by which TVA screens potential coal-ash contractors for their capacity
 and commitment to handle coal ash in a safe and healthy manner under circumstances
 such as: (a) orderly transfer as part of planned closure, or (b) massive emergency moving
 of material after an unplanned spill.

 2. Coal Ash Safety Training
 • Develop in consultation with the affected trades that contractors and subcontractors are
 using.
 • Should adequately communicate the risks involved, the safety procedures required, the
 right to refuse dangerous work, and the legal protections that workers can call upon if
 they need to lodge a complaint or seek assistance from someone higher up their
 immediate supervisory chain or from TVA itself.
 • Should follow the apprenticeship model: must be conducted in person by people with
 relevant experience and should include interactive hands-on components.
 • Should be ongoing - including training follow-up, assessment(s) of how well each trainee
 understood and absorbed the material, a period of active supervision by someone with
 more experience and preparation, and refresher events.
 • Unions should be asked for their active assistance in training programs, including support
 from international health and safety staff, and should be paid for providing their
 expertise.
 • Include support from the Center for Construction Research & Training.
 • TVA and federal OSHA should provide oversight of safety training programs.

 3. Protective Equipment & Procedures
 Require:
 • Onsite decontamination facilities with showers.
 • Company-provided work clothing and laundering service.
 • Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) for workers, including Tyvek protective suits when

 177

Final Report DE-FE0032045

 appropriate and respirators.
 • Abide by existing standards for PPE requiring medical assessment, proper fit testing, and
 safe procedures for timely replacement and/or cleaning.
 • Baseline medical examinations for workers.
 • Onsite medical personnel.
 • Continuous air monitoring capability – PM10, PM2.5, toxic metals, and transuranic
 elements.
 • Trucks, and Heavy Equipment:
 o Properly equip and consistently maintain trucks and heavy equipment used to
 excavate or transport coal ash so that ash is kept out of the cabs.
 o Daily truck (and worker vehicle) decontamination and changing of cabin filters
 • Implement effective tools and procedures to eliminate/minimize fugitive dust generation.
 • Require contractors to have a site-specific Safety and Health Plan
 o Approved and signed off by a certified industrial hygienist (ABIH certification)
 and then re-evaluated and re-approved by a CIH every 6 months or year.
 o Includes provisions for union and worker involvement.
 o Make the plan available to the public (i.e., opportunity for public oversight).

 4. Anti-Retaliation
 o Include in the Safety and Health plan specific whistleblower protections and
 procedures.

 5. Enforcement and Regulation
 • Establish a coal ash worker safety Hotline for worker complaints.
 • Safety and Health Plans must be submitted to OSHA who could provide monitoring &
 oversight and enforce when it's not being followed.
 • Include trigger (e.g., stop work order) when/if there's evidence that the Safety and Health
 Plan is not being followed.
 • Monitor contractor work more closely:
 o Require repeated, unannounced TVA and OSHA safety visits at the site of any
 significant coal-ash handling, whether that be at a spill site during clean-up, or on
 another kind of site where coal ash in significant quantities is being shipped out
 or moved from one site to another.
 o Consider providing that TVA inspectors will not sign off on interim payments to
 the contractor without first confirming compliance with the Safety and Health
 plan.

 178

Final Report DE-FE0032045

 o No completion bonuses should be paid to a contractor unless worker safety has
 been adequately protected across the life of the contract.
 o Consider using video to record safety equipment and decontamination stations
 being distributed and made available to workers on a regular basis.

APPENDIX B: Summary of Economic Revitalization and Job Creation Outcomes

The analysis begins with the identification of products that may utilize rare inputs. These
products are then aligned with industry sectors as defined by the standard accounting framework
of the North American Industrial Classification System (NAICS). Data on the number of
business establishments and employment for the identified industry sectors is then presented for
the three-state region that includes Alabama, Georgia and Tennessee, as well as the U.S. An
important finding is that the three-state region has a greater relative presence (or specialization)
of industry sectors using or potentially using rare inputs than the nation. The implication is that
the region has the opportunity to be a major user of rare inputs, supporting industrial production
and job creation and reducing dependence on rare input imports.
The first step in the analysis is identification of products that may use REE, CM and CBP, using
publicly-available resources and the expert judgement of the research team supporting this
project. 18 Table A2 provides a listing of products using or potentially using rare inputs. Most of
these are intermediate products that are used to produce other intermediate products, though
some final products that are further downstream in the supply chain are on the list as well. For
example, rare inputs may be used to make construction materials, including bricks. These bricks
may then be used in a variety of construction applications. Batteries may be an end product for
direct use by a consumer or an input to the production of a computer or another product.
 Table A2. Products using REE, CM and CBP.
 Products using REE Products using CM and CBP
 Concrete & masonry products Construction materials
 Ceramic tiles Batteries
 Kiln/refractory liners Vehicle bodies
 Electric vehicles Jet engine components
 Fracking poppants Turbines
 Catalysts (alumina, silica) Refractories and superalloys
 Vehicle bodies Steel products
 Batteries Carbon fiber

 Display screens

 Fiber optics

 See “Rare Earth Elements—End Use and Recyclability,” Scientific Investigations Report 2011-5094, U.S. Department of the
Interior and U.S. Geological Survey. Available at https://pubs.usgs.gov/sir/2011/5094/pdf/sir2011-5094.pdf and “Draft Critical
Minerals List—Summary of Methodological and Background Information—U.S. Geological Survey Technical Input Document

The second step is to link the products shown in Table A2 to specific industry sectors, a process
that requires judgement calls. For example, body armor may be produced using CM/CBP.
However, as the NAICS data show, there are 24 different industry sectors that produce or utilize
some form of body armor. In some instances, judgement calls are straightforward across these
sectors. For example, NAICS 561612, security guards and patrol services, is not included since
this sector reflects provision of a service that is several steps downstream from the direct use of
rare inputs. The same applies to NAICS 928110, national security, another service activity
several links away from direct rare input use. 19 The focus here is on sectors that are directly
involved in the use of rare inputs or just one step downstream in the production process, a
distinction that is difficult to make in practice.
Table A3 shows the industry sectors that have been mapped to products using one or more of the
rare inputs REE, CM and CBP. This includes 23 specific four-digit industries. Table A4 provides
a crosswalk showing how many of the rare inputs are mapped into specific four-digit NAICS
sectors.

The final step is to use the QCEW data to characterize the scope of rare input-related industries
and their business employment and establishment ecosystem in the southern Appalachian region.
Table A5 and Table A6 show employment and the number of establishments, respectively, for
the industries identified in Table A5 for Alabama, Georgia, Tennessee and the U.S. Note that
generally the combined Alabama-Georgia-Tennessee region has relatively more employment and
establishments in the industries than the U.S. This dominance, expressed in relative terms, is
intended to capture the degree of industry specialization across the respective regions.
Employment in the identified industries is 2.9 percent of total economy-wide employment for the
three states versus 2.6 percent for the U.S.; establishments are 0.8 percent of total establishments
for the three states combined compared to 0.7 percent for the U.S. 20
Note from the tables that Alabama and Tennessee are driving these overall findings, with
Georgia individually lagging the U.S. in terms of the share of both employment and
establishments linked to the possible use of rare inputs. 21 (Because of Georgia’s size, it has a
large number of establishments and jobs in the target sectors.) The establishment share figures

20 The overall manufacturing sector accounts for 10.7 percent of total employment for the three-state region versus 8.7 percent for

the U.S. Manufacturing business establishments represent 3.9 percent of all establishments for the three states compared to 3.4
percent for the U.S. U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages, 2020.
21 This is consistent with the states’ relative reliance on the overall manufacturing sector where Georgia places 3rd among the

three states. U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages, 2020.

 188

are less than 1 percent for all the regions while the employment share figures are all in excess of
2 percent.
Relative employment shares are higher and thus the degree of specialization is greater in the
three-state region than the U.S. in all sectors other than 3335, 3336, 3341, 3342, 3344, 3345 and
3359. With a smaller number of exceptions, the relative share of business establishments tends to
be higher in the three states than the U.S. There are 263,833 manufacturing jobs in the southern
Appalachian region, with 78,167 in Alabama, 91,768 in Georgia and 93,898 in Tennessee.
Counts of establishments at the county level in Alabama, Georgia, and Tennessee, on average,
range between zero and three for the NAICS codes of interest. The small number of firms
explains why employment data for the key sectors is suppressed at the county level, as
previously discussed. Summing across all of the four-digit NAICS reveals that there is an
average of 11 total establishments for counties in Georgia. Similarly, there is an average of 18
and 17 total establishments for counties in Alabama and Tennessee. Fulton County in Georgia,
which encompasses most of Atlanta, has the highest number of total establishments (212
establishments), followed by Gwinnett County in Georgia (201 establishments). Ranking third
and fourth is Jefferson and Madison County in Alabama, with a total of 173 and 146
establishments, respectively. Davidson County in Tennessee, which includes Nashville, has a
total of 145 establishments across the sectors of interest.
Together these data indicate that the Alabama-Georgia-Tennessee region offers a fertile base to
exploit the application of rare inputs. The industries identified here will have an existing supply
chain and a trained workforce that should be able to adapt to new production technologies. In
addition, the overall business environment is already supportive of manufacturing business
activity by virtue of the presence of a large share of employment and establishments in the
region.

 APPENDIX C: Environmental, Safety, and Health Analysis

Team member Alex Johs, in partnership with other ORNL staff, completed a draft report for
ESH concerns in coal ash processing during the April-June quarter. The ES&H report in its
current form outlines regulations and guidelines for beneficial use of CCRs. The U.S. EPA
broadly defines CCRs as industrial secondary materials that contain various constituents of
potential concern (COPC), such as low levels of toxic metals and traces of radioactive elements.
Potential risks associated with processing coal ash were reviewed, such as respiratory hazards
from dust emissions and other exposure hazards. The EPA and OSHA offer guidance for risk
evaluation practices regarding the handling and processing of industrial secondary materials,
such as CCRs. These guidelines aim to minimize environmental and occupational hazards while
encouraging the reuse of industrial materials. The evaluation of unencapsulated uses requires
additional, site- and process-specific considerations.
Concerns for chemical, biological, and electromagnetic separations were prepared with input
from Task 5 team members during the second quarter of 2023.
The Environmental, Safety, and Health analysis is provided below.

Background
CCRs are generated when coal is burned in coal-fired power plants for electricity production. A
large volume of CCRs is stored in impoundments or landfills within the SoApp region
representing a substantial potential resource for the extraction of REE and feedstocks for
graphite and graphene production, but contain fine particles with potentially hazardous
components, such as heavy metals and silica, which pose risks to human health and the
environment if not managed properly. The handling and processing of CCRs are primarily
governed by federal and state regulatory frameworks. On the federal level, EPA provides
guidance on Environmental Safety, and Health (ES&H) aspects through regulations, guidance
documents, and technical recommendations to assess and minimize potential risks to human
health and the environment.

Composition and Categories of Coal Combustion Products
CCRs are composed of a variety of components that can vary depending on the type of coal
being burned, the method of combustion, and the pollution control technologies used. Major
components of CCR are 50-70% silica, 15-30% alumina, 10-20% iron oxides, 2-15% calcium
oxide, and other minerals such as titanium oxide, magnesium oxide, and potassium oxide.
Generally, CCRs can be categorized into the following types of materials:

• Fly ash is a byproduct created through coal combustion and consists of fine particles carried
 by the exhaust gases and are typically captured by particle filtration equipment. Fly ash
 typically makes up the largest portion of CCRs and contains predominantly silica, alumina,
 calcium oxide, iron oxide and unburnt carbon. Fly ash may contain the following trace
 constituents or residuals (<0.1%): arsenic, antimony, barium oxide, boron, beryllium,
 cadmium, chromium, lead, manganese, mercury, nickel, phosphorus oxide, potassium oxide,
 selenium, titanium dioxide, uranium, vanadium and other metals in trace amounts.

 192

• Bottom ash is a coarser material that settles out of the flue gases and accumulates at the
 bottom of the furnace. Bottom ash is typically grey or black in color and is made up of larger
 particles than fly ash. Similar to fly ash, silica, alumina, calcium oxide, iron oxide and
 unburnt carbon are a major components of bottom ash, although the relative proportions can
 vary significantly. The particles sizes of bottom ash are larger than those of fly ash, with a
 gravel or sand-like texture.
• Boiler slag is molten ash that is removed from the bottom of the boiler and cooled with water
 trapping traps heavier impurities in the coal. Boiler slag is typically dark-colored and has a
 glassy or granulated appearance. Silica, alumina, iron oxide, calcium oxide, and magnesium
 oxide are a major components of boiler slag.

Potential environmental safety and health risks associated with coal ash utilization

Dust and air emissions
Coal ash is a fine powder that can easily become airborne, posing a risk of respiratory problems
for workers. Dust and air emissions can also be generated during the processing of coal ash,
which can lead to respiratory problems, especially for workers who are exposed to it regularly
without proper protective equipment. Coal ash contains small amounts of known pollutants,
including silica, heavy metals such as lead, mercury, and arsenic, and - depending on the coal
feedstock - radioactive elements, typically uranium, potassium, and thorium, which can be
released into the air during transportation and handling of the material. Inhalation, ingestion, or
contact with skin or eyes can lead to acute and chronic health effects, such as silicosis, chronic
obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), and asthma or allergic reactions. Long-term exposure to
silica and certain metals can result in elevated lung cancer risks.

Potential for contamination of surface and ground water
Coal ash can contaminate surface and ground water if it is not properly stored or managed.
Pollutants in coal ash can leach into water sources and harm aquatic life and humans who
consume the contaminated water. Heavy metals such as arsenic, lead, mercury, cadmium,
chromium, and selenium are of particular concern. Heavy metal levels depend on the type of coal
ash contain higher levels of hazardous pollutants than others. Wastewater generated by the
processing of coal ash should be treated and disposed of properly. Furthermore, the
environmental impact of reagents and processes used during the extraction of REE needs to be
considered.

Fire and explosion hazards
Coal ash can generate heat and spontaneous combustion if not stored properly. It can also pose
an explosion hazard if it is stored in large piles.

Hazardous substances in CCRs
Heavy metals are the most significant hazardous components of CCR due to their persistence,
bioaccumulation potential, and toxicity. Heavy metals present in coal ash include arsenic,
beryllium, cadmium, chromium, lead, mercury, and selenium. These metals can be found in
concentrations ranging from 0.1 mg/kg to 10,000 mg/kg, depending on the coal type (e.g.,

 193

bituminous, subbituminous, lignite), combustion process, and pollution control technologies
used. While CCR is classified as non-hazardous waste under the Resource Conservation and
Recovery Act (RCRA), it includes constituents recognized as hazardous in other regulatory
contexts due to their toxicity, carcinogenicity, or environmental mobility. Below is a detailed list
of the primary hazardous substances found in CCR, based on scientific data, EPA assessments,
and regulatory frameworks as of March 14, 2025.

Radioactivity
Coal ash can contain low levels of radioactive elements, such as uranium (238U, 235U), potassium
(40K), thorium (232Th) and their decay products, e.g. 226Ra, 228Ra, 210Pb, 210Po. While these
isotopes contribute to the radioactivity of coal ash, the overall radioactivity levels are typically
low. However, the concentration of these isotopes can be higher in coal ash than in the source
coal due to increased concentrations after the combustion process. CCRs derived from coals in
the Appalachian Basin show activities of 283 ± 34 Bq/kg on average (Lauer et al., 2015).
Generally, total Naturally Occurring Radioactive Materials (NORM) activities in CCRs are 7–10
times higher than NORM activities in parent coals and 3–5 times higher than average U.S. soil
(Lauer et al., 2015)

Occupational health risks and personal protective equipment
Workers who handle coal ash may be at risk of inhaling dust containing the pollutants mentioned
above and can lead to respiratory problems and other health issues. These potential exposure
risks can be addressed by appropriate safety measures, such as personal protective equipment
(PPE), to protect workers from exposure.
If coal ash is not properly stored or managed, nearby communities may be exposed to pollutants
from the ash through air and water pollution, which can lead to a variety of health problems,
including cancer, neurological disorders, and respiratory issues.

Inhalation
High concentrations of airborne dusts are irritating to the upper respiratory tract with symptoms
such as coughing, sneezing and shortness of breath. Long-term inhalation exposure to dusts
containing respirable size crystalline silica can cause silicosis and lung cancer.

Eye Contact
CCR dusts can be severely irritating in contact with eyes and can causes eye damage which may
be permanent and in severe cases cause blindness. Solid particles react with moisture in the eye
to form clumps of moist compound which may be difficult to remove. Sharp, glassy particles can
cause damage to eye tissue by mechanical abrasion.

Skin Contact
CCR dusts, when combined with water or sweat, produce an irritating alkaline solution and
possible burning of the skin. Potential symptoms include pain, skin dryness, cracking, eczema
and possible caustic burns. Sharp, glassy particles can cause damage to the skin.

 194

Ingestion
CCRs are severely irritating to the mouth, throat and gastro-intestinal system if swallowed.
Symptoms may include severe pain and burning of the mouth, throat, esophagus and
gastrointestinal tract with nausea, vomiting and diarrhea.

Regulations on the Disposal and Beneficial Use of Coal Combustion Residuals

The EPA published a CCR Rule on the Disposal of CCRs from electric utilities in 2015 (80 FR
21301, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2015-00257), which was revised in 2024 (89 FR
38950, https://www.federalregister.gov/d/2024-09157) after incorporating regulatory
requirements for legacy CCR surface impoundments. The CCR Rule regulates the disposal and
handling of coal ash, as a non-hazardous waste under Subtitle D under RCRA. This classification
means it is not subject to the stricter Subtitle C hazardous waste rules, but it still imposes
significant requirements to prevent environmental contamination and ensure safe management.
The establishment of this rule followed thorough research on the impacts of coal on the
environment and human health. It provides a comprehensive set of requirements for the safe
disposal of CCRs from coal-fired power plants. Governed by RCRA, the rule lays out technical
criteria for CCR landfills and surface impoundments, defines recordkeeping and reporting
requirements, and supports the responsible recycling of CCRs by distinguishing between safe
beneficial utilization and disposal. Beneficial utilization of CCRs includes both encapsulated and
unencapsulated uses in various applications such as in construction or agriculture. The federal
CCR Rule sets a minimum standard, but individual state regulations may impose stricter
regulations.

Encapsulated use of CCRs
Encapsulated use refers to applications where the coal ash is bound into a solid matrix
minimizing the risk of contaminants leaching into the surrounding environment. For example, fly
ash may be used as a direct substitute for Portland cement during the production of concrete or
bricks. The EPA has developed a methodology for evaluating encapsulated beneficial uses that
aims to keep environmental releases from encapsulated products containing CCRs comparable to
or lower than those from analogous non-CCR products. In addition, such products should be at
or below relevant regulatory and health-based benchmarks for human and ecological receptors,
during use by the consumer. Encapsulated CCR products that meet these criteria are considered
to be appropriate beneficial uses. (EPA, 2013) The rationale for encapsulation is that hazardous
constituents (e.g. heavy metals) are trapped within a stable matrix, reducing the likelihood of
migration into the environment compared to unencapsulated use scenarios. Encapsulated
applications to not require specific environmental demonstrations under the CCR Rule (e.g.
proving that leaching levels are below certain thresholds).

Unencapsulated use of CCRs
Unencapsulated use refers to applications where the coal ash is loose or mixed with other
materials but not bound into a solid matrix. It poses greater risks than encapsulated forms due to
its exposure to weathering, erosion, and potential for runoff. Examples include using coal ash for
structural fill, in road bases, or as a soil amendment. There is typically a higher risk of leaching
and human exposure with unencapsulated use because the coal ash is not sealed off from water

 195

Final Report DE-FE0032045

and air. The CCR Rule provides specific criteria and requirements for unencapsulated beneficial
use of CCR to ensure it does not pose significant risks to human health or the environment, while
still distinguishing it from disposal.

The CCR Rule defines “beneficial use of CCR” with four criteria, all of which must be met for
unencapsulated use to be exempt from disposal regulations:

 1. Functional benefit: The CCR must provide a practical purpose (e.g., improving soil stability or
 raising elevation in construction).
 2. Substitution for virgin material: It must replace a raw material (e.g., gravel or sand), conserving
 natural resources.
 3. Product specifications: It must meet relevant engineering or regulatory standards for the intended
 use.
 4. Environmental safety: For unencapsulated uses, it must not result in environmental releases (e.g.,
 leaching of contaminants) greater than those from the virgin material it replaces or exceed
 relevant regulatory thresholds.

Unlike encapsulated use, unencapsulated applications require an environmental demonstration to
qualify as beneficial use and avoid being classified as disposal. The CCR Rule outlines
conditions and limits for beneficial use of CCR. For unencapsulated use involving placement on
land in quantities exceeding 12,400 tons per project (e.g., structural fills or large embankments),
the user must demonstrate that constituent releases to groundwater do not exceed specified
levels. This threshold was set based on the volume typically associated with large-scale fills,
reflecting a point where environmental risks increase without oversight. For unencapsulated uses
below 12,400 tons (e.g., soil amendments or smaller fills), the rule does not mandate a formal
demonstration, but users must still ensure compliance with the four beneficial use criteria. In
practice, this relies on engineering judgment or state oversight.
The levels for non-carcinogenic constituents must not exceed the EPA’s Maximum Contaminant
Levels (MCLs) under the Safe Drinking Water Act (e.g., lead at 0.015 mg/L, cadmium at 0.005
mg/L). The levels for carcinogenic constituents must not exceed a cancer risk level of 1x10⁻⁵ (1
in 100,000) or the MCL, whichever is more protective (e.g., arsenic at 0.01 mg/L). In addition,
unencapsulated CCR must be managed to minimize airborne emissions during handling,
transport, and placement (40 CFR 257.80). This includes wetting the material, using wind
barriers, or covering it to prevent inhalation risks from silica or heavy metals. Furthermore, the
material must be evaluated (formally or informally, depending on scale) to ensure that leachate
does not contaminate groundwater beyond acceptable limits. This often involves comparing CCR
leachate data to the properties of the replaced virgin material.

ES&H risk evaluation guidelines
The extraction of REE from coal ash can present environmental safety and health risks that need
to be considered during handling and processing of coal ash. The EPA rule on the disposal of
CCRs from electric utilities does not regulate the beneficial use of coal ash but instead provides
guidelines for beneficial use evaluations. The EPA has set a threshold for environmental releases
from beneficial use of coal ash and recommends that uses which exceed this threshold should be

 196

evaluated with a site-specific environmental review. This approach aims to ensure that such uses
of coal ash do not pose significant risks to human health or the environment. As such, the use of
CCR for REE extraction should follow these guidelines (EPA, 2016a; b).
EPA’s Office of Solid Waste and Emergency Response (OSWER) published documents
describing a methodology for evaluating the beneficial use of industrial non-hazardous
secondary materials (EPA, 2016a; b). These secondary materials include the beneficial use of
CCR, both in encapsulated and unencapsulated form. It should be noted that the use of this
methodology is voluntary, and it does not change or substitute for any federal or state statutory
or regulatory provisions or requirements.

EPA Beneficial Use Compendium
The EPA Beneficial Use Compendium (EPA, 2016a) emphasizes a materials management
approach that aims to reduce impacts to human health and the environment associated with
materials over their entire life cycle. The compendium also provides a framework for evaluating
risks for environmental health and safety and offers guidelines to evaluate and manage risk. This
methodology can be used to determine whether the potential for adverse impacts to human health
and the environment from a proposed beneficial use is comparable to or lower than that from an
analogous product, or at or below relevant health-based and regulatory benchmarks. The
framework outlines three main phases: 1. planning and scoping, 2. impact analysis, and 3. final
characterization.
Following this methodology, the extraction process itself needs to be evaluated for its potential
environmental impacts. This may include impacts related to the use of chemicals, energy and
wastewater generation during the extraction process. Furthermore, it should be noted that after
REEs or other products are extracted, the remaining coal ash would still need to be managed in
accordance with the EPA's regulations for coal ash disposal. Depending on the extraction process
and the method of disposal of the CCRs after REE extraction, this may include measures to
prevent the leaching of contaminants into the environment. If the extraction process results in an
encapsulated product, guidelines for encapsulated use may apply. However, if the process leaves
the remaining ash material unbound, then the guidelines for unencapsulated use may apply.

Occupational Safety and Health
In addition to the U.S. EPA’s CCR Rule, which primarily governs the environmental
management and beneficial use of CCR, several other federal regulations administered by the
Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) and related agencies protect workers
handling unencapsulated CCR in coal-fired power plants, transport, or beneficial use settings.
Unencapsulated CCR poses specific hazards—such as respirable dust containing crystalline
silica and heavy metals that require workplace safety measures that protect workers against
inhalation risks like silicosis or heavy metal toxicity.

OSHA regulations
OSHA’s regulations under the General Industry Standards (29 CFR Part 1910) apply to workers
handling unencapsulated CCR in power plants, storage facilities, or processing sites. Workers
exposed to airborne CCR dust, which may contain respirable crystalline silica or heavy metal
particulates, must use respirators when exposures exceed permissible limits (29 CFR 1910.134).
Employers must provide respirators (e.g., N95 masks, half-face respirators with P100 filters, or

 197

powered air-purifying respirators) based on exposure levels. In addition, a respiratory protection
program is required, including fit testing, training, and medical evaluations to ensure workers can
safely use respirators.
The Hazard Communication standard (29 CFR 1910.1200), also known as the “HazCom”
standard, applies to CCR due to its chemical hazards (e.g., silica, arsenic, cadmium). Employers
must provide labels, safety data sheets (SDS), and worker education about CCR hazards,
including acute risks (e.g., irritation) and chronic risks (e.g., cancer, neurological damage).
Workers handling loose CCR need to understand its risks, especially since unencapsulated forms
are more likely to become airborne or contact skin. Employers must assess hazards and provide
appropriate Personal Protective Equipment (PPE), such as safety goggles, gloves, dust-resistant
coveralls, and steel-toed boots to protects workers against dust, chemical, and physical hazards
from unencapsulated CCR.

Exposure limits
Permissible Exposure Limits (PELs) are in effect for specific substances in CCR dust. Key limits
include respirable crystalline silica with a PEL of 50 μg/m³ (8-hour time-weighted average,
TWA), a particulate matter limit 5 mg/m³ (respirable fraction) or 15 mg/m³ (total dust). The
PELs for common heavy metals include lead (50 μg/m³), cadmium (5 μg/m³), and arsenic (10
μg/m³). Unencapsulated CCR often exceeds these limits without controls, triggering engineering
controls (e.g., ventilation) or PPE. Silica in unencapsulated CCR dust poses a silicosis and lung
cancer risk, especially during dry handling or transport. The silica standard (29 CFR 1910.1053)
specifically targets respirable crystalline silica, which is a major component of fly ash. Exposure
must be assessed and kept below the PEL of 50 μg/m³ (TWA) with an action level of 25 μg/m³.
Engineering controls to mitigate exposure include wet suppression, local exhaust ventilation and
specific work practices (e.g., avoiding dry sweeping) before relying on respirators. Medical
surveillance is required for workers exposed above the action level for 30+ days/year, including
chest X-rays and lung function tests.

Personnel protective measures to prevent exposure of workers
Potential risks of working with coal ash are largely preventable through proper management,
handling, and storage of the material. When working with coal ash, it is important to take
appropriate personal protective measures to minimize the risk of exposure to harmful pollutants
and dust. Recommended personal protective measures include:

 • Respiratory protection: Workers should wear appropriate respiratory protection, such as a
 NIOSH-approved dust mask or respirator, to protect against inhalation of coal ash dust.
 • Eye protection: Workers should wear safety glasses or goggles to protect against dust and
 debris.
 • Skin protection: Workers should wear protective clothing, such as coveralls, to protect
 against skin contact with coal ash. Gloves should also be worn to protect against skin
 contact with the ash.
 • Hearing protection: If working in an area with high noise levels, workers should wear
 earplugs or earmuffs to protect their hearing.

 198

 • Safety training: Workers should receive proper training on the hazards associated with
 coal ash, as well as the proper use and care of personal protective equipment.
 • Hygiene practices: Workers should be trained on proper hygiene practices, such as not
 eating, drinking, or smoking in areas where coal ash is present and washing their hands
 and face before eating, drinking, or smoking.
 • Environmental monitoring: Regular air and water monitoring should be carried out to
 ensure that the levels of pollutants remain within safe limits.

Personal protective measures may vary depending on the specific task and the level of exposure
to coal ash (see 4.1.1).

Transport of CCR
If unencapsulated CCR is transported (e.g., to a beneficial use site), it may be classified as a
hazardous material due to its chemical constituents, though it’s typically not a “hazardous waste”
under RCRA. The Department of Transportation regulates transport of hazardous materials (49
CFR Parts 100-185) and requires proper packaging (e.g., covered trucks) to prevent dust release,
placarding and shipping papers if thresholds for hazardous substances (e.g., arsenic) are met.

Conclusions
The extraction of REE from coal ash involves the unencapsulated use of CCR, such as fly ash,
which is governed by EPA and OSHA managing risks from hazardous substances present in
CCR. The EPA’s CCR Rule provides a framwork for the beneficial use of coal ash if the process
meets criteria ensuring functionality, resource conservation, and environmental safety. In
addition, the EPA mandates protective measures include dust suppression and, for larger
projects, proof that leaching of hazardous components stays within safe limits. OSHA enforces
worker safety through measures like respirable dust limits, mandatory protective gear, and
controls to prevent respiratory issues or skin irritation. In the context of unencapsulated CCR use
for rare earth element extraction, additional federal requirements may invoke stricter
requirements related to waste handling and disposal under RCRA (e.g. for chemical byproducts),
and air quality rules to manage emissions from processing. The specific ES&H requirements for
a specific implementation of a process for the extraction of REE from CCR need to be selected
through a systematic, risk-based approach that integrates scientific data, regulatory frameworks,
and operational context to protect workers, the public, and the environment. This approach
balances safety, environmental stewardship, and operational feasibility.
